Picard rank jumps for families of K3 surfaces in positive characteristic
Ruofan Jiang, Ananth N. Shankar, Ziquan Yang

Abstract
. Let 𝒳 / C \mathscr{X}/C be a non iso-trivial family of K3 surfaces over a curve C C defined over characteristic p > 2 p>2 field. We show that if 𝒳 \mathscr{X} avoids a necessary and structural obstruction coming from Frobenius, and satisfies a big monodromy condition, then there are infinitely may geometric fibers that have larger Picard rank than the geometric generic fiber.



1.
Introduction

In this paper, we address the question of the variation of Picard ranks in families of K3 surfaces over a base in positive characteristic
p>2
. This question is very well understood in characteristic zero. Indeed, let
\mathscr{X}\rightarrow\Delta
be a holomorphic family of K3 surfaces. Define the
Noether–Lefschetz locus
(succinctly, the NL locus) to be the set of points
s\in\Delta
such that the Picard rank of
\mathscr{X}_{s}
is greater than the generic Picard rank of
\mathscr{X}
. Work of Oguiso (
[
Ogu03
]
) and Green (
[
Voi13
, Thm. 3.5]
) shows that if the family is not isotrivial, the NL locus is dense in
\Delta
, and in particular is infinite.

The NL locus is can be defined for families of K3 surfaces over more general bases. Specifically, let
\mathscr{X}/C
be an algebraic family of K3 surfaces over some irreducible scheme
C
with generic point
\eta
. Define the NL locus to be the set of points
s\in C
such that the Picard rank of
\mathscr{X}_{\bar{s}}
is greater than that of
\mathscr{X}_{\bar{\eta}}
. The works of Green and Oguiso shows that if
C
is a positive dimensional scheme over
\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathscr{X}
is isotrivial, then the exceptional locus is Zariski-dense in
C
. Studying the NL locus becomes significantly more challenging when the
C
is a scheme over an arithmetic base. For instance, let
\mathscr{X}/\mathcal{O}_{K}[1/N]
be a K3 surface, where
\mathcal{O}_{K}
is the ring of integers of a number field
K
. In this setting, the NL locus is a union of points of the form
\Spec\mathbb{F}_{q}
. The series of works
[
Cha18
]
,
[
ST20
]
,
[
SSTT22
]
and
[
Tay22
]
shows that the NL locus is infinite, and therefore Zariski dense in
C
.

1.1.
Exceptional families: counterexamples in positive characteristic

The setting where
C
is defined over a field of positive characteristic is more subtle, and even the answer depends on characteristic
p
invariants that do not exist in characteristic zero. Note that the question reduces to the case where
C
is a curve. So in what follows, we will always assume that
C
is a curve. Moreover, up to replacing
C
by a finite cover, we assume that line bundles on the geometric generic fiber are already defined on the generic fiber.

The work
[
MST22a
]
(along with
[
Tay22
]
to treat the case of non-projective
C
) shows that the NL locus is Zariski dense if the generic K3 surface parameterized by
C
is ordinary. But as noted in
[
MST22a
]
, the ordinariness condition is very important. For example, the generic Picard rank and the Picard rank of every specialization is 22 for a family of supersingular K3 surfaces, and so the NL locus is empty. This is not the only obstruction to the exceptional locus not being dense. Consider any non-isotrivial family of elliptic curves
\mathscr{E}/C
, and let
E
be a supersingular elliptic curve. Then, consider the Kummer family
\mathcal{K}/C
associated to the abelian surface
\mathscr{E}\times E/C
. It is easy to check that the Picard rank of
\mathcal{K}_{\bar{s}}
is greater than the generic Picard rank if and only if
s\in C
is a point of supersingular specialization of
\mathscr{E}
. As there are only finitely many such points in
C
, it follows that the NL locus is finite. The above example is not an isolated one, and perists even for families of K3 surfaces with smaller Picard rank. We note that being non-ordinary alone is not sufficient for the NL locus to be finite. For instance,
[
Jia25
]
shows that families of non-ordinary abelian surfaces with trivial endomorphism ring are split at infinitely many fibers, a question that is intimately related to the distribution of the NL locus.

To explain the phenomenon underlying these examples, we will need to work with moduli spaces of lattice-polarized K3 surfaces. These spaces are (open parts of) GSpin Shimura varieties
S_{L}
associated to a quadratic lattice
L
having signature
(b,2)
with (
b\leq 19
). These Shimura varieties admit families of
special divisors
which are themselves GSpin Shimura varieties associated to sublattices of
L
having signature
(b-1,2)
. We make the standing assumption that the discriminant of
L
(or equivalently the discriminant of
\mathrm{Pic}(\mathscr{X}_{\eta})
) is prime to
p
. By
[
Kis10
]
, this implies the existence of smooth integral models
\mathscr{S}_{L}
of
S_{L}
at
p
. Under this assumption, there is a minimum-rank lattice
L
and a map
C\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{L}
that induces
\mathscr{X}
, and the assumption on the Picard lattice of the generic fiber of
\mathscr{X}
implies that
L
has prime-to-
p
discriminant. The mod
p
special fiber of
\mathscr{S}_{L}
admits a Newton stratification, with the open stratum being the ordinary locus and the smallest stratum being the supersingular locus (
\mathscr{S}_{\textrm{ss}}
). We consider the case when
b
is even – indeed, the NL locus in the odd case necessarily consists of every
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-point as the the Picard rank of a K3 surface over a finite field is necessarily even. This is also true in the more general setting of
GSpin
Shimura varieties and is a consequence of the group-theoretic fact that the rank of
\textrm{SO}_{2n-1}
equals the rank of
\textrm{SO}_{2n}
. The geometry and combinatorics of the Newton strata only depend on the isomorphism class of
L\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. There are two cases – when
L\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is a direct sum of hyperbolic planes, and when it isn’t. In the first case, the dimension of
\mathscr{S}_{\textrm{ss}}
is smaller than in the second case, and has dimension
b/2-1
(whereas, in the second case,
\mathscr{S}_{\textrm{ss}}
has dimension
b/2
). In the first case, the fact that
\mathscr{S}_{\textrm{ss}}
is smaller than expected has the crucial consequence that
every special divisor
contains the
\mathscr{S}_{\textrm{ss}}
. Further, the unique “almost supersingular” Newton stratum, which we call
\mathscr{S}_{\textrm{as}}
(whose closure
\overline{\mathscr{S}_{\textrm{as}}}
equals
\mathscr{S}_{\textrm{as}}\cup\mathscr{S}_{\textrm{ss}}
), does not intersect any special divisor! In other words, the intersection of
\overline{\mathscr{S}_{\textrm{as}}}
with every special divisor is contained in
\mathscr{S}_{\textrm{ss}}
. In particular, if
\mathscr{X}/C
is induced by a map
C\rightarrow\overline{\mathscr{S}_{\textrm{as}}}
, then the NL locus of
C
is necessarily contained in the supersingular locus of
C
, and thus must be finite. It is exactly this phenomenon that shows up in the Kummer example.

Definition 1.1.1
.

We say that the family of K3 surfaces
\mathscr{X}/C
is
exceptional
either if it is supersingular, or if
L\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is a direct sum of hyperbolic planes and the classifying map
C\to\mathscr{S}_{L}
factors through
\overline{\mathscr{S}}_{\mathrm{as}}
.

Remark 1.1.2
.

(1)

Consider the relative second crystalline cohomology of
\mathscr{X}/C
. This
F
-crystal has a quadratic pairing that makes it a
K3 crystal
; cf.
[
NO85a
, Def. 5.1]
. Its transcendental part, i.e., the orthogonal complement of the image of
\mathrm{Pic}(\mathscr{X}_{\eta})
, is also a K3 crystal. Let us call it
\mathbb{L}(\mathscr{X})
. It has at most three slopes at the generic point, and we have that the family
\mathscr{X}/C
is exceptional if and only if
\mathbb{L}(\mathscr{X})
generically has one or two slopes.

(2)

A generically ordinary non-isotrivial family of K3 surfaces can never be exceptional. Indeed, a generically ordinary family
\mathscr{X}\rightarrow C
would be exceptional if and only if the associated Shimura variety
S_{L}
satisfies
b=0
, and is therefore zero-dimensional. This implies that
\mathscr{X}\rightarrow C
is isotrivial.

1.2.
Statement of results in the setting of K3 surfaces

Our first result is in the setting of non-isotrivial families
\mathscr{X}/C
which are not exceptional, and which satisfy a monodromy result.

Theorem 1.2.1
.

Let
\mathscr{X}\rightarrow C
be a non iso-trivial non-exceptional family of K3 surfaces in characteristic
p>2
such that the discriminant of the generic Picard lattice is prime-to-
p
. Let
L
be the minimal rank lattice with
\mathscr{X}\rightarrow C
induced by a map
C\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{L}
. Suppose that the
\ell
-adic monodromy equals that of
\mathscr{S}_{L}
. Then the NL locus is dense.

We will elaborate on the big monodromy assumption – indeed, there are non-exceptional examples where the NL locus is provably finite when the family has smaller monodromy, which we describe in Section
1.3
. But first, we state a stronger theorem that implies the above result.

Theorem 1.2.2
.

Let
\mathscr{X}\rightarrow C
be a non iso-trivial non-exceptional family of K3 surfaces in characteristic
p>2
such that the discriminant of the generic Picard lattice is prime-to-
p
. Let
\eta
be the generic point of
C
. Suppose that the connected-étale exact sequence of the enlarged formal Brauer group of
\mathscr{X}_{\eta}
does not split. Then the NL locus is dense.

The notion of enlarged formal Brauer group traces back several years; cf.
[
AM77
, §IV]
and
[
NO85b
, §3]
, where the group scheme is defined at least for non-supersingular K3 surfaces over finite fields. We will define the enlarged formal Brauer group for
\mathscr{X}_{\eta}
in Section
8
. Roughly speaking, it is a
p
-divisible group characterized by
the property that (up to a Tate twist) its Dieudonné crystal equals the quotient of the K3 crystal of
\mathscr{X}_{\eta}
by the subcrystal of the smallest slope. Note that when
\mathscr{X}
is generically ordinary, the connected-étale exact sequence of the enlarged formal Brauer group admits a global splitting if and only if the family
\mathscr{X}\rightarrow C
is isotrivial.

A consequence of the enlarged formal Brauer group admitting such a splitting is that the generic Kuga–Satake abelian scheme admits extra endomorphisms. The above result therefore has the following corollary.

Corollary 1.2.3
.

Let
\mathscr{X}\rightarrow C
be a non iso-trivial non-exceptional family of K3 surfaces in characteristic
p>2
such that the discriminant of the generic Picard lattice is prime-to-
p
. Let
L
be the minimal rank lattice with
\mathscr{X}\rightarrow C
induced by a map
C\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{L}
, and let
\mathcal{A}/\mathscr{S}
be the universal Kuga–Satake abelian scheme over
\mathscr{S}
. Then the NL locus is dense if
\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{A}|_{C})\otimes\mathbb{Q}=\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{A})\otimes\mathbb{Q}
.

Remark 1.2.4
.

These theorems are stated for K3 surfaces only for concreteness. By combining a general formulation
Theorem

1.4.1
below with results from
[
HYZ25
,
GY25
]
, they extend verbatim to other varieties with
h^{2,0}=1
whose moduli satisfy mild assumptions—for example, elliptic surfaces of height
1
over genus
1
curves. We leave the precise formulations to the interested reader.

1.3.
Counterexamples with small monodromy

We note that the case of exceptional K3 surfaces are not the only counterexamples to the NL locus being dense. For example, let
F/\mathbb{Q}
be a real quadratic field, and let
V^{\prime}/F
be a 4-dimensional vector space along with a bilinear form that is positive definite at one real place and has signature
(2,2)
at the other. The Shimura variety
S_{V^{\prime}}
associated to quadratic space is defined over
F
, and embeds in
S_{L}
where
L
is a rank 8 quadratic
\mathbb{Z}
-lattice with signature
(6,2)
. By arguments of Geeman and Nikulin (see
[
vG08
, Prop. 3.3]
), one can arrange that
L
embeds into the K3 lattice so that
S_{L}
parametrizes K3 surfaces with real multiplication by
F
.

It is easy to see that there are infinitely many primes
p
such that
p
splits in
F
with
v_{1},v_{2}\mid p
the places above
p
, and such that
V^{\prime}_{v_{i}}
(for
i=1,2
) is a direct sum of two hyperbolic planes over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Fix such a
p
and
v=v_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,2\}
. The mod
v
Shimura variety
\mathscr{S}_{V^{\prime},v}
admits three Newton strata: the ordinary stratum, the supersingular stratum, and one intermediate stratum. This intermediate stratum does
not
intersect
\mathscr{S}_{\textrm{as}}\subseteq\mathscr{S}_{L}
. However, it behaves like the 1-dimensional Newton stratum in the moduli space of a pair of elliptic curves in that the NL locus intersects the closure only along the supersingular locus. Therefore, the NL locus is always empty for a family of K3 surfaces pulled back from this intermediate Newton stratum. It is easy to construct similar examples where the K3 surface in question has smaller Picard ranks.

1.4.
General GSpin Shimura varieties

In this section, we state the results in the general setting of GSpin Shimura varieties that go beyond the K3 case. We will always fix
p
to be an odd prime.

Let
L
be a lattice of signature
(b,2)
which is self-dual at
p
, and let
\mathscr{S}_{L}
be the canonical integral model of the associated GSpin Shimura variety at
p
. Though
\mathscr{S}_{L}
does not parametrize K3 surfaces in general, one can still equip
\mathscr{S}_{L}
with a universal Kuga–Satake abelian scheme
\mathcal{A}/\mathscr{S}_{L}
via a suitable Hodge embedding. Similar to the K3 case,
\mathscr{S}_{L}
is equipped with a family of special divisors
\{\mathcal{Z}(m)\}_{m\geq 1}
which are Zariski closure in
\mathscr{S}
of GSpin Shimura subvarieties associated to sublattices of
L
having signature
(b-1,2)
. The special divisors parameterize points with so-called special endomorphisms (see Section
2.1
for definitions). The notion of formal Brauer groups also generalizes; cf, §
8
.

Similar to the case of K3 surfaces, when
b
is even, there is a dichotomy between
L\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
being a direct sum of hyperbolic planes, and otherwise. In the former case, the supersingular locus has dimension
b/2-1
and
\overline{\mathscr{S}_{\textrm{as}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}(m)
is contained in the supersingular locus for every
m
, and thus the NL locus cannot be dense for curves generically mapping to this locus. Now we let
\iota:C\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{L}
be a nontrivial morphism, where the discriminant of
L
is prime to
p
. We further assume that
\mathscr{S}_{L}
is the smallest
GSpin
Shimura varieties containing the image of
\iota
, i.e.
\iota
does not factor through any
\mathcal{Z}(m)
.
Similar to the K3 case, we say that
\iota
is
exceptional
, if its image is supersingular, or
L\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is a direct sum of hyperbolic planes and
\iota
factors through
\overline{\mathscr{S}_{\textrm{as}}}\subseteq\mathscr{S}_{L}
.

Theorem 1.4.1
.

Let
C\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{L}
be as above, and suppose that it is non-exceptional. Then the NL locus is dense if one of the following is true:

(a)

the connected-étale exact sequence of the enlarged formal Brauer group over the generic point of
C
does not split.

(b)

\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{A}|_{C})\otimes\mathbb{Q}=\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{A})\otimes\mathbb{Q}
.

(c)

The
l
-adic monodromy of
\mathcal{A}|_{C}
equals that of
\mathcal{A}|_{\mathscr{S}_{L}}
.

1.5.
Outline of proof

The overarching outline follows
[
CO06
]
,
[
MST22b
]
and
[
MST22a
]
, and is a local-global argument. The idea (first used in
[
CO06
]
) is to use global inputs to estimate the global intersection
C.\mathcal{Z}(m)
as
m
varies, and to show that the local contribution
i_{P}(C.\mathcal{Z}(m))
(i.e., the intersection multiplicity of
C
with
\mathcal{Z}(m)
at
P
) from any one point
P\in C
is small relative to
C.\mathcal{Z}(m)
. The difficulty, of course, is in proving good enough bounds for
i_{P}(C.\mathcal{Z}(m))
. Our proof of this consists of three parts having distinct flavors. The first part is mostly global, and in particular shows that
i_{P}(C.\mathcal{Z}(m))
is small enough as long as
P
is not a supersingular point. For supersingular points, we reduce the main theorem to a purely local statement in the context of a formal curve
\Spec\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
. This technique first appeared in
[
SSTT22
]
and
[
MST22a
]
, and the lack of ordinarity introduces no additional difficulty. Part 2 is purely local, and we establish a combinatorial characterization of “decaying” lattices of special endomorphisms, which is needed to control
i_{P}(C.\mathcal{Z}(m))
when
P
is a supersingular point. The fact that the curve avoids the ordinary locus introduces significant difficulties, which take new ideas to overcome. Part 3 is an algebraization result used to handle an extreme situation occurring in Part 2 where “maximal amount” of special endomorphisms resist decay (hence the local intersections can not be effectively controlled). When this happens, we show that the connected-étale exact sequence of the enlarged formal Brauer group over the generic point of
C
must split. This implies that the generic Kuga–Satake abelian scheme admits extra algebraic endomorphisms.

We now describe these three parts in more detail.

1.5.1.
Local-global comparison

Borcherd’s theory tells us the asymptotic growth rate of the global intersection number
C.Z(m)
as
m\to\infty
. More precisely,
C.Z(m)
is of the form
|q_{L}(m)|(C\cdot\omega)+o(m^{b/2})
and
q_{L}(m)\asymp m^{(b+2)/2}
. Here,
\omega
is the Hodge bundle of
\mathscr{S}
.

Let
P
be a
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-point on
C
and let
t
be a uniformizer of
C
at
P
, so that the formal completion of
C
at
P
is
\Spf(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}[\![t]\!])
. Let
\mathcal{A}_{r}
be the restriction of
\mathcal{A}
to
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}[t]/(t^{r})
, and let
L_{r}:=L(\mathcal{A}_{r})
. Note that we have a nested sequence of lattices
L(\mathcal{A}_{P})=L_{1}\supseteq L_{2}\supseteq L_{3}\supseteq\cdots
, such that a special endomorphism
v\in L(\mathcal{A}_{P})
deforms to
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}[t]/(t^{r})
if and only if
v\in L_{r}
. By the moduli interpretation of
\mathcal{Z}(m)
, the local intersection number
i_{P}(C.\mathcal{Z}(m))
is computed by
\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\#\{v\in L_{r}:v^{2}=m\}
. It is proved in
[
MST22a
]
(crucially using inputs from
[
SSTT22
]
) that if
P
is non-supersingular, then for large
X
,
\sum_{m\leq 2X}i_{P}(C\cdot Z(m))=O(X^{b/2}\log X)
, so the growth rate is not on the same order as the global intersection number. By the work of Tayou
[
Tay22
]
, a similar statement holds when
P
is a boundary point. When
P
is supersingular, we have
i_{P}(C.\mathcal{Z}(m))\asymp m^{(b+2)/2}
as well, so we need to compare it with
|q_{L}(m)|(C\cdot\omega)
more carefully, and this is done in Part 2.

1.5.2.
Supersingular points and decaying lattices of special endomorphisms

Now let
P
be supersingular. Roughly speaking, to bound
i_{P}(C.\mathcal{Z}(m))
we need
L_{r}
to become small quickly as
r
grows. This is done by examining for each
n
, the “
n
-th order decay rate”
d_{n}(v)=\max\{r:p^{n-1}v\in L_{r}\}
for
v\in L(\mathcal{A}_{P})
. There are two kinds of results that we need:

(1)

There is a saturated sublattice (having large enough rank) of
L(\mathcal{A}_{P})
consisting of vectors
v
that decays rapidly. See sections
5
and
7
for the definition of rapid decay, which roughly means that
d_{n}(v)
’s are subject to certain bounds.

(2)

The first time a primitive endomorphism
v
in this sublattice decays is sufficiently early (i.e.,
d_{1}(v)
is sufficiently small). See section
6
.

We need a lot more control for both steps as compared to the ordinary case. In particular, “large enough rank” just means rank 2 in the ordinary case, and we are able to establish this in a comparatively ad-hoc way in
[
MST22a
]
and
[
MST22b
]
, whereas our treatment in the case of general Newton strata is (and needs to be) more systematic. Indeed, we are able to precisely relate the rates of higher-order decay to the degree of tangency of the curve to deeper
Newton
strata. This takes up all of Section
5
and part of
7
.

When
C
generically maps into the ordinary stratum, one only requires
one
special endomorphism to decay “early” to first order, which can be deduced just from the fact that curve mapping non-constantly to
\mathscr{S}
. However, the deeper the Newton stratum into which
C
generically maps, the more special endomorphisms are required to decay “sufficiently early” to first order. Even quantifying what sufficiently “early” (i.e., what bounds to put on
d_{n}(v)
’s) requires a significantly more involved setup. It turns out that the degree of tangency of the curve with the
Artin
strata is what governs the first order decay, and we end up proving that a large-rank isotropic space decays early to first order. Proving this takes up all of Section
6
and part of
7
.

Taking both these steps together, it is possible to obtain a comprehensive description of the lattices
\{L_{r}\}_{r\geq 1}
. We find that the decay of special endomorphisms must follow some fascinating stringent patterns, which we were not able to find a satisfactory conceptual explanation. For curious reader, we provide a typical pattern of decay:

Example 1.5.3
.

When the point
P
is superspecial,
L_{1}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
admits a (carefully chosen) basis

\{e^{\prime},f^{\prime},e_{1},...,e_{m},f_{1},...,f_{m}\},

such that
\langle e_{i},f_{j}\rangle=1
,
\langle e_{i},e_{j}\rangle=\langle f_{i},f_{j}\rangle=0
and
\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\{e^{\prime},f^{\prime}\}
is orthogonal to
\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\{e_{i},f_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
. Consider the decaying lattices
L_{r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
,
r\geq 1
. The decay obeys the following beautiful pattern (when
r
increases, the vectors decay in an order following the arrows:
e_{1}
first decays, then
e_{2}
, …, then
e_{m}
, then
f_{m}
, then
f_{m-1}
,
etc
).
e_{1}\
e_{2}\
...\
e_{m}\
f_{m}\
f_{1}\
f_{2}\
...\
e^{\prime},f^{\prime}\
pe_{1}\
pe_{2}\
...\
pe_{m}\
pf_{m}\
pf_{1}\
pf_{2}\
...\
pe^{\prime},pf^{\prime}\
p^{2}e_{1}\
p^{2}e_{2}\
...\
p^{2}e_{m}\
...\

In this particular example, one can also explicitly compute the
n
-th decay rate
d_{n}(e)
of a basis vector
e
. These decay rates themselves satisfy intriguing recursive relations, and are closely related to the intersection of
C
with the Hodge bundle.

In general situations, the decay may not strictly follow the above pattern. There may exist vectors that never decay, which complicates matters. However, the above pattern always happen for a sufficiently large quotient lattice (say, modulo the sublattice that resist decay).

1.5.4.
Algebraization

The precise description of decay in Part 2 allows us to find very tight bounds on the local intersection
i_{P}(C.\mathcal{Z}(m))
. For most curves
C
, this already yields the desired local-global comparison, hence the denseness of NL locus. The bound only fails when the curve is in an extreme position, where the
p
-divisible group
\mathcal{A}_{P}[p^{\infty}]
admits a “maximal amount” of formal special endomorphisms that do not decay (i.e., they deform all the way to the formal neighborhood
C^{/P}
), so
i_{P}(C.\mathcal{Z}(m))
is as large as possible. In fact, when this happens, the main term of the local intersections at supersingular points already equal the main term of the global intersection, hence the local–global comparison breaks down and one cannot conclude that NL locus is dense.

We note that this is not a byproduct of our decay results being insufficiently strong. Indeed, it is precisely this behaviour that gives rise to the counterexamples with small monodromy discussed in Section
1.3
.

We overcome this structural difficulty by proving that in this extreme case, the connected-étale sequence
\mathbf{\Psi}
of the enlarged formal Brauer group over the generic point of
C
must split, cf. section
8
. This is termed as algebraization in the following sense: let
K(C)
be the function field of
C
. The existence of this “maximal amount of” formal special endomorphisms of
\mathcal{A}_{C^{/P}}[p^{\infty}]
, which is a
p
-divisible group over a formal base, implies the splitting of
\mathbf{\Psi}
over
K(C)
, which is a global and algebraic statement. It then follows that

\mathcal{A}|_{C}
has strictly more endomorphisms than
\mathcal{A}
, i.e.,
\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{A}|_{C})\otimes\mathbb{Q}\supsetneq\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{A})\otimes\mathbb{Q}
.

Therefore, if
\mathbf{\Psi}
does not split over
K(C)
, or if
\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{A}|_{C})\otimes\mathbb{Q}=\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{A})\otimes\mathbb{Q}
, then we are not in the extreme situation, and the NL locus is dense as we have noted earlier.

1.6.
Prior and related work

Our paper fits into the broader context of studying the distribution of algebraic cycles in a family. Over
\mathbb{C}
, this reduces to studying the
Hodge locus
(at least when assuming the Hodge conjecture), and much recent progress has been made. In
[
KO21
]
,
[
BKU24
]
, and
[
KU25
]
, the authors thoroughly studied when the Hodge locus, or a distinguished part of it, is dense. From
[
KU25
]
, we typically expect a theorem ensuring density to involve two parts in its assumptions.

(I)

The period dimension (i.e., the rank of the Kodaira-Spencer map at a generic point) is large enough for the Hodge locus to have non-negative expected dimension.

(II)

The monodromy group is not “too degenerate”.

Note that for a variation of Hodge structures, having infinite monodromy is equivalent to having positive period dimension — both are equivalent to non-isotriviality. In retrospect, it is somewhat a coincidence that when
h^{2,0}=1
, non-isotriviality already ensures a dense NL locus (cf.
[
Moo17
, Prop. 6.4]
). In general, one would expect (I) and (II) to be separate (though related) conditions. In fact, this already happens in positive characteristic even when
h^{2,0}=1
: the supersingular locus has positive period dimension and yet finite monodromy.

In analogy to the complex analytic situation, the hypotheses of our
Theorem

1.4.1
consist of three parts: Non-isotriviality corresponds to (I). The non-exceptional-ness assumption excludes the obvious obstruction to a dense NL locus coming from Frobenius, while conditions (a), (b), (c) are analogues of (II). In particular, (a) implies the following: if the crystalline monodromy group, (cf.
[
Cre92
]
) on the open Newton stratum of
C
1
1

1

i.e., the group
G(\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}[p^{-1}]|_{U},\bar{\eta})
if we use the notation of
[
D’A23
, §2.2]
. Here
U
is the open Newton stratum of
C
, and
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}
is the natural
F
-crystal on
\mathscr{S}
recalled in
Definition

2.1.2
below.
does not split as a product over
W(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})[1/p]
, then the NL locus is dense. So (a) is indeed a condition on monodromy.

Another general theme which our results fit into is the intersection theory on Shimura varieties. During an AIM workshop, several participants (including the second named author) conjectured (
[
ST25
, Conj. 5.2]
) that for an integral model
\mathscr{S}
of a Shimura variety of Hodge type and generically ordinary subvarieties
X,Y\subset\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
of complementary dimension, the intersection of
X
with the union of prime-to-
p
Hecke translates of
Y
is Zariski dense in
X
. The ordinary Hecke orbit (HO) conjecture (
[
vH24
]
) is the special case
X=\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
,
Y
a point;
[
MST22a
]
treats the case where
X
is a curve in an orthogonal Shimura variety and
Y
a special divisor; and
[
AHS24
]
treats the case when
X,Y
are ordinary curves inside a suitable Hilbert modular surface. These density results have some unexpected geometric applications, e.g., to a good reduction criterion for K3 surfaces (
[
BY23
]
). There is more known about this conjecture in characteristic zero - for example, the work
[
TT23
]
address this question in many cases, including when
X
is a curve and
Y
is a divisor, and ongoing work of Baldi-Urbanik addresses this question over the complex numbers whenever
X,Y
are Hodge-generic in the ambient Shimura variety.

Beyond the ordinary case, these questions become significantly harder, and even the correct formulation becomes mysterious. For
X=\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
and
Y
a point, the full Hecke orbit conjecture (recently proved by van Hoften and D’Addezio
[
DvH25
]
) roughly says that the obvious Frobenius constraint—that Hecke translates lie in the same central leaf—is the only one. However, as this paper illustrates, to extend
[
MST22a
]
beyond the obvious Frobenius constraint, there are additional conditions whose failure may lead to counterexamples (e.g., §
1.3
). On the other hand, these additional conditions have certain parallels in complex Hodge theory. In
[
Jia25
]
, the first named author studied the almost ordinary locus when
b=3
(i.e., for abelian surfaces). In that paper, the question considered isn’t a Picard-rank jumping question (which would be automatic as
b
is odd), but the more fine question of showing that the almost-ordinary curve
C
intersects
\mathcal{Z}(m)
where
m
varies over a fixed square-class. In this paper, we now provide a full generalization for all
b
. In doing so, we hope to further illustrate how the Newton and Artin stratification governs the distribution of algebraic cycles in positive characteristic, and the parallel between the positive characteristic and the complex analytic situations.

1.7.
Organization of the paper

In Section
2
, we define the various objects in play, including
GSpin
Shimura varieties, special divisors and endomorphisms, and the Newton stratification. In Section
3
, we setup the intersection-theoretic framework required to prove our main result, and using results from
[
SSTT22
]
,
[
MST22a
]
and
[
Tay22
]
, we reduce our main theorem to a local statement at supersingular points. In Section
4
, we recall work of Kisin and Ogus to describe the universal K3 crystal at a formal neighbourhood of a supersingular point and use this to derive explicit equations for the Newton strata. Sections
5
and
6
are the technical heart of the paper where we bound local-intersection numbers at superspecial points under a certain local assumption. We generalize this to all supersingular points at
7
. We finally prove the main theorem in Section
8
, where we show that the local assumption always holds under the global hypotheses of our main result. We also have included a glossary with the notation that we use in our paper (especially for Sections
5
,
6
, and
7
) for the reader’s convenience.

2.
Preliminaries on orthogonal Shimura varieties

2.1.
Basic setup

We will set up basic definitions and terminology for our Shimura varieties, special endomorphisms, and special divisors. At first, our exposition and setup will follow
[
MST22a
, Section 2]
and
[
SSTT22
, Section2]
, and we will refer to
[
AGHMP18
]
and
[
MP16
]
. We will then work modulo
p
and collect various results (mostly from
[
Ogu79
]
,
[
Ogu01
]
, and
[
MST22a
]
) that together describe Newton strata.

Let
L
be a quadratic lattice with signature
(b,2)
which is self-dual at
p
. Write
V
for
L_{\mathbb{Q}}
. We may also assume that
L\subset V
is maximal among those lattices contained in
V
on which which the bilinear form has integer values. Let
\mathrm{Cl}(L)
denote the Clifford algebra associated to
L
. Note that we have a natural embedding of free
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
-modules
L\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Cl}(L)\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
. Let
G:=\mathrm{GSpin}(L\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})
be the group of spinor similitudes. Note that
G
is a reductive group over
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
and is naturally a subgroup of
\mathrm{Cl}(L\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})^{\times}
. Let
\mathcal{D}
be the Hermitian symmetric domain
\{z\in V_{\mathbb{C}}|\langle z,\overline{z}\rangle<0\}
. Then
(G,\mathcal{D})
defines a spinor Shimura datum with reflex field
\mathbb{Q}
. For every neat compact open subgroup
\mathbb{K}\subseteq G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, we obtain a Shimura variety
\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathbb{K}}(G,\mathcal{D})
over
\mathbb{Q}
.

Set
H=\mathrm{Cl}(L)
, viewed as a
\mathrm{Cl}(L)
-bimodule. Note that
H
has a natural
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-grading. Left multiplication of
\mathrm{Cl}(L)
on
H
the induces spin representation
G\to\mathrm{GL}(H)
and an embedding
L\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Cl}(L)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{End}(H)
. Equip
\mathrm{End}(H)
with the pairing
(\alpha,\beta):=2^{-\mathrm{rank\,}L}\mathrm{tr}(\alpha\circ\beta)
. Then the composite embedding
L\hookrightarrow\mathrm{End}(H)
is isometric and we can form the orthogonal projection
\psi:\mathrm{End}(H)\to L
. The spinor group
G
can be conversely viewed as the stabilizer of the
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-grading, the right
\mathrm{Cl}(L\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})
-action and the idempotent projector
\psi
.

Suppose now that
\mathbb{K}
is of the form
\mathbb{K}_{p}\mathbb{K}^{p}
for
\mathbb{K}^{p}\subseteq G(\mathbb{A}^{p}_{f})
and
\mathbb{K}_{p}=G(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, i.e., is hyperspecial at
p
.
By
[
Kis10
]
(cf.
[
MP16
]
) there is an canonical integral model
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{K}}(G)
over
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
.
We may endow
H
with a suitable symplectic form such that the left multiplication by
G
on
H
respects the form up to scaling, and indeed induces an embedding of Shimura data
(G,\mathcal{D})\hookrightarrow(\mathrm{GSp},\mathcal{H}^{\pm})
, where
\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
is the associated Siegel half spaces. This Siegel embedding equips
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{K}}(G)
with a universal abelian scheme
\mathcal{A}
, called the
Kuga-Satake abelian scheme
.
2
2

2

Technically, in
[
Kis10
]
and
[
MP16
]
,
\mathcal{A}
is only defined as a sheaf of abelian schemes up to prime-to-
p
quasi-isogeny.
However, for
\mathbb{K}^{p}
sufficiently small, we can take
\mathcal{A}
to be an actual abelian scheme (cf.
[
Kis10
, (2.1.5)]
).
We use
\mathcal{A}[p^{\infty}]
to denote the
p
-divisible group associated to
\mathcal{A}
. Throughout, we fix a choice of such level structure, and for brevity let
\mathrm{Sh}
(resp.
\mathscr{S}
) denote
\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathbb{K}}(G,\mathcal{D})
(resp. the integral model
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{K}}(G,\mathcal{D})
).

Define the sheaves
\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{B}}/\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathbb{C}}
,
\mathbb{H}_{\ell}/\mathscr{S}
(
\ell\neq p
),
\mathbb{H}_{p}/\mathrm{Sh}
,
\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}/\mathscr{S}
and
\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{cris}}/\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
denote the (first relative) Betti cohomology,
\ell
-adic etale cohomology,
p
-adic etale cohomology, de Rham cohomology, and the crystalline cohomology of the universal abelian scheme.
The abelian scheme
\mathcal{A}
is equipped with a “CSpin-structure”: a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-grading,
\mathrm{Cl}(L)
-action and an idempotent projector
\psi_{?}:\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{H}_{?})\to\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{H}_{?})
for
?\in\{B,\ell,p,\mathrm{dR},\mathrm{cris}\}
on (various applicable fibers of)
\mathscr{S}
. We use
\mathbb{L}_{?}
to denote the images of
\psi_{?}
.

Remark 2.1.1
.

The embedding
L\subseteq\mathrm{End}_{\mathrm{Cl}(L)}(H)
can be generalized to some extent. For every
r\geq 0
, one can construct a natural map
\wedge^{r}V\to\mathrm{Cl}(V)
by sending
v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{r}
to
\sum_{\sigma}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)v_{\sigma(1)}v_{\sigma(2)}\cdots v_{\sigma(r)}
.
This induces an isomorphism between
\mathbb{Q}
-spaces
\wedge^{\bullet}V
and
\mathrm{Cl}(V)
. The natural action of
\wedge^{\bullet}V
on
H_{\mathbb{Q}}
results in a
G
-invariant embedding
\wedge^{\bullet}V\hookrightarrow\mathrm{End}_{\mathrm{Cl}(V)}(H_{\mathbb{Q}})
. As a consequence, there is a natural embedding of rational cohomology sheaves

\wedge^{\bullet}\mathbb{L}_{?}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{H}_{?}\otimes\mathbb{Q})
.

Definition 2.1.2
.

We now use the sheaves
\mathbb{L}_{?}
to define the notions of special endomorphisms of points of
\mathscr{S}
and special divisors in
\mathscr{S}
.

(1)

Given any
\mathscr{S}
-scheme
T
, an endomorphism
f\in\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{A}_{T})
is called a
special endomorphism
(
[
MP16
, Def. 5.2, see also Lem. 5.4, Cor. 5.22]
) if all cohomological realizations of
f
lie in the image
\mathbb{L}_{?}\rightarrow\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{H}_{?})
where
?=\mathrm{B},\mathrm{dR},\mathrm{cris},\ell\neq p,p
. For brevity, we simply drop those subscripts that do not make sense. For example, if
p
is invertible in
T
, then
?=\mathrm{cris}
doesn’t make sense and if
p
is not invertible then
?=p,\mathrm{B}
do not make sense.

We denote the submodule of
\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{A}_{T})
of special endomorphisms by
L(\mathcal{A}_{T})
. By
[
MP16
, Lem. 5.2]
, for
v\in L(\mathcal{A}_{T})
, we have
v\circ v=[Q(v)]
for
some
Q(v)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and
Q(v)
is a positive definite quadratic form on the
\mathbb{Z}
-lattice
L(\mathcal{A}_{T})
.

(2)

For
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, the
special divisor

\mathcal{Z}(m)
is the Deligne–Mumford stack over
\mathscr{S}
with
functor of points
\mathcal{Z}(m)(T)=\{v\in L(\mathcal{A}_{T})\,|\,Q(v)=m\}
for any
\mathscr{S}
-scheme
T
. We use the
same notation for the image of
\mathcal{Z}(m)
in
\mathscr{S}
. By
[
AGHMP18
, Prop. 4.5.8]
,
\mathcal{Z}(m)
is an
effective Cartier divisor flat over
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
and hence
\mathcal{Z}(m)_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}

is still an effective Cartier divisor of

\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
. We denote
\mathcal{Z}(m)_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
by
Z(m)
.

(3)

Suppose that
p
is not invertible in
T
. We say that
f\in\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{A}_{T}[p^{\infty}])
is a
formal special endomorphism
if its crystalline realization lies in
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}
. We denote the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-submodule of
\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{A}_{T}[p^{\infty}])
of formal special endomorphisms by
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{T})
.

Remark 2.1.3
.

Consider a connected scheme
T
and a
T
-valued point of
\mathscr{S}
. An endomorphism
f\in\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{A}_{T})
or
\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{A}_{T}[p^{\infty}])
being special can be checked at any geometric point of
T
(
[
AGHMP18
, Proposition 4.3.4]
). Further if
x\in T_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
is a geometric point, then
f
is special if and only if
f_{t,\mathrm{cris}}\in\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}
.

2.2.
Newton stratification

Let
k
be any perfect field of characteristic
p\geq 5
and let
W=W(k)
. We describe the Newton stratification on
\mathscr{S}_{k}
following the scheme-theoretic approach of
[
Ogu01
]
. In order to extend the results there without referring to K3 surfaces, we can use the language of orthogonal
F
-zips. This extends the data of Hodge and conjugation filtrations, as well as the Frobenius action on the de Rham cohomology of K3 surfaces.

For any closed subscheme
T
of
\mathscr{S}_{k}
, we write
\widetilde{T}
for the (
p
-complete) PD-envelope of
T
in
\mathscr{S}_{W}
. When
\widetilde{T}
is
p
-torsion-free, we say that
T
is
admissible
, and the restriction
(\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}|_{T},p\Frob)
is a K3 crystal in the terminology of Nygaard–Ogus (
[
NO85a
, Def. 5.1]
), ignoring the rank
22
condition. Recall that a local complete intersection is admissible (
[
BBM82
, §2.3.3]
). This condition allows us to view
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{T})
as a submodule of
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{T})[1/p]
, on which
\Frob
natually acts.

The de Rham cohomology
\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}
is equipped with a Hodge and conjugate filtration, these structures are inherited by the direct summand
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}
of
\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{dR}})
. Note that there is a canonical (reduction mod
p
) map
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\tilde{T})\rightarrow\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},T}
, which we will denote in this section by
\pi
. By Mazur–Ogus inequality
[
BO78
, Thm. 8.26]
, one can recover these structures using the Frobenius structure on
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}|_{T}
:

(1)

\displaystyle\Fil^{i}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},T}

\displaystyle=\pi\left(\Frob^{-1}(p^{i}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{T}))\right)

(2)

\displaystyle\Fil_{c}^{i}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},T}

\displaystyle=\pi\left(p^{-i}\left[\Frob(\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{T}))\cap p^{i}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{T})\right]\right)

The first equation breaks into two

(3)

\displaystyle\pi^{-1}(\Fil^{0}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},T})

\displaystyle=\{\widetilde{x}\in\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{T})\mid\Frob(\widetilde{x})\in\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{T})\}

(4)

\displaystyle\pi^{-1}(\Fil^{1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},T})

\displaystyle\supsetneq\{\widetilde{x}\in\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{T})\mid\Frob(\widetilde{x})\in p\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{T})\}.

Note that the first is an equality because
\Frob(\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}})\in p^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}
, but the second is not. Technically,
[
BO78
, Thm. 8.26]
applies to a smooth base. So in order to obtain (
1
) and (
2
) for arbitrary
T
(with
p
-torsion free
\widetilde{T}
), one can use that for any closed embedding
T\hookrightarrow T^{\prime}
, there are commutative diagrams

(5)

We will often implicitly make use of such diagrams. Over
T=\mathscr{S}_{k}
(and hence any
T
), the
F
-zip
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},T}
is equipped with two
\mathcal{O}_{T}
-linear isomorphisms

(6)

\displaystyle\gamma_{-1}:(F_{T})^{*}(\mathrm{gr}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},T})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Fil^{-1}_{c}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},T}\text{ and }\gamma_{0}:(F_{T})^{*}(\mathrm{gr}^{0}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},T})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{gr}^{0}_{c}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},T}.

Also, note that
\mathrm{gr}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},T}=\pi(p\Frob(\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{T})))
.

Before we proceed, we briefly recall some basic facts abour K3 crystals over a point (
[
Ogu01
, p.326-328]
).

Definition 2.2.1
.

(1)

A K3 crystal over
k
is a finite free
W
-module
H
equipped with a pairing
\langle-,-\rangle
and a
\sigma
-linear Frobenius automorphism
\Frob
on
H[1/p]
such that
p\Frob(H)\subseteq H
has rank
1
quotient and
\langle\Frob(x),\Frob(y)\rangle=\sigma(\langle x,y\rangle)
.

(2)

Define Hodge and conjugation filtrations on
H_{k}
using (
1
) and (
2
).

(3)

Define a sequence of subspaces
E_{1}\subseteq E_{2}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\Fil^{0}_{c}H_{k}
as follows. Define
E_{1}=\Fil^{-1}_{c}H_{k}
and for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, define
E_{i+1}
recursively as follows:

(a)

if
E_{i}\subseteq\Fil^{0}H_{k}
, define
E_{i+1}:=\varpi^{-1}(\gamma_{0}(E_{i}))
, where
\varpi
is the projection
\Fil^{0}_{c}H_{k}\to\mathrm{gr}^{0}_{c}H_{k}
.

(b)

if
E_{i}\not\subseteq\Fil^{0}H_{k}
, we stop (meaning that
E_{i+1}
will not be defined).

(4)

The height of
(H,\Frob)
is the maximal
h\in\mathbb{N}
such that
E_{h}
is well-defined. If
h=\infty
, then we say that
(H,\Frob)
is
supersingular
.

Our definition differs from that in
[
Ogu01
]
up to a Tate twist. Namely, we divide the Frobenius operator in the original definition by
p
. Moreover, our conjugation filtration is ascending, which is compatible with the convention for F-zips but is opposite to the convention used in
[
Ogu01
]
. In particular, if
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},T}
comes from (the primitive part of) the de Rham cohomology of a K3 surface
X
over
T
as in
[
Ogu01
]
, then our
\Fil^{-1}_{c}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},T}
corresponds to
\Fil^{2}_{c}H^{2}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X/T)
.

Lemma 2.2.2
.

Let
(H,\Frob)
be a K3 crystal over a point
k
.

(a)

(H,\Frob)
has finite height
h
if and only if its slopes contain
\{-1/h,1/h\}
, if and only if
h
is the maximal number such that
p\Frob^{i}(H)\subseteq H
for every
i\leq h
.

(b)

(H,\Frob)
is supersingular if and only if its Newton polygon has constant slope
0
, if and only if
p\Frob^{i}(H)\subseteq H
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

(c)

If
(H,\Frob)
has finite height, then for every
i\leq h
,
\dim E_{i}=i
and
\Fil^{1}H_{k}\not\subseteq E_{i}
.

(d)

If
(H,\Frob)
is supersingular and has Artin invariant
\sigma_{0}
, then
\dim E_{i}=i
for every
i\leq\sigma_{0}
and
E_{i}=E_{\sigma_{0}}
for every
i\geq\sigma_{0}
; moreover,
E_{\sigma_{0}}=E_{\sigma_{0}-1}+\Fil^{1}H_{k}
and
\Fil^{1}H_{k}\not\subseteq E_{i}
for
i<\sigma_{0}
.

Recall that the
Artin invariant

\sigma_{0}
of the supersingular K3 crystal
(H,\Frob)
is the integer such that the Tate module
H^{\Frob=1}
has discriminant isomorphic to
(\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z})^{2\sigma_{0}}
.

Proof.

Every statement is extracted directly from
[
Ogu01
, Prop. 3, Lem. 5]
, except the
\Fil^{1}H_{k}\not\subseteq E_{i}
statement in (c) and (d). The former can be easily deduced from Lem. 4 and Lem. 5(1) of
loc. cit.
, and the latter holds for
i<\sigma_{0}
because
E_{\sigma_{0}}\neq E_{\sigma_{0}-1}
for dimension reasons.
∎

Now, following
[
Ogu01
, p. 334]
, we define a sequence of closed subschemes
\mathcal{F}_{1}=\mathscr{S}_{k}\supseteq\mathcal{F}_{2}\supseteq
of
\mathscr{S}_{k}
as follows:

Definition 2.2.3
.

Define
E_{1}(T):=\Fil^{-1}_{c}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},T}
. For any
\mathscr{S}_{k}
-scheme
T
that factors through
\mathcal{F}_{h}
,
T
factors through
\mathcal{F}_{h+1}
if and only if
E_{h}(T)\subseteq\Fil^{0}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},T}
. If this happens, then we define a coherent subsheaf
E_{h+1}(T)
of
\Fil^{0}_{c}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},T}
by
\varpi^{-1}(\gamma_{0}(E_{h}(T)))
, where
\varpi
denotes the projection
\Fil^{0}_{c}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}\to\mathrm{gr}^{0}_{c}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}
.

Meanwhile, let us consider also the naive definition of Newton stratification which is customary in the literature on Shimura varieties. Namely, we simply define
\mathscr{S}_{h}
to be the closed subset consisting of points of height
\geq h
, and then endow it with the reduced subscheme structure (see e.g.,
[
SZ22
, §2]
). We write
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}
for the supersingular (or basic) locus on
\mathscr{S}_{k}
. We recall the following dimension formula on
\mathscr{S}_{h}
’s from Howard–Pappas
[
HP17
, Thm. C]
(see also Shen–Zhang
[
SZ22
, Cor. 7.3.4]
).

Proposition 2.2.4
.

For the lattice
L
of rank
b+2
, we have the following.

(1)

If
b=2m-1
is odd, then for
h=1,\cdots,m+1
,
\dim\mathscr{S}_{h}=b+1-h
; the supersingular locus
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}=\mathscr{S}_{m+1}
has dimension
m-1
.

(2)

If
b=2m
is even and
\det(V_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})=(-1)^{m}
, then for
h=1,\cdots,m+1
,
\dim\mathscr{S}_{h}=b+1-h
; the supersingular locus
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}=\mathscr{S}_{m+1}
has dimension
m
.

(3)

If
b=2m
is even and
\det(V_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})\neq(-1)^{m}
, then for
h=1,\cdots,m+2
,
\dim\mathscr{S}_{h}=b+1-h
; the supersingular locus
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}=\mathscr{S}_{m+2}
has dimension
m-1
.

In all cases, a general point on a non-supersingular
\mathscr{S}_{h}
has height exactly
h
, i.e.,
\mathscr{S}_{h}\neq\mathscr{S}_{h-1}
.

Within
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}
, let us write
\mathscr{S}_{\infty,\sigma_{0}}
for the closed subscheme of points
s
where
\sigma_{0}(s):=\sigma_{0}(\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris},s},p\Frob_{s})
has Artin-invariant
\leq\sigma_{0}
. We recall that:

Proposition 2.2.5
.

\dim\mathscr{S}_{\infty,\sigma_{0}}=\sigma_{0}-1
.

Proof.

This is essentially due to Ogus
[
Ogu79
, Prop. 4.6.1]
(see also
[
HP17
, Prop. 5.3.2]
, the relation to the supersingular locus of orthogonal Shimura varieties is explained in §7.3 of
loc. cit.
).
∎

Most of the arguments and results in
[
Ogu01
]
hold verbatim in the context of the general orthogonal Shimura varieties, except for Corollary 16 and Theorem 19, which only extend to the case (1). We shall need a statement for case (2). Therefore, below we rephrase and summarize the key inputs in
[
Ogu01
]
which hold regardless of the three cases in our general context.

Lemma 2.2.6
.

Suppose that a
\mathscr{S}_{k}
-scheme
T
factors through
\mathcal{F}_{h}
, and let
i\leq h
be a positive integer.

(a)

Each
E_{i}(T)
is a coherent totally isotropic subsheaf of
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},T}
locally generated by
i
elements, and
E_{0}(T)\subseteq E_{1}(T)\subseteq\cdots\subseteq E_{h}(T)
. In fact, each
E_{i}(T)/E_{i-1}(T)
is locally monogenic.

(b)

If
g:T^{\prime}\to T
is a morphism of
\mathscr{S}_{k}
-schemes, then the natural isomorphism
g^{*}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},T}\to\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},T^{\prime}}
induces a surjection
g^{*}E_{h}(T)\twoheadrightarrow E_{h}(T^{\prime})
, and an isomorphism
g^{*}Q_{i}(T)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}Q_{i}(T^{\prime})
, where
Q_{i}(T):=\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},T}/E_{i}(T)
.

(c)

Suppose that
T
is of finite type over
k
and
T\times_{\mathscr{S}_{k}}\mathscr{S}_{\infty,i-1}=\emptyset
. Then
E_{i}(T)
is a local direct summand of
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},T}
and its formation commutes with base change.

The reader may be concerned that in
Definition

2.2.3
,
F_{h}
is a priori only defined as a subfunctor for the functor of the points of
\mathscr{S}_{k}
. That they are indeed representable by closed subschemes is justified by the surjectivity statement of (b) above (cf.
[
Ogu01
, Prop. 11]
). Indeed, suppose that this is true for
h
. Then
\mathcal{F}_{h+1}
is the closed subscheme of
\mathcal{F}_{h}
defined by the ideal given by the image of the pairing

(7)

\Fil^{1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},\mathcal{F}_{h}}\otimes\left(E_{h}(\mathcal{F}_{h})/E_{h-1}(\mathcal{F}_{h})\right)\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}_{h}}.

Suppose that
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{h+1}
is defined by this ideal. Then a
\mathcal{F}_{h}
-scheme
T
factors through
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{h+1}
if and only if
E_{h}(\mathcal{F}_{h})|_{T}\subseteq\Fil^{0}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},T}
. But there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{h}(\mathcal{F}_{h})|_{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E(T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathrm{gr}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},\mathcal{F}_{h}})|_{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{gr}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},T}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

so the left vertical arrow vanishes if and only if the right one does. Hence
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{h+1}=\mathcal{F}_{h+1}
. In fact, as
E_{h}/E_{h-1}
is locally monogenic for
\mathcal{F}_{h}
, (
7
) implies that
\mathcal{F}_{h+1}
is locally cut out by a single equation from
\mathcal{F}_{h}
. Next, we recall Ogus’ characterization of the tangent spaces of
\mathcal{F}_{h}
.

Proposition 2.2.7
.

(
[
Ogu01
, Cor. 13]
)

The tangent space of
\mathcal{F}_{h}
at a closed point
s
is naturally isomorphic to

(8)

\mathrm{gr}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},s}\otimes\overline{E}_{h-1}(s)^{\perp}\subseteq\mathrm{gr}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},s}\otimes\mathrm{gr}^{0}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},s}=T_{s}\mathscr{S}_{k},

where
\overline{E}_{i}(s)
denotes the image of
E_{i}(s)
in
\mathrm{gr}^{0}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},s}
.

Now we can extend
[
Ogu01
, Cor. 16, Thm. 19]
as follows.

Corollary 2.2.8
.

In the three cases of
Proposition

2.2.4
, we have the following.

(1)

When
b=2m-1
is odd, for every
h\leq m
,
\mathcal{F}_{h}
is reduced and hence is equal to
\mathscr{S}_{h}
,
\mathcal{F}_{m+1}
is genercially nonreduced and
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}=(\mathcal{F}_{m+1})_{\mathrm{red}}
.

(2)

When
b=2m
is even and
\det(V_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})=(-1)^{m}
, for every
h\leq m+1
,
\mathcal{F}_{h}
is reduced and hence is equal to
\mathscr{S}_{h}
, and
\mathcal{F}_{m+1}=\mathscr{S}_{\infty}
. Moreover, for every
h\geq m+1
,
(\mathcal{F}_{h},E_{h})=(\mathcal{F}_{m+1},E_{m+1})
.

(3)

When
b=2m
is even and
\det(V_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})\neq(-1)^{m}
, for every
h\leq m+1
,
\mathcal{F}_{h}
is reduced and hence is equal to
\mathscr{S}_{h}
,
\mathcal{F}_{m+2}
is generically non-reduced and
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}=(\mathcal{F}_{m+2})_{\mathrm{red}}
.

Proof.

All three statements follow from some dimension computations. Define
m^{\prime}=m+1
in cases (1) and (2) and
m+2
in case (3).

First, assume that
h<m^{\prime}
. Take a point
s\in\mathcal{F}_{h}
of height exactly
h
, which is in particular non-supersingular. By
Lemma

2.2.2
(c),
\Fil^{1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},s}\not\subseteq E_{h}(s)
, and hence
\dim\overline{E}_{h-1}(s)=\dim E_{h-1}(s)=h-1
. By
Proposition

2.2.7
,
\dim T_{s}\mathcal{F}_{h}=\dim\overline{E}_{h-1}(s)^{\perp}=b+1-h
, which is equal to
\dim\mathcal{F}_{h}
. This implies that
\mathcal{F}_{h}
is smooth at
s
. In particular,
\mathcal{F}_{h}
is generically reduced. However, since any local complete intersection is Cohen-Macaulay, generic reducedness implies reducedness. Hence
\mathcal{F}_{h}
is reduced and is equal to
\mathscr{S}_{h}
.

Now assume that
h=m^{\prime}
. In case (1),
\dim\mathscr{S}_{\infty}=m-1
, so for a general
s\in\mathscr{S}_{\infty}
,
\sigma_{0}(s)=m
. Since a finite height point on
\mathscr{S}_{k}
has height at most
m
, we already know
(\mathcal{F}_{m+1})_{\mathrm{red}}=\mathscr{S}_{\infty}
. By
Lemma

2.2.2
(d),
\Fil^{1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},s}\subseteq E_{h}(s)
, and hence
\dim\overline{E}_{m}(s)=\dim E_{m}(s)-1=m-1
. By
Proposition

2.2.7
,
\dim T_{s}\mathcal{F}_{m+1}=\dim\overline{E}_{m}(s)^{\perp}=m>\dim\mathcal{F}_{m+1}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{F}_{m+1}
is generically singular. The argument for cases (2) and (3) are entirely similar. What happens for case (2) is that, for a general point
s\in\mathscr{S}_{\infty}
,
\sigma_{0}(s)=m+1
. But from
Lemma

2.2.2
(d) we infer that
\Fil^{1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},s}\not\subseteq E_{m}(s)
, so the same argument as in case (1) implies that
\dim T_{s}\mathcal{F}_{m+1}=m=\dim\mathcal{F}_{m+1}
, so that
\mathcal{F}_{m+1}
is reduced. We remark that case (3) is analogous to case (1), but
\dim T_{s}\mathcal{F}_{m+2}=m+1
, which is greater than
\dim\mathcal{F}_{m-2}
by
2
, so
\mathcal{F}_{m^{\prime}}
in case (3) is more nonreduced than that in case (1).

Finally, we show that in case (2),
(\mathcal{F}_{h},E_{h})
stablizes after
h\geq m+1
. Let us note first that
\mathcal{F}_{h}=\mathcal{F}_{m+1}
because
\mathcal{F}_{h}
is also supported on
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}
, but
\mathcal{F}_{m+1}
is already reduced. Consider
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{m+1}:=\mathcal{F}_{m+1}\smallsetminus\mathscr{S}_{\infty,m}
. By
Lemma

2.2.6
(c), the formation of
E_{m+1}
over
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{m+1}
commutes with base change, and
E_{m+1}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{m+1})
is a direct summand of
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{m+1}}
. This implies that its orthogonal complement
E_{m+1}^{\perp}:=\ker(\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}\to E^{\vee}_{m+1})
is also a direct summand, and its formation also commutes with base change. Now, for every closed point
s\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{m+1}
,
E_{m+1,s}
is an isotropic subspace of
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},s}
of exactly half the dimension, so that
E_{m+1,s}=E^{\perp}_{m+1,s}
. As
E_{m+1}
itself is totally isotropic,
E_{m+1}\subseteq E_{m+1}^{\perp}
and hence they are equal. Since
E_{m+2}
on
\mathcal{F}_{m+2}=\mathcal{F}_{m+1}
is a totally isotropic subsheaf and contains
E_{m+1}
, it is sandwiched by
E_{m+1}
and
E_{m+1}^{\perp}
, so the claims follows by induction.
∎

In the next lemma, we extend
[
Ogu01
, Lem. 2, Prop. 3(2)]
to a general base.
3
3

3

Technically, the lemma below uses that each
\mathcal{F}_{h}
is a local complete intersection. One easily checks that this holds for all cases in
Proposition

2.2.4
, though later we only make use of case (2).
Recall that
\widetilde{T}
denotes the PD-envelope in
\mathscr{S}_{W}
for a closed
\mathscr{S}_{k}
-scheme
T
.

Lemma 2.2.9
.

For every
h\in\mathbb{N}
,

(a)

p\Frob^{j}
is integral on
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{h})
for every
j\leq h
;

(b)

E_{h}=\pi(p\Frob^{h}(\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{h})))+E_{h-1}
over
\mathcal{F}_{h}
; and

(c)

each
\mathcal{F}_{h+1}
is cut out from
\mathcal{F}_{h}
by a section of the line bundle
\omega^{p^{h}-1}|_{\mathcal{F}_{h}}
, where
\omega
is the Hodge bundle
\Fil^{1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}
.

Proof.

Both parts (a) and (b) hold for
h=1
. For (c), we may assume that
T=\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
. Note that the non-ordinary locus
\mathcal{F}_{2}
is cut out by the condition that
p\Frob
kills
\mathrm{gr}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}
, which has already been observed in
[
MST22a
, Lem. 4.9]
(it is unnecessary to assume that the point
P
in
loc. cit.
is supersingular). Let
F_{T}
denote the absolute Frobenius morphism on
T
. Then
p\Frob
induces a linear map
F_{T}^{*}(\mathrm{gr}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR}})\to\mathrm{gr}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}
. But
\mathrm{gr}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}
is dual to
\omega
via the pairing on
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}
, so the conclusion follows.

Now we proceed by induction. Since over
\mathcal{F}_{h}
we already have
E_{h-1}\subseteq\Fil^{0}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}
, using that (b) is true for
i=h
, we have that by (
1
) the condition
E_{h}\subseteq\Fil^{0}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}
over
\mathcal{F}_{h+1}
is equivalent to

(9)

\Frob(p\Frob^{h}(\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{h+1})))=p\Frob^{h+1}(\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{h+1}))\subseteq\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{h+1}).

This proves part (a) for
h+1
. This also proves (c). Indeed, it is easy to see that (
9
) is equivalent to
p\Frob^{h+1}
kills
\mathrm{gr}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}
, so we conclude by the same argument as in the preceeding paragraph.

Now let us prove part (b). Let us set

E^{+}_{h+1}:=\pi(p\Frob^{h+1}(\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{h+1})))

and
E_{h+1}^{\prime}=E^{+}_{h+1}+E_{h}
. Our goal is to prove that
E_{h+1}=E^{\prime}_{h+1}
. First, we note that
E_{h+1}^{+}
(and hence also
E^{\prime}_{h+1}
) lies in
\Fil^{0}_{c}
over
\mathcal{F}_{h+1}
. By (
2
), it suffices to show that

p\Frob^{h+1}(\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{h+1}))\subseteq\Frob(\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{h+1}))\cap\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{h+1}).

This is true by (
9
) and the fact that
p\Frob^{h}(\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{h+1}))\subseteq\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{h+1})
.
Now we have that over
\mathcal{F}_{h+1}
,
E_{h+1}^{\prime}
and
E_{h+1}
are both submodules of
\Fil^{0}_{c}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}
which contains
E_{1}=\Fil^{1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}
. Therefore, to check that they are equal it suffices to check that they are equal modulo
E_{1}
.

By the inductive hypothesis on
E_{h}
, for every
x\in E_{h}
, there exist
\widetilde{x}_{1},\widetilde{x}_{2},\cdots,\widetilde{x}_{h}\in\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{h+1})
and
x=\pi(\sum_{\ell=1}^{h}p\Frob^{\ell}(\widetilde{x}_{\ell}))
.
By the definition of
\gamma_{0}
, its image in
\mathrm{gr}^{0}_{c}
is given by

\Frob(\sum_{\ell=1}^{h}p\Frob^{\ell}(\widetilde{x}_{\ell}))=\sum_{\ell=1}^{h}p\Frob^{\ell+1}(\widetilde{x}_{\ell}).

modulo
\Fil^{1}_{c}
. The conclusion easily follows.
∎

Remark 2.2.10
.

Ogus’ theory in
[
Ogu01
]
has been generalized by Koskivirta, La Porta and Reppen in
[
KPR25
]
. Much of the statements about even dimensional orthogonal Shimura varieties (corresponding to the
D_{n}
-types) above should also follow from the general theory of
loc. cit
. However, for our purposes it is easier to simply extend Ogus’ original approach.

3.
Arithmetic intersection theory

In this section, we will set up the intersection-theoretic framework required to prove Theorem
1.4.1
. We will also reduce the proof of Theorem
1.4.1
to Theorem
3.2.3
, which is a purely local statement at supersingular points.

3.1.
Global intersection

We let
L
as in Section
2
.

3.1.1.
The global Eisenstein series

Let
L
be as in Section
2
. Set
U=\mathbb{C}[L^{\vee}/L]
. Let
\mathrm{Mp}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
denote the metaplectic double cover of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, which is equipped with a Weil representation
\rho_{L}:\mathrm{Mp}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\to\mathrm{GL}(U)
. Let
\kappa=1+\frac{b}{2}
and
M_{\kappa}
be the space of vector-valued modular forms of
\mathrm{Mp}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
of weight
\kappa
with respect to
\rho_{L}
. Let
\{\mathfrak{e}_{\mu}:\mu\in L^{\vee}/L\}
be the standard basis for
U
. Let
E_{0}(\tau)\in M_{\kappa}(\rho_{L})
be the vector valued Eisenstein series with constant term
\mathfrak{e}_{0}
as in
[
Bru17
, §2.1]
. Denote the Fourier expansion of
E_{0}(\tau)
as

(10)

E_{0}(\tau)={\sum_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}}\sum_{m\geq 0,\mu\in\mathbb{Z}+Q(\mu)}q_{L}(m,\mu)q^{m}\mathfrak{e}_{\mu},\text{ where }q=e^{2\pi i\tau}.

Let
q_{L}(m)=q_{L}(m,0)
. The theorem
[
BK01
, Theorem 11]
provides an explicit expression of
q_{L}(m)
. Before stating it, we first recall the notion of local density, which will be used throughout the paper:

Definition 3.1.2
.

For a quadratic
\mathbb{Z}
-lattice
(L,Q)
, a prime
l
and an integer
m
, the local density of
L
representing
m
over
\mathbb{Z}_{l}
is defined as

\delta(l,L,m)=\lim_{a\rightarrow\infty}l^{a(1-\mathrm{rk}\,L)}\#\{v\in L/l^{a}L|Q(v)\equiv m\mod l^{a}\}.

By
[
BK01
, Lem. 5]
, the limit stablizes when
a\geq 1+2v_{\ell}(2m)
(take
d_{\mu}=1
as
\mu=0
).

Given
0\neq D\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
D\equiv 0,1\pmod{4}
, we use
\chi_{D}
to denote the Dirichlet character
\chi_{D}(a)=\left(\frac{D}{a}\right)
,
where
\left(\frac{\cdot}{\cdot}\right)
is the Kronecker symbol. For a Dirichlet character
\chi
we set
\sigma_{s}(m,\chi)=\sum_{d\mid m}\chi(d)d^{s}
.

Theorem 3.1.3

(
[
BK01
, Thm. 11]
)
.

In the above setting, we have:

(a)

For
b
even, the Fourier coefficient
q_{L}(m)
is

-\frac{2^{\kappa}\pi^{\kappa}m^{\frac{b}{2}}\sigma_{1-\kappa}(m,\chi_{(-1)^{\kappa}4\det L})}{\sqrt{|L^{\vee}/L|}\Gamma(\kappa)L(\kappa,\chi_{(-1)^{\kappa}4\det L})}\prod_{\ell\mid 2\det(L)}\delta(\ell,L,m).

(b)

For
b
odd, write
m=m_{0}f^{2}
, where
\gcd(f,2\det L)=1
and
v_{\ell}(m_{0})\in\{0,1\}
for all
\ell\nmid 2\det L
. Then the Fourier coefficient
q_{L}(m)
is

-\frac{2^{\kappa}\pi^{\kappa}m^{\frac{b}{2}}L(\kappa+1/2,\chi_{\mathcal{D}})}{\Gamma(\kappa)\sqrt{|L^{\vee}/L|}\zeta(2\kappa)}\left(\sum_{d\mid f}\mu(d)\chi_{\mathcal{D}}(d)d^{-(\kappa+1/2)}\sigma_{1-2\kappa}(f/d)\right)\prod_{\ell\mid 2\det L}\Big(\frac{\delta(\ell,L,m)}{1-\ell^{-2\kappa}}\Big),

where
\mu
is the Mobius function and
\mathcal{D}=(-1)^{\kappa-1/2}2m_{0}\det L
.

In particular,
|q_{L}(m)|\asymp m^{\frac{b}{2}}
for all
m
representable by
(L,Q)
.

Here the last assertion is a direct consequence of the above explicit formulae and the fact
\delta(\ell,L,m)\asymp 1
(see also
[
MST22a
, §4.3.1]
).
It plays an important role in arithmetic intersection theory.

3.1.4.
Arithmetic intersection theory

Let
k=\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
. In the following we review the arithmetic intersection theory on
\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{k}
. Let the setup be as in §
3.1.1
. We
will use the notation
\overline{\mathcal{Z}(\mu,m)}
to denote the closure of the special divisor
\mathcal{Z}(\mu,m)
in
\mathscr{S}^{\text{tor}}
. To every
\overline{\mathcal{Z}(\mu,m)}
, there is a carefully chosen associated divisor

\mathcal{Z}^{\text{tor}}(\mu,m)=\overline{\mathcal{Z}(\mu,m)}+\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{Pic}(\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{tor}})_{\mathbb{R}},

where
\mathcal{B}
is a certain
\mathbb{R}
-linear combinations of boundary divisors; see
[
Tay22
, (3.1.1)]
. Write
Z^{\mathrm{tor}}(\mu,m)
for the mod
p
reduction of
\mathcal{Z}^{\text{tor}}(\mu,m)
, and let
Z^{\mathrm{tor}}(m)
be
Z^{\mathrm{tor}}(0,m)
. Let
\overline{Z(m)}
be the Zariski closure of
Z(m)
in
\mathscr{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{tor}}
. Consider the following generating series

\Phi^{\text{tor}}_{L}:=\mathcal{L}^{-1}\mathfrak{e}_{0}+\sum_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum_{m\in Q(\mu)+\mathbb{Z}}{Z}^{\text{tor}}(\mu,m)q^{m}\mathfrak{e}_{\mu},\text{ where }q=e^{2\pi i\tau}.

It is known that
\Phi^{\mathrm{tor}}_{L}
is modular, i.e.,
\Phi^{\mathrm{tor}}_{L}\in M_{\kappa}(\rho_{L})\otimes\mathrm{Pic}(\mathscr{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{tor}})_{\mathbb{Q}}
; cf.
[
Tay22
, Theorem 3.1]
.

Let
C
be a proper smooth connected curve with a non-constant map
C\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{k}^{\mathrm{tor}}
whose image lies generically in
\mathscr{S}_{k}
. The modularity of
\Phi^{\mathrm{tor}}_{L}
enables us to relate the intersection number
(\overline{Z(m)}\cdot C)
and the coefficients
q_{L}(m)
of the Eisenstein series
E_{0}(\tau)
:

Proposition 3.1.5

(
[
Tay22
]
, Proposition 4.10)
.

We have
\overline{Z(m)}\cdot C=|q_{L}(m)|(C\cdot\omega)+o(m^{\frac{b}{2}}).

Definition 3.1.6
.

We call a point
P\in C
a
boundary point
if it does not map to
\mathscr{S}_{k}
, and an
interior point
otherwise. We call an interior point
P\in C
a
supersingular point
if it maps to the supersingular locus. We write
i_{P}(C\cdot\overline{Z(m)})
for the intersection multiplicity of
C
and
\overline{Z(m)}
at
P
.

We note that
q_{L}(m)\asymp m^{\frac{b+2}{2}}
.
[
MST22a
]
and
[
Tay22
]
control the local intersection numbers at non-supersingular points. For
X
some integer, let
S_{X}
denote the set of positive integers up to
X
which are relatively prime to
p
.

Theorem 3.1.7

(
[
MST22a
]
,
[
Tay22
]
)
.

(a)

(
[
MST22a
, Proposition 7.11]
) Let
P\in C
be an interior point which doesn’t map to the superinsginular locus. Then, we have

\sum_{m\in S_{X}}i_{P}(C\cdot\overline{Z(m)})=O(X^{\frac{b}{2}}\log X).

(b)

(
[
Tay22
, Proposition 4.12]
) Let
P\in C
be a boundary point. Then we have

\sum_{m\in S_{X}}i_{P}(C\cdot\overline{Z(m)})=O(X^{\frac{b}{2}}\log X).

In order to prove the main theorem, we will need the following result that treats supersingular points.

Theorem 3.1.8
.

Let the setup be as above. Suppose
C
is as in Theorem
1.4.1
. There exists a constant
\alpha<1
independent of
X
such that we have

\sum_{m\in S_{X}}\sum_{P\in C_{\mathrm{ss}}}i_{P}(C\cdot Z(m))<\alpha(C\cdot\omega)\sum_{m\in S_{X}}|q_{L}(m)|+O(X^{\frac{b+1}{2}}).

We now prove Theorem
1.4.1
assuming Theorem
3.1.8
. As the deduction of Theorem
1.4.1
from the above stated local and global results follows the same contours as the proofs in
[
CO06
]
,
[
Cha18
]
,
[
ST20
]
,
[
MST22b
]
,
[
SSTT22
]
, and
[
MST22a
]
, we will be content with a brief account.

Proof of Theorem
1.4.1
assuming Theorem
3.1.8
.

We will obtain a contradiction by assuming that there are only finitely many points
P_{1}\ldots P_{n}
of
C
contained in
\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}}\overline{Z(m)}
. We may assume that the first
r
of these points are supersingular. Then, we have that
C\cdot\overline{Z(m)}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}i_{P_{i}}(C\cdot\overline{Z(m)})
for every positive
m
. For
X
large enough, summing both sides of the equality for
m\in S_{X}
and applying Proposition
3.1.5
, we obtain

\sum_{m\in S_{X}}\left[|q_{L}(m)|(C\cdot\omega)+o(m^{\frac{b}{2}})\right]=\sum_{m\in S_{X}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}i_{P_{i}}(C\cdot\overline{Z(m)}).

By Theorem
3.1.8
, we have

(1-\alpha)(C\cdot\omega)\sum_{m\in S_{X}}|q_{L}(m)|+O(X^{\frac{b+1}{2}})<\sum_{m\in S_{X}}\sum_{i=r+1}^{n}i_{P_{i}}(C\cdot\overline{Z(m)}).

As
P_{i}
is non-supersingular for
i>r
, we apply Theorem
3.1.7
to see that the RHS has order of magnitude
O(X^{\frac{b}{2}}\log X)
. The LHS has order of magnitude
{X^{\frac{b+2}{2}}}
, which yields the required contradiction.
∎

In the next subsection, we will reduce Theorem
3.1.8
(using
[
MST22a
]
) to purely local statement for formal curves passing through supersingular points.

3.2.
The local and global intersection numbers at a supersingular point

Recall that
a
is the largest integer such that
C
maps to the locus
\mathcal{F}_{a+1}\subseteq\mathscr{S}
. Set
h_{P}=i_{P}(C\cdot\mathcal{F}_{a+2})
, the local intersection multiplicity of
C
and
\mathcal{F}_{a+2}
at
P
.

Definition 3.2.1
.

For an integer
m>0
, define
global intersection number of
(C\cdot Z(m))
at
P
to be

g_{P}(m)=\frac{h_{P}}{p^{a+1}-1}|q_{L}(m)|.

Since
\mathcal{F}_{a+2}
is the vanishing locus of a section of
\omega^{p^{a+1}-1}|_{\mathcal{F}_{a+1}}
; cf. Lemma
2.2.9
, we immediately get

(11)

(C\cdot\omega)|q_{L}(m)|=\sum_{P\in C\cap\mathcal{F}_{a+2}}g_{P}(m).

Now, let
P\in C_{\mathrm{ss}}
be a supersingular point, and let
t
be a local parameter at
P
. Let
\mathcal{A}_{r}:=\mathcal{A}/\Spf k[\![t]\!]/(t^{r})
denote the pullback of
\mathcal{A}
to the
r
th infinitesimal neighbourhood of
C
at
P
.

Definition 3.2.2
.

We let
L_{P,r}=L(\mathcal{A}_{r})
. Let
L_{P,1}\subset L^{\prime}_{P,1}\subset L_{P,1}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
be a lattice that is maximal at all primes
\ell\neq p
, and let
L^{\prime}_{P,r}=L_{P,r}\cap L^{\prime}_{P,1}
.

By construction, we have that
L_{P,r}\subset L_{P,r-1}
. The equation below follows directly from the moduli interpretation of
Z(m)
.

(12)

i_{P}(C\cdot Z(m))=\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\#\{v\in L_{P,r}:Q(v)=m\}.

For the rest of this section, we will reduce Theorem
3.1.8
to the purely local result stated just below:

Theorem 3.2.3
.

Notation as above. Let
q_{L^{\prime}_{P,r}}(m)
denote the
m
th Fourier coefficient of the Eisenstein series associated to the lattice
L^{\prime}_{P,r}
, and define the series

S^{\prime}(m):=\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{q_{L^{\prime}_{P,r}}(m)}{g_{P}(m)}.

There is a constant
\alpha<1
such that for any
m
not a multiple of
p
, we have
S^{\prime}(m)<\alpha
.

Remark 3.2.4
.

By the first displayed equation in the proof of Lemma 7.16 of
[
MST22a
]
, we have that

q_{L^{\prime}_{P,r}}(m)=\frac{q_{L}(m)\delta(p,L^{\prime}_{P,r},m)}{\sqrt{[L^{\prime,\vee}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}\ :\ L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}]}\left(1-\chi_{(-1)^{1+b/2}4\det L}(p)p^{-1-b/2}\right)}.

So each term of the sum
S^{\prime}(m)
can be explicitly written as

\frac{q_{L^{\prime}_{P,r}}(m)}{g_{P}(m)}=\frac{p^{a+1}-1}{h_{P}}\left(1-\chi_{(-1)^{1+b/2}4\det L}(p)p^{-1-b/2}\right)^{-1}\frac{\delta(p,L^{\prime}_{P,r},m)}{\sqrt{[L^{\prime,\vee}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}\ :\ L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}]}}.

Proof of Theorem
3.1.8
assuming Theorem
3.2.3
.

The implication follows directly from
[
MST22a
, Section 7]
so we will be brief. Clearly, we have that
i_{P}(C\cdot Z(m))\leq\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\#\{v\in L^{\prime}_{P,r}:q(v)=m\}
. An analysis identical to the one in
[
MST22a
, §7.12, Proposition 7.13, §7.14, Theorem 7.18]
allows us to replace
i_{P}(C\cdot Z(m))
in Theorem
3.1.8
with
\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}q_{L^{\prime}_{P,r}}(m)
up to an error term.

Assuming Theorem
3.2.3
, we have
\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}q_{L^{\prime}_{P,r}}(m)<\alpha g_{P}(m)
for some
\alpha<1
. Using (
11
), we have

\sum_{P\in C_{\mathrm{ss}}}g_{P}(m)\leq(C\cdot\omega)|q_{L}(m)|.

Summing up over
P\in C_{\mathrm{ss}}
and
m\in S_{X}
, and possibly enlarging
\alpha
, we immediately find that

\sum_{m\in S_{X}}\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}q_{L^{\prime}_{P,r}}(m)<\alpha\sum_{m\in S_{X}}\sum_{P\in C_{\mathrm{ss}}}g_{P}(m)\leq\alpha(C\cdot\omega)\sum_{m\in S_{X}}|q_{L}(m)|.

This implies Theorem
3.1.8
as required.
∎

4.
Local structure of Shimura varieties at a superspecial point

4.1.
Local structure in characteristic
p

Let
k
be a perfect field of characterisic
p
. We recall the group-theoretic description of
[
Kis10
]
of the formal neighborhood of a closed point
P
in
\mathscr{S}
, which we shall denote by
\widehat{\mathscr{S}}_{P}
.

Let us write the K3 crystal
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris},P}
simply as
\mathbb{L}
. Then recall that
\mathbb{L}\otimes_{W}k=\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR},P}
is equipped with a Hodge filtration. Let
\mu:\mathbb{G}_{m,W}\to\text{SO}(\mathbb{L})
be a co-character (referred to as the “Hodge co-character”) whose reduction modulo
p
splits the Hodge filtration. Define
U
to be the opposite unipotent subgroup in
\mathrm{SO}(\mathbb{L})
relative to
\mu
, and let
\Spf(R)
denote the completion of
U
at the identity section. Let
\sigma
be a lift of the Frobenius endomorphism on
R\otimes_{W}k
, and let
u\in U(R)
be the tautological point. Then, according to
[
Kis10
, §§1.4, 1.5]
(see also
[
KLSS25
, Proposition 4.7]
), there exists an isomorphism
\mathscr{S}^{/P}_{W}\simeq\Spf(R)
through which
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(R)
can be identified with
\mathbb{L}\otimes_{W}R
such that the Frobenius action is given by
\Frob=u\circ(\varphi\otimes\sigma)
. Let us fix these identifications.

Using the above description, together with Ogus’ results on supersingular K3 crystals (
[
Ogu79
]
), we can express F-crystal
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}
over
\mathscr{S}^{/P}_{W}
in terms of local coordinates. This has been done in
[
MST22a
]
so we briefly recap the main points for readers’ convenience and set up some notations. First, the Tate module
\mathcal{L}:=\mathbb{L}^{\varphi=1}=
orthogonally splits into two parts
\mathcal{L}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{1}
such that , if we denote by
\langle-,-\rangle_{i}
the symmetric pairing on
\mathcal{L}_{i}
, then
\langle-,-\rangle_{1}
is self-dual over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
whereas
\langle-,-\rangle_{0}
is divisible by
p
and
p^{-1}\langle-,-\rangle_{0}
is self-dual. Define
\mathbb{L}_{1}=\mathcal{L}_{1}\otimes W
. Then
\mathbb{L}_{1}
is a direct summand of
\mathbb{L}
, which splits into
\mathbb{L}_{0}\oplus\mathbb{L}_{1}
. The
W
-lattice
\mathbb{L}_{0}
is self-dual such that
\mathbb{L}_{0}^{\varphi=1}=\mathcal{L}_{0}
and
\mathcal{L}_{0}\otimes W\subseteq\mathbb{L}_{0}\subseteq p^{-1}(\mathcal{L}_{0}\otimes W)
. Set
t_{P}:=\mathrm{rk}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{L}_{0}
. Then
2\mid t_{P}
and
a_{P}:=t_{P}/2
is called the
Artin-invariant
of
\mathbb{L}
. The point
P
is defined to be
superspecial
if
a_{P}=1
. In this section, we address the case when
P
is a superspecial point, and will deal with more general supersingular points in a later section.

As in
[
MST22a
, Section 4.7]
, we have the following trichotomy.

(1)

\textrm{rank }\mathcal{L}_{1}
is odd. We call this the
odd case
, and it corresponds to
b
odd.

(2)

\textrm{rank }\mathcal{L}_{1}=2m
and
\mathcal{L}_{1}
is a split quadratic space. We call this the
split case
, and it corresponds to
b
even and
L\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}

not
being split.

(3)

\textrm{rank }\mathcal{L}_{1}=2m
and
\mathcal{L}_{1}
is
not
a split quadratic space. We call this the
inert case
, and it corresponds to
b
even and
L\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}

being split
.

Remark 4.1.1
.

The cases (1), (2), (3) above match up with the cases (1), (2), (3) in
Proposition

2.2.4
. This is essentially a consequence of the Hasse–Minkowski theorem, and the reader may refer to the proof of
[
HP17
, Thm. 7.2.4]
for this argument.

Remark 4.1.2
.

We note that we are overloading the symbol
m
.
Earlier,
m
indexed special divisors, whereas we are now using
m
to denote half the rank of
\mathcal{L}_{1}
. We will explicitly alert the reader before reverting to the former usage.

Remark 4.1.3
.

As in
[
MST22a
]
, the main theorem in the setting of the inert case follows from the main theorem in the setting of the split case by embedding inside a larger dimensional Shimura variety. Indeed, in the inert setting (under the assumptions of Theorem
1.4.1
), the local calculations needed to establish the inequality in Theorem
3.2.3
follow by embedding the global lattice
L
inside a higher-rank maximal lattice
L^{\prime}
which is
self-dual but not
split at
p
(which therefore corresponds to the split case), calculating the quantities
q_{L^{\prime}}(m)
, and using this to calculate the quantities
q_{L}(m)
to obtain the main theorem. Henceforth, we will constrain ourselves to the split case. In this case, the term with the character
\chi
equals
\frac{1}{(1+p^{-(m+1)})}
.

We will briefly need the odd case (despite the statement of Theorem
1.4.1
being trivial) to analyze singularities of Newton strata.
For the rest of this section, and in Sections
5
,
6
, and
7
, we will work in the split case unless explicitly stated otherwise.

4.2.
Equations for Newton strata

4.2.1.

In order to compute with coordinates, we need to introduce bases to
\mathbb{L}
. For
\mathbb{L}_{1}
, we just take any basis
\{e_{i},f_{i}\}
for
\mathcal{L}_{1}
such that the intersection matrix is given by
\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}0&I\\
\hline\cr I&0\end{array}\right]
. Using work of Ogus as in
[
MST22b
]
and
[
MST22a
]
,
\mathcal{L}_{0}
has a basis
e^{\prime},f^{\prime}
such that
\langle e^{\prime},e^{\prime}\rangle_{0}=2p,\langle f^{\prime},f^{\prime}\rangle_{0}=-2\lambda^{2}p
and
\langle e^{\prime},f^{\prime}\rangle_{0}=0
, where
\lambda\in W(\mathbb{F}_{p^{2}})^{\times}
satisfies
\sigma(\lambda)=-\lambda
(cf.
[
MST22a
, Lem. 4.3]
). Then, a basis of
\mathbb{L}_{0}
is given by
w^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2\lambda p}(\lambda e^{\prime}+f^{\prime}),v^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2\lambda}(\lambda e^{\prime}-f^{\prime})
.
From
[
MST22a
, §4.8]
, we can extract that for some choice of coordinates

(13)

\mathscr{S}^{/P}_{W}=R\simeq W[\![x_{1},\cdots,x_{m},y_{1},\cdots,y_{m}]\!]

the operator
\Frob
on
\mathbb{L}\otimes R
(identified with
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(R)
) is given by the matrix
(uB)\circ\sigma

where

(14)

u=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc|cc}1&Q&\mathbf{x}&\mathbf{y}\\
&1&&\\
\hline\cr&-\mathbf{y}^{t}&I_{m}&\\
&-\mathbf{x}^{t}&&I_{m}\end{array}\right]\text{ and }B=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc|cc}&p&&\\
p^{-1}&&&\\
\hline\cr&&I_{m}&\\
&&&I_{m}\end{array}\right]\Rightarrow uB=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc|cc}p^{-1}Q&p&\mathbf{x}&\mathbf{y}\\
p^{-1}&&&\\
\hline\cr-p^{-1}\mathbf{y}^{t}&&I_{m}&\\
-p^{-1}\mathbf{x}^{t}&&&I_{m}\end{array}\right]

with respect to the basis
\{v^{\prime},w^{\prime},e_{1},\cdots,e_{m},f_{1},\cdots,f_{m}\}
.
Note that compared to
loc. cit.
, we switched the role played by the letters
e_{i},f_{i},x_{i},y_{i}
’s with
e^{\prime}_{i},f^{\prime}_{i},x^{\prime}_{i},y^{\prime}_{i}
, so that now basis vectors or coordinates related to
\mathbb{L}_{0}
are labelled with a (
′
). In the above,
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
are the row vectors
[x_{1},\cdots,x_{m}]
and
[y_{1},\cdots,y_{m}]
respectively. Here,
Q=-\sum_{k=1}^{m}x_{k}y_{k}=-\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}^{t}
.

For a matrix
M
we denote
M^{(i)}
the matrix whose entries are twisted by
\sigma^{i}
, set

(15)

Q_{j}=-\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left(x_{k}y^{(j)}_{k}+x^{(j)}_{k}y_{k}\right)=-\left(\mathbf{x}^{(j)}\mathbf{y}^{t}+\mathbf{x}(\mathbf{y}^{(j)})^{t}\right)\in W[\![x_{i},y_{i}]\!].

In particular, we have that
Q=\frac{1}{2}Q_{0}
.

Remark 4.2.2
.

By abuse of notation, we will also use
Q_{i}
to denote the image of
Q_{i}
in the ring
\mathbb{F}[\![x_{i},y_{i}]\!]
, just as what we do in the next proposition.

Proposition 4.2.3
.

In case (2) of
Proposition

2.2.4
, we have the following.

(a)

for
0\leq h<m-1
, the NP strata
\mathcal{F}_{h+2}
of height
(h+2)
is cut out by
Q_{0},Q_{1},...,Q_{h}
;

(b)

the supersingular strata
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}=\mathcal{F}_{m+1}
is cut out by
Q_{0},Q_{1},...,Q_{m-1}

(c)

for
h\geq m
,
Q_{h}
is contained in the ideal
(Q_{0},Q_{1},...,Q_{m-1})

Proof.

Let us first show that
\mathcal{F}_{h+1}
is cut out from
\mathcal{F}_{h}
by
Q_{h-1}=0
. By
Lemma

2.2.9
,
\pi(p\Frob^{h})
defines
\sigma
-semilinear endormorphism on
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{\mathcal{F}_{h}}
. Then
E_{h}=\pi(p\Frob^{h})(\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR}})+E_{h-1}
. By definition,
\mathcal{F}_{h+1}
is cut out from
\mathcal{F}_{h}
by the condition that
E_{h}\subseteq\Fil^{0}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}
, which is equivalent to

(16)

\pi(p\Frob^{h})=0\textrm{ on }\mathrm{gr}^{-1}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR}},

since
E_{h-1}
is already in
\Fil^{0}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}
over
\mathcal{F}_{h}
.

Let
A_{i}
be the
R[1/p]
-matrix such that
p\Frob^{i}=A_{i}\circ\sigma^{i}
. Via the base change
R\to\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{i}}
,
A_{i}
defines a matrix with
\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{i}}[1/p]
-coefficients, which by the integrality condition in fact has coefficients in
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}
. Reducing mod
p
, we obtain a matrix
\overline{A}_{i}
such that
\pi(p\Frob^{i})=\overline{A}_{i}\circ\sigma^{i}
on
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}|_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}
. By (
16
), we are only concerned with the first column of
\overline{A}_{i}
, i.e.,
\pi(p\Frob^{i})([1,0,\cdots,0]^{t})
.

We make the following definitions for all
i,n\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

Let
W_{i}(n)
denote the length
n
words in
\{Q_{j}^{(\beta)}|0\leq j\leq i,\beta\in\mathbb{N}\}
.

•

Let
\Delta_{i}(d)
denote the set of sums of the elements of the form
p^{-r}w
for
w\in W_{i}(n)
and
n-r=d
.

For book-keeping set them to be empty when
i=-1
. One can easily prove by induction that for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Frob^{i}(e_{1})\in R[1/p]^{2+2m}
is of the following form:

(17)

\begin{bmatrix}a+p^{-1}Q_{i-1}\\
b\\
-\left(\sum_{\ell=0}^{i-2}c_{\ell}(\mathbf{y}^{t})^{(\ell)}\right)-p^{-1}(\mathbf{y}^{t})^{(i-1)}\\
