-\left(\sum_{\ell=0}^{i-2}c_{\ell}(\mathbf{x}^{t})^{(\ell)}\right)-p^{-1}(\mathbf{x}^{t})^{(i-1)}\end{bmatrix}\text{ such that }a\in\Delta_{i-2}(0)\text{ and }b,c_{0},\cdots,c_{i-2}\in\Delta_{i-2}(-1).

For example, the first entry of
\Frob^{i+1}(e_{1})=uB\cdot\Frob^{i}(e_{1})^{(1)}
is given by

(18)

[p^{-1}Qa^{(1)}+p^{-2}QQ_{i-1}^{(1)}+pb^{(1)}-\sum_{\ell=0}^{i-2}c_{\ell}^{(1)}Q_{\ell+1}]+p^{-1}Q_{i},

and the term in the bracket is the new
a
for
\Frob^{i+1}(e_{1})
, which indeed lies in
\Delta_{i-1}(0)
. We leave the checking of the other entries to the reader.

Note that for every
i\leq h
, since
\mathcal{F}_{i}=R_{0}/(Q_{0},\cdots,Q_{i-2})
, we have
p\mid Q_{j}
inside
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
for every
j\leq i-2
, and hence
p^{-d}\Delta_{i}(d)
defines an element in
\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{i}}
for all
d\in\mathbb{Z}
. Using this observation, one checks that over
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{h}
,
p\Frob^{h}(e_{1})
indeed has coefficients in
\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{h}}
and modulo
p
, its first entry is just
Q_{h-1}
. Therefore, the condition (
16
) amounts to
Q_{h-1}=0
, or equivalently,
\mathcal{F}_{h+1}
is cut out from
\mathcal{F}_{h}
by
Q_{h-1}
, as desired.

Note that the above argument has no restriction on
h
, including those
h\geq m+1
. Parts (2) follows from the above when
h=m+1
, and part (3) follow from the fact that
(\mathcal{F}_{h},E_{h})=(\mathcal{F}_{m+1},E_{m+1})
for all
h\geq m+1
.
∎

Remark 4.2.4
.

We now summarize the procedure above, since we need to use it again but do not wish to repeat the details. First, we select a basis for
\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{cris},P}(W)
such that, upon reduction modulo
p
, all but the first basis vector lie in
\mathrm{Fil}^{0}\mathbf{L}_{\mathrm{dR}}
. We then express the Frobenius operator as
b\sigma
for an explicit matrix
b
. Next, we write the tautological point
u
in the local coordinates of the opposite unipotent subgroup for a chosen cocharacter
\mu
that splits the Hodge filtration mod
p
. The remainder of the argument is essentially formalizing the following: the condition that the
(1,1)
-entries of the matrices
p(ub),\dots,p(ub)(ub)^{(h)}
vanish modulo
p
is equivalent to the vanishing of the polynomials
Q_{0},\dots,Q_{h}
.

5.
Decay of special endomorphisms: superspecial points.

Let us keep using the notations in the preceeding section. In particular,
P
is a superspecial point on
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}}
and assume that we are in the split case. Suppose that
v\in\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{P})
is a formal special endomorphism and
C
is a formal curve at
P
on
\mathscr{S}_{\mathbb{F}}
which is not generically ordinary. In this section, we study the decay rate of
v
, which measures how far
v
extends along
C
.

To formulate this notion, let us make a preliminary definition.

Definition 5.0.1
.

Given a formal special endomorphism
v\in\mathcal{L}
, we let
x_{v}\in\mathbb{F}[\![x_{i},y_{i}]\!]
denote the equation whose vanishing is the locus to which
v
extends.

For the fact that the locus to which
v
extends is indeed cut out by a single equation, the reader may refer to
[
MP16
, Cor. 5.17]
. Technically,
x_{v}
is only defined up to
\mathbb{F}^{\times}
. For convenience we fix one by asking its leading term to be
1
with respect to the lexicographic order of
x_{1},\cdots,x_{m},y_{1},\cdots,y_{m}
.
Note that then the coordinates we have chosen for
\mathscr{S}^{/P}_{W}
can be re-interpreted by
x_{i}=x_{e_{i}},y_{i}=x_{f_{i}}
.

For the purpose of this section, we shall express the Frobenius operator
\Frob
on
\mathbb{L}[1/p]\otimes R
with respect to the Frobenius-invariant basis
\{e^{\prime},f^{\prime},e_{i},f_{i}\}_{i=1,\cdots,m}
of
\mathbb{L}[1/p]
, as opposed to
\{v^{\prime},w^{\prime},e_{i},f_{i}\}_{i=1,\cdots,m}
used in the preceeding section.
4
4

4

We again remark that the letter
m
is used in relation to the rank of
\mathbb{L}
, unlike in Section
3
. We will alert the reader if we resume using
m
in the context of special divisors.
Now, with respect to
\{e^{\prime},f^{\prime},e_{i},f_{i}\}_{i=1,\cdots,m}
,
\Frob=(1+F)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\circ$}}}\sigma
, where

(19)

F=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc|cc}\frac{Q}{2p}&\frac{-\lambda Q}{2p}&\frac{\mathbf{x}}{2p}&\frac{\mathbf{y}}{2p}\\
\frac{Q}{2p\lambda}&\frac{-Q}{2p}&\frac{\mathbf{x}}{2p\lambda}&\frac{\mathbf{y}}{2p\lambda}\\
\hline\cr-\mathbf{y}^{t}&\lambda\mathbf{y}^{t}&&\\
-\mathbf{x}^{t}&\lambda\mathbf{x}^{t}&&\end{array}\right]

Set
F_{\infty}=\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}(I+F^{(i)})
. Recall that
\mathbf{x}=[x_{1},...,x_{m}]
,
\mathbf{y}=[y_{1},...,y_{m}]
and we defined
Q_{i}
’s in (
15
).

5.1.
Explicit computation of
F_{\infty}

In this subsection, we explicitly compute
F_{\infty}
over the non-ordinary Newton strata. The assumption implies that
Q=0
. So the upper left block of
F
in (
19
) is 0. Then the upper right, and lower left blocks of
F
can be written as

(20)

\frac{1}{2p}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\lambda^{-1}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}&\mathbf{y}\end{bmatrix},\text{ and }\begin{bmatrix}-\mathbf{y}^{t}\\
-\mathbf{x}^{t}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&-\lambda\end{bmatrix},

respectively. The following are some basic observations:

Lemma 5.1.1
.

Let
0\leq i<j
, then

(a)

\begin{bmatrix}1&-\lambda\end{bmatrix}^{(i)}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\lambda^{-1}\end{bmatrix}^{(j)}=1-(-1)^{j-i}{=2\delta_{i<j}}
.

(b)

\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}&\mathbf{y}\end{bmatrix}^{(i)}\begin{bmatrix}-\mathbf{y}^{t}\\
-\mathbf{x}^{t}\end{bmatrix}^{(j)}=Q_{j-i}^{(i)}
.

Proof.

Follows by a direct computation.
∎

Notation 5.1.2
.

We make several definitions, whose importance will be clear in a short while.
For
n\geq 0
, let
l_{1}<l_{2}<...<l_{2n}
be an increasing chain of
2n
nonnegative integers. For
n>0
, define

(21)

\displaystyle Q_{l_{1}<l_{2}<...<l_{2n}}=\prod_{k=1}^{n}Q_{l_{2k}-l_{2k-1}}^{(l_{2k-1})}.

(22)

\displaystyle\delta_{l_{1}<l_{2}<...<l_{2n}}=\prod_{k=1}^{n}\frac{1}{2}[{1-(-1)^{l_{2k}-l_{2k-1}}}].

When
n=0
, we also define by default

Q_{\varnothing}=1,\delta_{\varnothing}=1.

It is immediate from the definition that
\delta_{l_{1}<l_{2}<...<l_{2n}}=\prod_{k=1}^{n}\delta_{l_{2k-1}<l_{2k}}
can only take value in
\{0,1\}
. It is 1 only when
l_{2k}+l_{2k-1}\equiv 1(\mod 2)
for all
k=1,2,...,n
. Otherwise it is 0.

We now give a thorough description of
F_{\infty}
:

Lemma 5.1.3
.

Notation as above. We have

\displaystyle F_{\infty}=I+\sum_{n\geq 1}p^{-n}F_{n},

where
F_{n}=\begin{bmatrix}X_{n}&Y_{n}\\
Z_{n}&W_{n}\end{bmatrix}
, and
X_{n}
is the upper left
2\times 2
block,
Y_{n}
is the upper right
2\times 2m
block,
Z_{n}
is the lower left
2m\times 2
block, and
W_{n}
is the lower left
2m\times 2m
block, with explicit description as follows:

\displaystyle X_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{2n}}\frac{1}{2}\delta_{i_{2}<i_{3}<...<i_{2n-2}<i_{2n-1}}Q_{i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{2n}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
(-1)^{i_{1}}\lambda^{-1}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&(-1)^{1+i_{2n}}\lambda\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle Y_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{2n-1}}\frac{1}{2}\delta_{i_{2}<i_{3}<...<i_{2n-2}<i_{2n-1}}Q_{i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{2n-2}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
(-1)^{i_{1}}\lambda^{-1}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}&\mathbf{y}\end{bmatrix}^{(i_{2n-1})},

\displaystyle Z_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq i_{0}<i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{2n}}\delta_{i_{0}<i_{1}<...<i_{2n-2}<i_{2n-1}}Q_{i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{2n}}\begin{bmatrix}-\mathbf{y}^{t}\\
-\mathbf{x}^{t}\end{bmatrix}^{(i_{0})}\begin{bmatrix}1&(-1)^{1+i_{2n}}\lambda\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle W_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq i_{0}<i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{2n-1}}\delta_{i_{0}<i_{1}<...<i_{2n-2}<i_{2n-1}}Q_{i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{2n-2}}\begin{bmatrix}-\mathbf{y}^{t}\\
-\mathbf{x}^{t}\end{bmatrix}^{(i_{0})}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}&\mathbf{y}\end{bmatrix}^{(i_{2n-1})}.

Proof.

This follows easily from a direct computation.
∎

Lemma 5.1.4

(Recursion formula)
.

Notation as above. Let
X_{n}^{+}
(resp.
Y_{n}^{+}
) be the sub-sum of
X_{n}
(resp.
Y_{n}
) with
i_{1}
even. For
n\geq 1
, define
\mathbf{U}_{n}
to be the first row of
\begin{bmatrix}X_{n}^{+}&Y_{n}^{+}\end{bmatrix}
.
Then for
r>0
, we have

(23)

\mathbf{U}_{n+r}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{2r}\\
i_{1}\text{ even}\end{subarray}}\delta_{i_{2}<i_{3}<...<i_{2r-1}}Q_{i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{2r}}\mathbf{U}^{(1+i_{2r})}_{n}.

Moreover, the matrix
F_{n}
can be recovered from
\mathbf{U}_{n}
as follows:

(24)

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}X_{n}&Y_{n}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\lambda^{-1}\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{U}_{n}+\begin{bmatrix}1\\
-\lambda^{-1}\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{U}_{n}^{(1)},

(25)

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}Z_{n}&W_{n}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq i_{0}}2\begin{bmatrix}-\mathbf{y}^{t}\\
-\mathbf{x}^{t}\end{bmatrix}^{(i_{0})}\mathbf{U}^{(1+i_{0})}_{n}.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
5.1.3
and a direct computation.
∎

We shall now use
F_{\infty}
to study the deformation theory of a special endomorphism.
Recall that
\{e^{\prime},f^{\prime},e_{i},f_{i}\}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-basis for
\mathcal{L}
.

Notation 5.1.5
.

For an element
v\in\mathcal{L}
, we define

(26)

\tilde{v}:=F_{\infty}v\in\mathbb{L}(W)\otimes K[\![x_{i},y_{i}]\!].

There exists a rank
2m+2
column vector
\mathbf{c}
(resp.
\tilde{\mathbf{c}}
) valued in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
(resp.
K[\![x_{i},y_{i}]\!]
) such that

(27)

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=(e^{\prime},f^{\prime};e_{i},f_{i})\mathbf{c},

\displaystyle\tilde{v}

\displaystyle=(e^{\prime},f^{\prime};e_{i},f_{i})\tilde{\mathbf{c}}.

Express
\mathbf{c}
as
\sum_{k\geq 0}\mathbf{c}_{k}p^{k}
, where each entry of
\mathbf{c}_{k}
is a Teichmüller lift of some element in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. Let
\mathbf{U}_{n}
be as in Lemma
5.1.4
. Define

(28)

\displaystyle D_{n}(v)

\displaystyle:=2\sum_{k\geq 0}\mathbf{U}_{n+k}\mathbf{c}_{k}\in W[\![x_{i},y_{i}]\!].

\displaystyle\overline{D}_{n}(v)

\displaystyle:={D}_{n}(v)\bmod p\in\mathbb{F}[\![x_{i},y_{i}]\!].

Lemma 5.1.6
.

The following are true:

(a)

For
l\geq 0
,
D_{n}(v)^{(l)}-D_{n}(v)^{p^{l}}\in p^{l}W[\![x_{i},y_{i}]\!]
.

(b)

For
r>0
, we have:

D_{n+r}(v)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{2r}\\
i_{1}\text{ even}\end{subarray}}\delta_{i_{2}<i_{3}<...<i_{2r-1}}Q_{i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{2r}}D_{n}(v)^{(1+i_{2r})}.

(c)

The first two entries of
\tilde{\mathbf{c}}
are

\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n\geq 1}p^{-n}\left(\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\lambda^{-1}\end{bmatrix}D_{n}(v)+\begin{bmatrix}1\\
-\lambda^{-1}\end{bmatrix}D_{n}(v)^{(1)}\right)+O(1).

Here by
O(1)
we mean that the entries have coefficients in
W
. The last
2m
entries of
\tilde{\mathbf{c}}
are

\sum_{n\geq 1}p^{-n}\left(\sum_{0\leq i_{0}}\begin{bmatrix}-\mathbf{y}^{t}\\
-\mathbf{x}^{t}\end{bmatrix}^{(i_{0})}D_{n}(v)^{(1+i_{0})}\right)+O(1).

(d)

If we write
\tilde{v}=(v^{\prime},w^{\prime};e_{i},f_{i})\tilde{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}
, then the first two entries of
\tilde{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}
are
\sum_{n\geq 1}p^{-n}D_{n}(v)^{(1)}+O(1)
and
\sum_{n\geq 1}p^{-n}D_{n+1}(v)+O(1)
, respectively. The last
2m
entries of
\tilde{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}
coincide with that of
\tilde{\mathbf{c}}
.

Proof.

Note that
a^{(l)}=a^{p^{l}}
for a Teichmüller lift. For part (a), we can reduce to showing that if
u
is an entry of
2\mathbf{U}_{n}
, then
u^{(l)}\equiv u^{p^{l}}(\bmod p^{l})
. This follows from the explicit formulae in Lemma
5.1.3
. Part (b) and part (c) are direct consequences of Lemma
5.1.4
. Part (d) follows from (c) and the change of basis
w^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2\lambda p}(\lambda e^{\prime}+f^{\prime}),v^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2\lambda}(\lambda e^{\prime}-f^{\prime})
.
∎

Next we will show that
D_{n}(v)
controls the deformation of
v
.

Lemma 5.1.7
.

Consider a map
\iota:\Spf\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]\rightarrow\Spf\mathbb{F}[\![x_{i},y_{i}]\!]
. Lift
\iota
to a map
\Spf W[\![t]\!]\rightarrow\Spf W[\![x_{i},y_{i}]\!]
and by abuse of notation, still call it
\iota
. Let
I=(t^{s}),0\leq s\leq\infty
be an ideal of
\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]
, and
\tilde{I}=(p,t^{s})
be the preimage of
I
in
W[\![t]\!]
. A special endomorphism
v
deforms to
\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]/I)
if and only if
{\iota}^{*}\tilde{v}\in\mathbb{L}(W)\otimes\hat{D}_{I}
, where
\hat{D}_{I}=\hat{D}_{W[\![t]\!]}(\tilde{I})\subseteq K[\![t]\!]
is the
p
-adic completion of the PD-envelope of
\tilde{I}
.

Proof.

We use the standard terminology “MIC” for module with integrable connection. Recall that
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}:=\mathbb{L}_{\mathscr{S}^{/P}_{\mathbb{F}}}
can be identified with a filtered Frobenius MIC over
W[\![x_{i},y_{i}]\!]
, i.e., a quadruple

(\mathbb{L}(W)\otimes W[\![x_{i},y_{i}]\!],\nabla,\Fil^{\bullet}\mathbb{L}(W)\otimes W[\![x_{i},y_{i}]\!],F)
. Let
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{I}
be the base change of
\mathbb{L}_{\mathscr{S}^{/P}_{\mathbb{F}}}
to
\hat{D}_{I}
along the map
W[\![x_{i},y_{i}]\!]\rightarrow W[\![t]\!]\hookrightarrow\hat{D}_{I}
, which is again a quadruple

(\mathbb{L}(W)\otimes\hat{D}_{I},\nabla\otimes\hat{D}_{I},\Fil^{\bullet}\mathbb{L}(W)\otimes\hat{D}_{I},F)
.

By
[
dJ95
, §2.2, §2.3]
, there is a natural functor from the category of
p
-divisible groups over
\Spf(\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]/I)
to Dieudonné modules over
\hat{D}_{I}
. Since
\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]/I
is a complete intersection scheme,
[
DM99
, Theorem 4.6]

further implies that the aforementioned natural functor is fully faithful. As a result, there exists a formal special endomorphism of
\mathcal{A}_{\Spf(\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]/I)}[p^{\infty}]
deforming
v
, if and only if there exists an endomorphism of the corresponding Dieudonné modules over
\hat{D}_{I}
deforming
v
, if and only if there is a horizontal section of
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{I}
extending
v
.

Recall that
\tilde{v}
is the unique horizontal section of the MIC
(\mathbb{L}(W)\otimes K[\![x_{i},y_{i}]\!],\nabla\otimes K[\![x_{i},y_{i}]\!])
extending
v
, and
{\iota}^{*}\tilde{v}
is the unique horizontal section of the MIC
(\mathbb{L}(W)\otimes K[\![t]\!],\nabla\otimes K[\![t]\!])
extending
v
. If
v
deforms to
\Spf(\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]/I)
, then there is a horizontal section
v_{I}
of
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{I}
extending
v
. Then the base change of
v_{I}
along the natural embedding
\hat{D}_{I}\hookrightarrow K[\![t]\!]
identifies with
{\iota}^{*}\tilde{v}
. In other words,
{\iota}^{*}\tilde{v}=v_{I}\in\mathbb{L}(W)\otimes\hat{D}_{I}
. Conversely, if

{\iota}^{*}\tilde{v}\in\mathbb{L}(W)\otimes\hat{D}_{I}
, the same argument shows that it must be the horizontal section of
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{I}
extending
v
.
\hfill\square

Corollary 5.1.8
.

Consider a map
\iota:\Spf\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]\rightarrow\Spf\mathbb{F}[\![x_{i},y_{i}]\!]
that lies in the non-ordinary stratum. Let
\alpha
be the degree of the series
\iota^{*}\overline{D}_{1}(v)\in\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]
. Then a formal special endomorphism
v
deforms to
\Spf(\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]/t^{\alpha})
but not
\Spf(\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]/t^{\alpha+1})
.

Proof.

Let
I=(t^{s})
be an ideal of
\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]
. It suffices to show that
v
lifts to
\Spf(\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]/I)
if and only if
\Spf(\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]/I)
factors through
\Spf(\mathbb{F}[\![x_{i},y_{i}]\!]/\overline{D}_{1}(v))
.

Let
n\geq 2
. Combining Lemma
5.1.6
(a) and (b), and note that
i_{2n-2}\geq 2n-3
, we have:

(29)

D_{n}(v)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{2n-2}\\
i_{1}\text{ even}\end{subarray}}\delta_{i_{2}<i_{3}<...<i_{2n-3}}Q_{i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{2n-2}}[D_{1}(v)]^{p^{1+i_{2n-2}}}+O(p^{2n-2}).

Lift
\iota
to a map
\Spf W[\![t]\!]\rightarrow\Spf W[\![x_{i},y_{i}]\!]
and still call it
\iota
. If
\overline{D}_{1}(v)\in I
, then
\iota^{*}D_{1}(v)\in(p,I)=(p,t^{s})
. Take
\iota^{*}
on both sides of (
29
), use the fact that
i_{2n-2}\geq 2n-3
, then plug in Lemma
5.1.6
(d). We find that all entries of
\iota^{*}\tilde{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}
lie in the PD envelope
\hat{D}_{I}
. This shows that
\iota^{*}\tilde{v}\in\mathbb{L}(W)\otimes\hat{D}_{I}
. Apply Lemma
5.1.7
to conclude that
v
lifts to
\Spf(\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]/I)
. Conversely, suppose that
\overline{D}_{1}(v)\notin I
. A similar argument shows that the pullback under
\iota
of the first entry of
\tilde{\mathbf{c}}
in Lemma
5.1.6
(d) already forces
\iota^{*}\tilde{v}
to lie outside of
\mathbb{L}(W)\otimes\hat{D}_{I}
. So
v
does not lift to
\Spf(\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]/I)
.
∎

Remark 5.1.9
.

The corollary shows, in particular, that we can take
x_{v}=\overline{D}_{1}(v)
, where
x_{v}
is the series in Remark
5.0.1
. This provides us with an explicit recipe of computing
x_{v}
.

5.2.
Setting up the local curve

Throughout, our curve
C
will be contained in the non-ordinary Newton strata. Therefore, the running assumption will be that
Q_{0}=0
. For the decay calculation, we will choose a uniformizer so that
C=\Spf\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]
. The equation of the local curve is give by

(30)

C:t\rightarrow(x_{1}(t),...,x_{m}(t);y_{1}(t),...,y_{m}(t)),\;\;x_{i}(t),y_{i}(t)\in\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!].

As in
[
MST22b
]
, we will also use
x_{i}(t),y_{i}(t)
to denote the power series in
W[\![t]\!]
where all the coefficients are Teichmuller lifts of the coefficients of
x_{i}(t),y_{i}(t)
in (
30
). This abuse of notation won’t cause confusion if one keeps track of which base ring they work in. To ease notation, we often just write
x_{i},y_{i}
for the corresponding power series in
t
.

Notation 5.2.1
.

We will assume that
Q_{0}(t),\ldots Q_{a-1}(t)=0
and that
Q_{a}(t)
is the first non-zero function of
t
. Let
h_{i}:=v_{t}(Q_{i}(t))
. Then
h_{a}
matches up with
h_{P}
as per §
3.2
. Note that we have
h_{i}=\infty
for
i<a
. Recursively define a sequence of integers
a_{1}<a_{2}<...
, with
a_{1}=a
, and
a_{i+1}
is the smallest integer bigger than
a_{i}
such that
h_{a_{i+1}}\leq h_{a_{i}}
. Let
a_{k}
be the biggest number in the sequence that is smaller than
m
(in fact, the sequence terminates at
a_{k}
). Throughout this section, the notation
a_{i}
will exclusively refer to the numbers listed above.

As the
a_{i}
’s play a pivotal rule in our analysis, we draw a picture for readers’ convenience. If we draw a bar chart for
h_{a},h_{a+1},\cdots
, then the
a_{i}
’s look like the following. The dashed lines will be “thrown away” in our analysis.

a_{1}
a_{2}
a_{3}
a_{4}

Figure 1
.
A picture for the
a_{i}
’s

5.2.2.
Setup with special endomorphisms

In this section, we always assume that
v\in\mathcal{L}
is a primitive formal special endomorphism, i.e.,
p^{-1}v\notin\mathcal{L}
. Write
\tilde{v}=F_{\infty}v
.

Definition 5.2.3
.

For every
n\geq 1
, let
d_{n}(v)
be the smallest integer such that the coefficients of
1=t^{0},...,t^{d_{n}(v)}
in the power series
p^{n-1}\tilde{v}
with respect to the basis
\{e^{\prime},f^{\prime};e_{i},f_{i}\}
do not all lie in
W
(if such an integer does not exist, we define
d_{n}(v)=\infty
). We call
d_{n}(v)
the
n
-th order rate of decay
for
v
.

The concrete meaning of
d_{n}(v)
is given in the following lemma. The quantity turns out to be equal to
v_{t}(x_{p^{n-1}v})
and
v_{t}(\overline{D}_{n}(v))
. However, as compared to
v_{t}(x_{p^{n-1}v})
, its description is more concrete; as compared to
v_{t}(\overline{D}_{n}(v))
, its definition is more conceptual. This makes it easier to use in many situations.

Lemma 5.2.4
.

(a)

p^{n-1}v
lifts to
\Spf\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]/(t^{d_{n}(v)})
but not
\Spf\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]/(t^{d_{n}(v)+1})
.

(b)

d_{n}(v)=v_{t}(x_{p^{n-1}v})=v_{t}(\overline{D}_{n}(v))
.

(c)

d_{n+1}(v)\geq\min_{i\geq a}\{h_{i}+p^{i+1}d_{n}(v)\}
.

Proof.

By definition, we have
D_{1}(p^{n-1}v)=D_{n}(v)
. So we can reduce part (a) and (b) to the case where
n=1
. For this, we can assume that
d_{1}(v)<\infty
. From the explicit formulae in Lemma
5.1.6
(c), the
t^{d_{1}(v)}
term in the first entry of
\tilde{\mathbf{c}}
has coefficient
p^{-1}u,u\in W^{*}
. While for any
d<d_{1}(v)
, the term
t^{d}
has integral coefficient. This implies that
v_{t}(\overline{D}_{1}(v))=d_{1}(v)
. Given this, part (a) follows from Corollary
5.1.8
, and part (b) follows from Remark
5.1.9
. Part (c) follows from the
r=1
case in Lemma
5.1.6
(b).
∎

Suppose that
n\geq 2
. It is helpful to get a feeling of what
\overline{D}_{n}(v)
should be, and how large
d_{n}(v)
can get. By recursion formula Lemma
5.1.6
(b) and Remark
5.1.9
,
\overline{D}_{n}(v)
is a huge linear combination of terms of the following form

(31)

w=Q_{i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{2r}}\,x_{v}^{(1+i_{2r})},\,\,\,\,\,\,r\geq n-1\text{ and }2|i_{1}

We can compute the
v_{t}
-valuation of a term in (
31
) as

(32)

v_{t}(w(t))=\sum_{k=1}^{r}p^{i_{2k-1}}h_{i_{2k}-i_{2k-1}}+p^{1+i_{2r}}v_{t}(x_{v}).

Let
H
be the minimal
v_{t}
-valuation among all the terms in (
31
). To estimate how large
d_{n}(v)
is, the first step is to look at all possible terms in (
31
) with
v_{t}
-valuation
H
.

Lemma 5.2.5
.

Every term
w
as in (
31
) that achieves
v_{t}(w)=H
satisfies the following conditions.

(a)

r=n-1
,
i_{1}=0
.

(b)

for
k\geq 1
,

i_{2k+1}-i_{2k}=1
,

(c)

for
k\geq 1
,
i_{2k}-i_{2k-1}\in\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{k}\}
.

Proof.

Consider a term
w=Q_{i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{2r}}\,x_{v}^{(1+i_{2r})}
in (
31
). If
r>n-1
, then by the explicit computation (
32
),
w^{\prime}=Q_{i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{2(r-1)}}\,x_{v}^{(1+i_{2(r-1)})}
has strictly smaller
v_{t}
-valuation than
w
. So
w
can not have
v_{t}
-valuation
H
. If
r=n-1
but
i_{1}>0
, then the term
w^{\prime\prime}=Q_{0<i_{2}-i_{1}<...<i_{2r-i_{1})}}\,x_{v}^{(1+i_{2r}-i_{1})}
has strictly smaller
v_{t}
-valuation than
w
. Again,
w
can not have
v_{t}
-valuation
H
. This proves (a). Part (b) can be proved similarly.

For part (c), we first show that
a\leq i_{2k}-i_{2k-1}
. Since we assumed that
Q_{i}(t)=0
for
i<a
, if
w
is such that
i_{2k}-i_{2k-1}<a
for some
k
, then
v_{t}
-valuation of
w
is
\infty
. To see that
i_{2k}-i_{2k-1}\in\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{k}\}
, let us assume that this is false for some
k
. Then from
Fig.

1
one easily sees that for some
a^{\prime}\in\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{k}\}
,
a^{\prime}<i_{2k}-i_{2k-1}
and
h_{a^{\prime}}\leq h_{i_{2k}-i_{2k-1}}
. Set
\delta:=(i_{2k}-i_{2k-1})-a^{\prime}>0
. Now we set a new sequence
i^{\prime}_{1}<i^{\prime}_{2}<\cdots<i^{\prime}_{2r}
by

i_{j}^{\prime}=\begin{cases}i_{j}&\text{ for }j\leq 2k-1\\
i_{j}-\delta&\text{ for }j\geq 2k.\end{cases}

Then one readily sees that the new
w^{\prime}
defined by
i^{\prime}_{1}<i^{\prime}_{2}<\cdots<i^{\prime}_{2r}
achieves smaller
v_{t}
.
∎

Remark 5.2.6
.

Clearly,
d_{n}(v)\geq H
. In generic cases, we even have
d_{n}(v)=H
. However, since
\overline{D}_{n}(v)
is a linear combination of the terms in (
31
), it can happen that multiple terms with minimal
v_{t}
-valuation cancel out, leaving us
d_{n}(v)>H
. This makes the estimation of
d_{n}(v)
much more subtle. Nevertheless, this essentially a complicated combinatorial problem which we solve in the next section.

5.3.
Precise definition of rapid decay

In this section, we study the terms in (
31
) that achieve the minimal
v_{t}
-valuation
H
; cf. Lemma
5.2.5
. In order to be able to work with the combinatorics in a flexible way, we introduce the following notions:

Definition 5.3.1
.

(i)

Let
\mathbb{I}_{r}
be the set of all words in
Q_{a},Q_{a+1},...,Q_{m-1}
of length
r
.

(ii)

Let
v\in\mathcal{L}
be a primitive formal special endomorphism.

(33)

\mathbb{I}_{r}(v)=\begin{cases}\{\textrm{words of the form }\mathcal{W}x_{v}:\mathcal{W}\in\mathbb{I}_{r-1}\}&\textrm{ if }v\bmod p\in\mathcal{L}_{1}/p\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\mathbb{I}_{r}&\textrm{ if }v\bmod p\in\mathcal{L}_{0}/p\end{cases}

(iii)

Given
\mathcal{W}\in\mathbb{I}_{r}
,
\mathcal{W}[r^{\prime}]
is the sub-word consisting of the last
r^{\prime}
letters of
\mathcal{W}
.
[r^{\prime}]\mathcal{W}
is the sub-word consisting of the first
r^{\prime}
letters of
\mathcal{W}
.
\mathcal{W}_{i}
is the
i
th letter (from the left) of
\mathcal{W}
.

(iv)

Define a function
\nu:\mathbb{I}_{r}\to\mathbb{N}
recursively by setting

(34)

\nu(Q_{i})=h_{i}\textrm{ and }\nu(Q_{i}\mathcal{W})=h_{i}+p^{i+1}\nu(\mathcal{W}).

For
\mathbb{I}_{r}(v)
defined in (ii), define
\nu:\mathbb{I}_{r}(v)\to\mathbb{N}
by adding the rule
\nu(x_{v})=v_{t}(x_{v})
.

(v)

Let
\mathbb{I}^{\min}_{r}
(resp.
\mathbb{I}^{\min}(v)
) be the set of all words in
\mathbb{I}_{r}
(resp.
\mathbb{I}_{r}(v)
in (ii)) having the minimal valuation. Write
\nu^{\min}_{r}
(resp.
\nu_{r}^{\min}(v)
) for this minimal valuation.

Lemma 5.3.2
.

There is a bijective correspondence between the terms in
\mathcal{I}^{\min}(v)
and the words in
\mathbb{I}^{\min}(v)
given by

(35)

w=Q_{i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{2r}}\,x_{v}^{(1+i_{2r})}\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{W}=Q_{u_{1}}Q_{u_{2}}...Q_{u_{n-1}}x_{v}\textrm{ where }u_{i}=i_{2k}-i_{2k-1}.

Proof.

One can recover
w\in\mathcal{I}^{\min}(v)
from
\mathcal{W}
by setting

(36)

i_{1}=0,i_{2}=u_{1},i_{2k-1}=\big(\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}u_{j}\big)+(k-1),i_{2k}=\big(\sum_{j=1}^{k}u_{j}\big)+(k-1)\text{ for }k\geq 2.

by
Lemma

5.2.5
.
∎

Notation 5.3.3
.

For every
\mathcal{W}\in\mathbb{I}_{r}
or
\mathbb{I}_{r}(v)
, we define
\mathcal{W}(t):=w(t)\in K[\![t]\!]
for
w
defined by (
36
). Then
\nu(\mathcal{W})=v_{t}(\mathcal{W}(t))
. The
H
in
Lemma

5.2.5
is simply
\nu^{\min}(v)
. For every
\mathcal{W}\in\mathbb{I}_{r}
or
\mathbb{I}_{r+1}(v)
and
j\leq r
, we write
a(\mathcal{W}_{j})
for the index such that
\mathcal{W}_{j}=Q_{a(\mathcal{W}_{j})}
.

Proposition 5.3.4
.

For any primitive formal special endomorphism
v
, we have
d_{r}(v)\geq\nu_{r}^{\min}(v)
.

Proof.

For
v\bmod p\in\mathcal{L}_{1}/p
, this is just a rephrase of the first sentence in Remark
5.2.6
. For
v\bmod p\in\mathcal{L}_{0}/p
, we additionally need to check that
d_{1}(v)\geq h_{a_{k}}
. Recall that
h_{a_{k}}=\min\{v_{t}(Q_{j}):j\in\mathbb{N}\}
. The conclusion follows by inspecting the formula for
D_{1}(v)
in
5.1.5
when only the first two entries of
\mathbf{c}
are possibly nonzero.
∎

The argument above also explains why in the above definition we treated the cases
v\bmod p
lie in
\mathcal{L}_{0}/p
or
\mathcal{L}_{1}/p
separately.

Definition 5.3.5
.

A primitive special endomorphism
v\in\mathcal{L}
is said to
decay rapidly to
r
th order
provided that

\begin{cases}d_{r}(v)=\nu_{r}^{\min}(v)\text{ and }v_{t}(x_{v})<h_{a_{k}}&\text{ when }v\bmod p\in\mathcal{L}_{1}/p\\
d_{r}(v)=\nu_{r}^{\min}(\text{in particular, }v_{t}(x_{v})=h_{a_{k}}.)&\text{ when }v\bmod p\in\mathcal{L}_{0}/p\end{cases}

We say that a saturated submodule
\Lambda\subseteq\mathcal{L}

decays rapidly to
r
th order
if every primitive special endomorphism
v\in\Lambda
decays rapidly to
r
th order.

The lemma below is a basic reality check for the definition above.

Lemma 5.3.6
.

If a primitive formal endomorphism
v\in\mathcal{L}
decays rapidly to
r
th order, then it decays rapidly to
s
th order for any
s\leq r
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that if
v
does not decay rapidly to
r
th order, then it does not decay rapidly to
(r+1)
th order. Suppose that
v\bmod p\in\mathcal{L}_{0}/p
and
v
does not decay rapidly to
r
th order, so
d_{r}(v)>{\nu_{r}^{\min}}
. Then by Lemma
5.2.4
, there exists
i
such that

d_{r+1}(v)\geq h_{i}+p^{i+1}d_{r}(v)>h_{i}+p^{i+1}\nu_{r}^{\min}\geq{\nu_{r+1}^{\min}}.

So
v
does not decay rapidly to
(r+1)
th order. The proof for
v\bmod p\in\mathcal{L}_{1}/p
is similar.
∎

Remark 5.3.7
.

Suppose that
a_{k}=a
. In this setting,
\mathbb{I}_{r}^{\min}(v)
is a singleton, and therefore
d_{r}(v)=\nu_{r}^{\min}(v)
. It follows that in this special case, every vector
v
that satisfies
v\bmod p\in\mathcal{L}_{1}/p
that decays rapidly to first order must decay rapidly to every order.

Recall that we have used the symbol “
v^{\prime}
” for an element in the basis
\{v^{\prime},w^{\prime};e_{i},f_{i}\}
of
\mathbb{L}(W)
. As we won’t use this basis again, in the rest of this section, we will abuse the symbol “
v^{\prime}
” to mean a special endomorphism in
\mathcal{L}
.

Lemma 5.3.8
.

Let
v,v^{\prime}
be primitive formal special endomorphisms in
\mathcal{L}
. Let
\mathcal{W}\in\mathbb{I}^{\min}_{r+1}(v)
and
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{r+1}^{\min}(v^{\prime})
.

(a)

If
v_{t}(x_{v})>v_{t}(x_{v^{\prime}})
, then
\nu(\mathcal{W})>\nu(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})
.

(b)

If both
v
and
v^{\prime}
decay rapidly to first order, then for every
i<j\leq r
,
a(\mathcal{W}_{i})\leq a(\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{j})
.

(c)

If both
v
and
v^{\prime}
decay rapidly to first order and
v_{t}(x_{v})>v_{t}(x_{v^{\prime}})
, then for every
i\leq r
,
a(\mathcal{W}_{i})\leq a(\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{i})
.

Proof.

Part
(a)
is clear. The proofs for part
(b)
and
(c)
are entirely similar so we only explain the former. As
\mathcal{W}[r]\in\mathbb{I}^{\min}_{r}(v)
,
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}[r]\in\mathbb{I}^{\min}_{r}(v^{\prime})
and we allow
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
, by induction we may always assume that
i=1
and
j=2
. The base case
r=2
follows from the assumption that
\nu(x_{v^{\prime}})\leq h_{a_{k}}\leq h_{a(\mathcal{W}_{2})}
. Define
\alpha=a(\mathcal{W}_{1})
,
\alpha^{\prime}=a(\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{1})
,
\nu=\nu(\mathcal{W}[r])
and
\nu^{\prime}=\nu(\mathcal{W}^{\prime}[r-1])
. Note that by induction we have
\nu^{\prime}\leq\nu
. By the minimality of
\mathcal{W}=Q_{\alpha}\cdot(\mathcal{W}[r])
and
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}[r]=Q_{\alpha^{\prime}}\cdot(\mathcal{W}^{\prime}[r-1])
, if we swap
Q_{\alpha}
with
Q_{\alpha^{\prime}}
, the
\nu
-value may only increase. This leads to inequalities:

\displaystyle h_{\alpha^{\prime}}+p^{\alpha^{\prime}}\nu^{\prime}\leq h_{\alpha}+p^{\alpha}\nu^{\prime}\leq h_{\alpha}+p^{\alpha}\nu\leq h_{\alpha^{\prime}}+p^{\alpha^{\prime}}\nu

Therefore,
p^{\alpha^{\prime}}(\nu-\nu^{\prime})\geq p^{\alpha}(\nu-\nu^{\prime})
. As
\nu-\nu^{\prime}\geq 0
, we obtain
\alpha^{\prime}\geq\alpha
as desired.
∎

We have the following result.

Proposition 5.3.9
.

Let
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}
be two primitive special endomorphisms.

(a)

If
v
decays rapidly to
r
th order and
v\equiv v^{\prime}\bmod p
, then
d_{r}(v)=d_{r}(v^{\prime})
. In particular,
v_{t}(x_{v})=v_{t}(x_{v^{\prime}})
and
v^{\prime}
also decays rapidly to
r
th order.

(b)

If both
v
and
v^{\prime}
decays rapidly to
r
th order, and
v_{t}(x_{v})\neq v_{t}(x_{v^{\prime}})
, then so does any primitive
v^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{L}
that is a linear combination of
v,v^{\prime}
.

Proof.

(a)
Suppose that
v=v^{\prime}+pu
for
u\in\mathcal{L}
. It suffices to show that the coefficients of
t^{0},...,t^{\nu_{r}^{\min}}
in the power series
p^{r}\tilde{u}
all lie in
W
. Let
pu=p^{\gamma}u^{\prime}
, where
\gamma\geq 1
and
u^{\prime}
is primitive. It suffices to show that
\nu_{r}^{\min}<w_{r+\gamma}^{\min}(u^{\prime})
. Let
\mathcal{W}\in\mathbb{I}_{r+\gamma}^{\min}(u^{\prime})
, then
[r]\mathcal{W}\in\mathbb{I}_{r}
. We have

\nu_{r}^{\min}\leq\nu([r]\mathcal{W})<\nu(\mathcal{W})=\nu_{r+\gamma}^{\min}(u^{\prime})

as desired. In particular, when
r=1
, we have
v_{t}(x_{v})=v_{t}(x_{v^{\prime}})
. The statement that
v^{\prime}
also decays rapidly to
r
th order is clear from definition.

(b)
Let
v^{\prime\prime}=\alpha v+\beta v^{\prime}
be a primitive vector. Then at least one of
\alpha,\beta
lies in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{*}
. If one of
\alpha
and
\beta
lies in
p\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, we reduce to
(a)
. So we can assume that
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{*}
. Without loss of generality, assume that
v_{t}(x_{v})=d_{1}(v)<v_{t}(x_{v^{\prime}})=d_{1}(v^{\prime})
. By
Definition

5.3.5
, this can happen only when
v\bmod p\in\mathcal{L}_{1}/p
. No matter what
v^{\prime}
is, we have
d_{r}(v)=\nu^{\min}_{r}(v)
and
d_{r}(v^{\prime})=\nu^{\min}_{r}(v^{\prime})
by definition. It follows from
Lemma

5.3.8
that

d_{r}(v)=\nu^{\min}_{r}(v)<\nu^{\min}_{r}(v^{\prime})=d_{r}(v^{\prime}).

So the coefficients of
t^{0},...,t^{d_{r}(v)}
in the power series
p^{r-1}\widetilde{v}
do not all lie in
W
, while the coefficients of
t^{0},...,t^{d_{r}(v)}
in the power series
p^{r-1}\widetilde{v^{\prime}}
all lie in
W
. It follows that the coefficients of
t^{0},...,t^{d_{r}(v)}
in the power series
p^{r-1}\widetilde{v^{\prime\prime}}
do not all lie in
W
. Therefore,
d_{r}(v^{\prime\prime})=d_{r}(v)
. So the claim holds.
∎

Part
(b)
of the above has the following consequence.

Corollary 5.3.10
.

If
v_{0}\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
and
v_{1}\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
are primitive vectors that decay rapidly to
r
th order, then so does every linear combination of
v_{0}
and
v_{1}
that is a primitive vector in
\mathcal{L}
.

Definition 5.3.11
.

Let
v
be a primitive formal special endomorphism that decays rapidly up to first order. We define
I_{r}(v)\subseteq\mathbb{N}
to be the interval

I_{r}(v)=\Big[\min_{\mathcal{W}\in\mathbb{I}_{r}^{\min}(v)}a(\mathcal{W}_{1}),\max_{\mathcal{W}\in\mathbb{I}_{r}^{\min}(v)}a(\mathcal{W}_{1})\Big],

and write
|I_{r}(v)|
for the length of this interval.

Note that for any
r\geq s
,

I_{s}(v)=\Big[\min_{\mathcal{W}\in\mathbb{I}_{r}^{\min}(v)}a(\mathcal{W}_{r-s+1}),\max_{\mathcal{W}\in\mathbb{I}_{r}^{\min}(v)}a(\mathcal{W}_{r-s+1})\Big].

We have the following results about intervals.

Proposition 5.3.12
.

Let
v
and
v^{\prime}
be primitive formal endomorphisms decay rapidly up to first order.

(a)

If
v_{t}(x_{v})\neq v_{t}(x_{v^{\prime}})
,
I_{r}(v)\cap I_{s}(v^{\prime})
is either empty or a singleton for any
r,s\in\mathbb{N}
.

(b)

If
v_{t}(x_{v})=v_{t}(x_{v^{\prime}})
,
I_{r}(v)\cap I_{s}(v^{\prime})
is either empty or a singleton for any distinct
r\neq s\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

For
(a)
, suppose that
v_{t}(x_{v})>v_{t}(x_{v^{\prime}})
. Let
\mathcal{W}\in\mathbb{I}_{r}^{\min}(v)
,
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{s}^{\min}(v^{\prime})
. Suppose that
r=s
. Then, by
Lemma

5.3.8
(c)
, we have that
a(\mathcal{W}_{1})\leq a(\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{1})
. That is, every integer in
I_{r}(v)
is bounded above by every integer in
I_{r}(v^{\prime})
. Hence the intervals are either disjoint, or intersect only at a singleton. For
(b)
identical argument goes through, except that one replaces
(c)
of
Lemma

5.3.8
by
(b)
.
∎

5.4.
Isotropic subspaces

We have the following result.

Lemma 5.4.1
.

The number of distinct
i
such that
v_{t}(x_{i})\geq h_{a_{k}}
is at most
m-1-a_{k}
.

Proof.

The key observation is that we can find a suitable sublattice
L^{\prime}\subseteq L
defining an orthogonal Shimura subvariety
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{S}
that is cut out by the ideal
(x_{a_{k}+1}\dots x_{m},\,y_{a_{k}+1}\dots y_{m})
. Then, by applying
Proposition

4.2.3
to
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
, we deduce that
Q_{a_{k}}\in(Q_{0},Q_{1},\ldots Q_{a_{k}-1},x_{a_{k}+1},\cdots x_{m})
. It follows that at least one of the numbers
v_{t}(x_{a_{k}+1})
,…,
v_{t}(x_{m})
must be smaller or equal to
h_{a_{k}}
. We may reshuffle indicies to deduce that the number of distinct
i
such that
v_{t}(x_{i})>h_{a_{k}}
is at most
m-1-a_{k}
.

We have therefore proved that the number of distinct
i
such that
v_{t}(x_{i})>h_{a_{k}}
is at most
m-1-a_{k}
. In order to prove the claim with
>
replaced with
\geq
, write
Q_{a_{k}}=\sum f_{i}Q_{i}+\sum g_{i}x_{i}
in the ring
\mathbb{F}[\![x_{1}\ldots x_{m},y_{1}\ldots y_{m}]\!]
. It suffices to prove that elements
g_{i}
have non-zero constant term. But this is true by just comparing coefficients of each monomial on the LHS and RHS.
∎

Lemma
5.4.1
yields the following corollary.

Corollary 5.4.2
.

(a)

Let
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{L}
be a saturated sub-lattice having rank
m-a_{k}
such that
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}/p\mathcal{L}\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{1}/p\mathcal{L}
, and such that
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}/p\mathcal{L}
is isotropic. Then there is at least one primitive vector
v
that decays rapidly to first order, i.e.,
v_{t}(x_{v})<h_{a_{k}}
.

(b)

There exists a saturated sublattice
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{L}_{1}
having rank
2a_{k}
such that every primitive vector decays rapidly to first order.

Proof.

(a) As observed earlier, we may replace
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
by a sublattice that has the same mod
p
reduction. Therefore, we will assume throughout that
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{L}_{1}
and that
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
is already isotropic. Recall that in §
4.2.1
, the basis
\{e_{i},f_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq m}
on
\mathbb{L}_{1}
can be chosen to be any basis for which the Gram matrix is
\begin{bmatrix}&I_{m}\\
I_{m}&\end{bmatrix}
. Therefore, by Witt cancellation, we are allowed to assume that the basis was chosen such that
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
is the span of
e_{1}\ldots e_{m-a_{k}}
. Recall that
x_{i}=x_{e_{i}}
. Then we conclude by
Lemma

5.4.1
.

(b) Let
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{1}
be the saturated lattice spanned by all primitive vectors which resist decay up to first order and let
N:=\dim\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}_{1}
.
By (a),
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
cannot contain an isotropic subspace of dimension
\geq m-a_{k}
. By the classification of quadratic forms over a field in odd characteristic,
N\leq 2(m-a_{k})
. Therefore, we may take
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{\prime}
to be any saturated sublattice of
\mathcal{L}_{1}
that is complementary to
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
. ∎

5.5.
Proof of the decay Lemma

We are now ready to state and prove the decay Lemma.

Theorem 5.5.1
.

There is a saturated sublattice
\Lambda\subset\mathcal{L}
having rank
\geq a_{1}+a_{k}+2
that decays rapidly.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1}\subset\mathcal{L}_{1}
be the saturated sub-lattice produced in part (b) of Corollary
5.4.2
. We will show the existence of a saturated rank-
(a_{1}+a_{k}+2)
sublattice
\Lambda=\Lambda_{0}\oplus\Lambda_{1}\subset\mathcal{L}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1}=:\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
that decays rapidly. We first define a filtration on
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}/p\mathcal{L}
. For
i<h_{a_{k}}
, let

\overline{\Fil^{i}}=\{\bar{v}\in(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}/p\mathcal{L})\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}:v_{t}(x_{v})\geq i\}\cup\{\mathbf{0}\}.

Define
\overline{\Fil^{h_{a_{k}}}}=\mathcal{L}_{0}
, and define higher filtered pieces to be just the zero vector. Intersecting this filtration with
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1}/p\mathcal{L}
, we get an exhaustive filtration with the
h_{a_{k}}
piece equal to zero by the hypothesis that every primitive vector in
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1}
decays to first order. We pick a splitting of this filtration and lift it to
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
such that the splitting respects the direct sum decomposition
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=\mathcal{L}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1}
. Therefore, we have written
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=\oplus_{0<i\leq h_{a_{k}}}\mathcal{L}^{i}
where for
i<h_{a_{k}}
, every primitive vector
v\in\mathcal{L}^{i}
satisfies
v_{t}(x_{v})=i
, and
\mathcal{L}^{h_{a_{k}}}=\mathcal{L}_{0}
.

Fix some
\mathcal{L}^{i}
, some integer
r
, and consider
T_{r}(i)=\sum_{1<s\leq r}|I_{s}(v_{i})|
, where
v_{i}
is any primitive vector in
\mathcal{L}^{i}
(by definition the intervals do not depend on the choice of
v_{i}
). By Proposition
5.3.12
, we have that

T_{r}(1)+T_{r}(2)+\ldots+T_{r}(h_{a_{k}})\leq a_{k}-a_{1}.

It follows that

\sum_{i=1}^{h_{a_{k}}}\max\{0,\dim\mathcal{L}^{i}-T_{r}(i)\}\geq 2a_{k}+2-a_{k}+a_{1}=a_{k}+a_{1}+2.

Together with Proposition
5.3.9
, the two claims below implies the existence of a saturated rank-
a_{k}+a_{1}+2
sublattice that decays rapidly to
r
th order.

Claim 5.5.2
.

Let
i<h_{a_{k}}
, and suppose that
\mathcal{L}^{i}
has rank
d_{i}
with
T_{r}(i)<d_{i}
. Then, there is a rank
d_{i}-T_{r}(i)
sublattice of
\mathcal{L}^{i}
that decays rapidly to
r
th order.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the claim with
d_{i}-T_{r}(i)=1
– indeed, proving that any saturated rank-
T_{r}(i)+1
subspace contains a primitive vector that decays rapidly up to
r
th order would imply that any saturated rank
T_{r}(i)+2
subspace must contain a saturated rank-2 subspace that decays rapidly up to
r
-th order, etc.

We may assume that
\mathcal{L}^{i}
is spanned by
v_{1},...,v_{d_{i}}
. Let
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
be the coefficients of the leading order terms of
x_{v_{j}}(t)
for
j=1\ldots d_{i}
. We now consider the power-series expansion for the vector
\tilde{v_{j}}
- specifically, the first two entries of this vector. Consider the sum of the
\mathcal{W}(t)
where
\mathcal{W}
ranges over all words in
\mathbb{I}_{r}^{\min}(v_{j})
. It suffices to prove that this sum has
t
-adic valuation equal to
\nu_{r}^{\min}(v_{j})
for some
1\leq j\leq d_{i}
. Note that as
v_{t}(x_{v_{j}})
is the same for all
j
,
\mathbb{I}_{r}^{\min}(v_{j})
does not depend on
j
. Let
\mathcal{W}\in\mathbb{I}_{r}^{\min}(v_{j})
, and let
M_{\mathcal{W}}
be the number such that the function
\mathcal{W}(t)
equals
([r-1]\mathcal{W}(t))(x_{v_{j}})^{(M_{\mathcal{W}})}(t)
. In other words, under the equivalence of
Lemma

5.3.2
,
M_{\mathcal{W}}=1+i_{2r}=r+\sum_{j=1}^{r}a(\mathcal{W}_{j})
. There is a unique word
\mathcal{W}_{\min}\in\mathbb{I}_{r}^{\min}(v_{j})
(resp.
\mathcal{W}_{\max}\in\mathbb{I}_{r}^{\min}(v_{j})
) which minimizes (resp. maximizes) the integer
M_{\mathcal{W}}
. Indeed, they are constructed by minimizing or maximizing each of
a(\mathcal{W}_{s})
, i.e.,

\mathbb{I}_{r-s+1}=\Big[\min_{\mathcal{W}\in\mathbb{I}_{r}^{\min}(v)}a(\mathcal{W}_{s}),\max_{\mathcal{W}\in\mathbb{I}_{r}^{\min}(v)}a(\mathcal{W}_{s})\Big]=\big[a(\mathcal{W}^{\min}_{s}),a(\mathcal{W}^{\max}_{s})\big]

for each
1<s\leq r
. Note therefore that
T_{r}(i)=M_{\mathcal{W}^{\max}}-M_{\mathcal{W}^{\min}}
.

Now, for every
M
in the range
M_{\mathcal{W}^{\min}}\leq M\leq M_{\mathcal{W}^{\max}}
consider all words with the property
M_{\mathcal{W}}=M
. The sum of these words equals
(\sum[r-1]\mathcal{W})(t)(x_{v_{j}})^{(M)}(t)
. We write the sum
(\sum[r-1]\mathcal{W})(t)
as
\beta_{M}t^{N_{M}}+t^{N_{M}+1}(...)
, where
\beta_{M}
might equal zero. Note that
N_{M}
is just the valuation of
[r-1]\mathcal{W}
. We see that the vector
v_{j}
decays rapidly to
r
th order if and only if
\sum_{M=M_{\mathcal{W}^{\min}}}^{M_{\mathcal{W}}^{\max}}\beta_{M}\alpha_{j}^{(M)}\neq 0
.

We now use the fact that the rank of
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
(i.e.
d_{i}
) is one greater than
T_{r}(i)=M_{\mathcal{W}^{\max}}-M_{\mathcal{W}^{\min}}
. Suppose that every
v_{j}
fails to decays rapidly to
r
th order. Then, it follows that
\sum_{M=M_{\mathcal{W}^{\min}}}^{M_{\mathcal{W}}^{\max}}\beta_{M}\alpha_{j}^{(M)}=0
for every
j
. Now, consider the matrix whose
jth
row is
\alpha_{j}^{(M_{\mathcal{W}^{\min}})},\alpha_{j}^{(M_{\mathcal{W}^{\min}}+1)},\ldots\alpha_{j}^{(M_{\mathcal{W}^{\max}})}
. The matrix has
d_{i}
rows and
M_{\mathcal{W}^{\max}}-M_{\mathcal{W}^{\min}}+1=T_{r}(i)+1=d_{i}
columns. This is (the Frobenius twist of) the so-called Moore matrix, whose determinant is non-zero precisely when the
\alpha_{j}
are
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-linearly independent. As every vector in
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
decays rapidly up to first order, we have that the
\alpha_{i}
are
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-linearly independent! Therefore, the determinant of the matrix is non-zero, and hence the only way we have
\sum_{M=M_{\mathcal{W}^{\min}}}^{M_{\mathcal{W}}^{\max}}\beta_{M}\alpha_{j}^{(M)}=0
is for every
\beta_{M}=0
. But
\beta_{\mathcal{W}^{\min}}\neq 0,\beta_{\mathcal{W}^{\max}}\neq 0
! We have therefore proved that for every integer
r
, there has to be at least one vector that decays rapidly to
r
th order. As we have already reduced the claim to the case
T_{r}(i)+1=d_{i}
, the proof is finished. ∎

Claim 5.5.3
.

Suppose that
T_{r}(h_{a_{k}})<\textrm{rank}\mathcal{L}_{0}
. Then, there is a rank
(\textrm{rank}\mathcal{L}_{0}-T_{r}(h_{a_{k}}))
sublattice of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
that decays rapidly.

Proof.

The setting of this claim is somewhat degenerate, as
\mathcal{L}_{0}
is only a rank 2 lattice. There are only two cases –
T_{r}(h_{a_{k}})
is 0 or 1. The case of
T_{r}(h_{a_{k}})=0
is easy – indeed, in the sum-expansion of the matrix
X_{r}(t)
in Lemma
5.1.3
has a unique term with minimal
t
-adic valuation and the claim holds. In the case where
T_{r}(h_{a_{k}})=1
, there are exactly two terms in the sum-expansion of
X_{r}
with minimal
t
-adic valuation. The sum of these two matrices (without counting powers of
p
) will be of the form
\mathcal{W}_{1}(t)A+\mathcal{W}_{2}(t)B
, where the first row of
A
is
[1\phantom{a}\lambda]
and of
B
is
[1\phantom{a}-\lambda]
. Either
\mathcal{W}_{1}(t)+\mathcal{W}_{2}(t)
or
\mathcal{W}_{1}(t)-\mathcal{W}_{2}(t)
has the form
\alpha t^{\nu_{r}^{\min}}+O(t^{\nu_{r}^{\min}+1})
where
\alpha
is a
p
-adic unit, in which case either
e+f
or
e-f
decays rapidly to
r
th order. The claim follows.
∎

By
Proposition

5.3.4
and
Definition

5.3.5
, we have proved that there exists a saturated sublattice
\Lambda_{r}\subset\mathcal{L}
of rank at least
a_{k}+a_{1}+2
that decays rapidly to
r
th order for any given
r
. As whether a saturated lattice
\Lambda_{r}
decays rapidly or not (to any fixed order) depends only on its mod
p
reduction, and as there are only finitely many sublattices of any rank of a finite-dimensional
\mathbb{F}_{p}
vector space, we may pass to a subsequence
r_{j}
of the set of all positive integers such that
\Lambda_{r_{j}}/p\mathcal{L}\subseteq\mathcal{L}/p\mathcal{L}
doesn’t depend on
r_{j}
. Then by
Lemma

5.3.6
and
Proposition

5.3.9
(a), the sublattice
\Lambda_{r_{j}}
for any value of
r_{j}
decays rapidly and the theorem is proved.
∎

6.
First order decay and Eisentein series: superspecial points

In this section we prove the following main result on the Eisenstein series, which is a major middle step towards Theorem
3.2.3
:

Theorem 6.0.1
.

Let
C
be a curve as in §
5.2
, and let
P
be a superspecial point. If
\mathcal{A}_{C^{/P}}
does not admit a saturated lattice of formal special endomorphisms of rank
2(m-a)
, then there exists a constant
\alpha<1
, depending only on
C
and
P
, such that for
M\in\mathbb{N}
coprime to
p
, we have

S^{\prime}(M)=\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{{q_{L_{r,P}^{\prime}}(M)}}{g_{P}(M)}<\alpha.

The theorem will be proved at the end of §
6.7
. There are two main inputs: the first input is the decay lemma established in §
5
. The second is a thorough understanding of the first order decay, which is the main contributor to the local Eisenstein series. The study of the first order decay is carried out in §
6.2
\sim
§
6.4
, culminating in Proposition
6.4.7
and
6.4.8
, which state that a decaying sequence of lattices must follow a strict pattern.

Remark 6.0.2
.

In this section,
m
will be as in Sections
4
and
5
. The symbol
M
will play the role that
m
plays in Section
3
.

6.1.
An example

Let’s assume
m=2
. We will use the setup and notation in §
5.2
. Then the only case we care is that
C
lies generically on the almost supersingular stratum. Suppose that the local coordinates for
C^{/P}
is given by series
x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2}\in\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]
. Then

(37)

\displaystyle Q_{0}=x_{1}y_{1}+x_{2}y_{2}=0,

(38)

\displaystyle Q_{1}=x_{1}^{p}y_{1}+x_{1}y_{1}^{p}+x_{2}^{p}y_{2}+x_{2}y_{2}^{p}\neq 0.

In particular, we must have
a=a_{k}=1
in this case. Let
c_{i}=v_{t}(x_{i})
and
d_{i}=v_{t}(y_{i})
. Note that (
37
) implies that
c_{1}+d_{1}=c_{2}+d_{2}
. We also suppose that
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}
are the leading coefficients of the
x_{i},y_{i}
respectively. Note that replacing the vectors
e_{i},f_{i}\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
by an
\textrm{SO}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
coordinates where each matrix entry is the teichmüller-lift of its mod
p
reduction induces changing the variables
(x_{i},y_{i})
by an element of
\textrm{SO}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
. Note that doing this leaves the equations of the height strata unchanged. We shall refer to transformations of this form as orthogonal change of coordinates. Clearly, there are only finitely orthogonal change of coordinates. Note that swapping
x_{i}
with
y_{i}
is such change of coordinates.

Lemma 6.1.1
.

Consider the set
\Sigma=\{\alpha_{1}t^{c_{1}},\alpha_{2}t^{c_{2}},\beta_{1}t^{d_{1}},\beta_{2}t^{d_{2}}\}
. After an orthogonal change of coordinates, we may always assume that
c_{1}\leq c_{2}\leq d_{2}\leq d_{1}
. Further, in addition to this holding, we belong to one of the following cases:

(1)

The set
\Sigma
is linearly independent over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.

(2)

There is exactly one
\mathbb{F}_{p}
linear relation in
\Sigma
, and which is of the form
\frac{\alpha_{2}t^{c_{2}}}{\beta_{2}t^{d_{2}}}=\gamma_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{*}
.

(3)

There are exactly two
\mathbb{F}_{p}
linear relations in
\Sigma
, and these have the form
\frac{\alpha_{i}t^{c_{i}}}{\beta_{i}t^{d_{i}}}=\gamma_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{*}
. Further, the quadratic form
\gamma_{1}X^{2}+\gamma_{2}Y^{2}
is anisotropic over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.

The proof consists of a fairly straightforward process of iteratively applying orthogonal change of coordinates. We prove (with full detail) a far more general statement in a far more general setting later in this section (Lemmas
6.2.13
and
6.2.14
).

We will explain how to deduce Theorem
6.0.1
for
m=2
. This amounts to upper-bounding the series
S^{\prime}(M)
. Following Remark
3.2.4
, we can explicate each term of
S^{\prime}(M)
as

\frac{q_{L_{P,r}^{\prime}}(M)}{g_{P}(M)}=\frac{p^{2}-1}{h_{P}}\frac{1}{p(1+p^{-3})}\frac{\delta(p,L^{\prime}_{P,r},M)}{[L^{\prime}_{P,1}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}:L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}]}.

Note that in the setting of Lemma
6.1.1
, we have that

(39)

h_{P}=v_{t}(Q_{1})\geq pc_{1}+d_{1},

so the term
\frac{p^{2}-1}{h_{P}}
can be bonded from above accordingly. Therefore, in theory we can bound
S^{\prime}(M)
by bounding the local density
\delta(p,L^{\prime}_{P,r},M)
and covolume
{[L^{\prime}_{P,1}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}:L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}]}
. However, in practice, the shape of
L^{\prime}_{P,r}
is somewhat evasive. Instead of directly bounding the local density and covolume for
L^{\prime}_{P,r}
, we will construct a sequence of auxiliary lattices
L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}
which we can describe exactly. For each lattice
L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}
and each integer
M
not a multiple of
p
, we can also precisely compute
\delta(p,L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r},M)
and
[L^{\prime\prime}_{P,1}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}:L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}]
. This allows us to explicitly upper-bound the auxiliary series

S^{\prime\prime}(M):=\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{q_{L_{P,r}^{\prime\prime}}(M)}{g_{P}(M)}=\frac{p^{2}-1}{h_{P}}\frac{1}{p(1+p^{-3})}\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{\delta(p,L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r},M)}{[L^{\prime\prime}_{P,1}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}:L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}]}.

We will then show that
S^{\prime}(M)\leq S^{\prime\prime}(M)
. Upon computing
S^{\prime\prime}(M)
explicitly, we obtain that
S^{\prime}(M)<1
for all
M
coprime to
p

unless

\mathcal{A}_{C^{/P}}
admits a rank-2 saturated lattice of formal special endomorphisms. Since
\delta(p,L^{\prime}_{P,r},M)
depends only on
\left(\frac{M}{p}\right)
and
{[L^{\prime}_{P,1}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}:L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}]}
is independent of
M
, there exists a constant
\alpha<1
independent of
M
, such that
S^{\prime}(M)<\alpha
. This yields the theorem.

We will now carry out this procedure in case 3 of Lemma
6.1.1
(the hardest case). In other words, we will describe
L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}
in case 3 of Lemma
6.1.1
, and will explicitly compute
S^{\prime\prime}(M)
. Note that in case 3 we must have
c_{1}=c_{2}=d_{2}=d_{1}
, and we will use
d
to denote this number. In the following, we construct
L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}
. First, let
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{brd}}:=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\{e_{1}-\gamma_{1}f_{1},e_{2}-\gamma_{2}f_{2}\}
, where by abuse of notation
\gamma_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{*}
denotes the Teichmüller lift of
\gamma_{i}
in Lemma
6.1.1
(3). Second, for each
j\geq 1
, define
H_{j}:=h_{P}(1+p^{2}+...+p^{2(j-1)})
and
H_{0}:=0
. Finally, define
L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}
to be the
\mathbb{Z}
-lattice such that
L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{l}=L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{l}
for
l\neq p
, and

L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{brd}}+p^{j}\mathcal{L},\,\,\,\,&r\in(H_{j},H_{j}+p^{2j}d],\\
&\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{brd}}+p^{j}\mathcal{L}_{0}+p^{j+1}\mathcal{L}_{1},\,\,\,\,&r\in(H_{j}+p^{2j}d,H_{j+1}].\end{aligned}\right.

The lattice
L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}
can be thought of as
L^{\prime}_{P,r}
in the special case where all vectors in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{brd}}
never decay. For
M
coprime to
p
, one computes that

\delta(p,L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r},M)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &1-p^{-2},\,\,\,\,&r\in(0,a],\\
&1+p^{-1},\,\,\,\,&r\in(a,\infty).\end{aligned}\right.

[L^{\prime\prime}_{P,1}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}:L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}]=\left\{\begin{aligned} &p^{4j},\,\,\,\,&r\in(H_{j},H_{j}+p^{2j}d],\\
&p^{4j+2},\,\,\,\,&r\in(H_{j}+p^{2j}d,H_{j+1}].\end{aligned}\right.

It follows that

\displaystyle S^{\prime\prime}(M)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{p^{2}-1}{d(p+1)}\frac{1}{p(1+p^{-3})}\left[d(1-p^{-2})+(1+p^{-1})\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{dp^{2j}}{p^{4j}}+(1+p^{-1})\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(h_{P}-d)p^{2j}}{p^{4j+2}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{p^{2}-1}{d(p+1)}\frac{d}{p(1+p^{-3})}\left[(1-p^{-2})+\frac{(1+p)^{2}}{p(p^{2}-1)}\right]=1.

Each inequality in the above formula comes from (
39
).

Lemma 6.1.2
.

Notation as above. For all
M
coprime to
p
we have
S^{\prime}(M)\leq S^{\prime\prime}(M)
. If
\mathcal{A}_{C^{/P}}
does not admit a rank-2 saturated lattice of formal special endomorphisms, then
S^{\prime}(M)<S^{\prime\prime}(M)
.

Proof.

For
\Lambda\subseteq\mathcal{L}
a sublattice, we let
\overline{\Lambda}
denote its mod
p\mathcal{L}
reduction. We immediately see that for
d<r\leq h_{P}
, we have
\overline{\mathcal{L}_{0}}\subseteq\overline{L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{brd}}}\oplus\overline{\mathcal{L}_{0}}=\overline{L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
. For
d>h_{P}
, every primitive vector in the lattice
\mathcal{L}_{0}
decays. It follows that
\overline{L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
is a sublattice of
\overline{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{brd}}}\oplus\overline{\mathcal{L}_{0}}
whose projection to
\overline{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{brd}}}
is an embedding, which is moreover an isometrical embedding since
\overline{\mathcal{L}_{0}}
carries the trivial quadratic form. In the following, we show that for all
r\geq 1
,

(40)

\frac{\delta(p,L^{\prime}_{P,r},M)}{[L^{\prime}_{P,1}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}:L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}]}\leq\frac{\delta(p,L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r},M)}{[L^{\prime\prime}_{P,1}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}:L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}]}.

Since
L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}=L^{\prime}_{P,r}
when
r\leq d
, it suffices to consider the case where
r>d
. By the Decay Lemma
5.5.1
(also see Remark
5.3.7
), the lattice
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{rv}}:=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\{e^{\prime},f^{\prime},f_{1},f_{2}\}
decays rapidly to arbitrary order. So

(41)

[L^{\prime}_{P,1}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}:L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}]\geq[L^{\prime\prime}_{P,1}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}:L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}].

Suppose that (
41
) is a strict inequality, then (
40
) holds and is a strict inequality. Suppose that (
41
) is an equality, then
\overline{L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
and
\overline{L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
need to have the same dimension. If
d<r<h_{P}
, this means

\overline{L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}=\overline{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{brd}}}\oplus\overline{\mathcal{L}_{0}}
; if
r>h_{P}
, then the projection of
\overline{L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
to
\overline{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{brd}}}
is an isomorphism. It follows that
\delta(p,L^{\prime}_{P,r},M)=\delta(p,L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r},M)
. So (
40
) holds and is an equality. Therefore (
40
) holds for all
r\geq 1
, and
S^{\prime}(M)\leq S^{\prime\prime}(M)
.

To show
S^{\prime}(M)<S^{\prime\prime}(M)
under the extra condition, we proceed by contradiction. If
S^{\prime}(M)=S^{\prime\prime}(M)
, then (
40
) need to be an equality for all
r\geq 1
, hence (
41
) must be an equality for all
r\geq 1
. In the following let
r>h_{P}
. The projection of
\overline{L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
to
\overline{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{brd}}}
is an isomorphism. In particular,
\overline{L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
does not depend on
r
. Pick
\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, such that
E_{1}:=e_{1}-\gamma_{1}f_{1}+\alpha_{1}e^{\prime}+\beta_{1}f^{\prime}
and
E_{2}:=e_{2}-\gamma_{2}f_{2}+\alpha_{2}e^{\prime}+\beta_{2}f^{\prime}
reduce mod
p
to a basis of
\overline{L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
. Then for each
r
there exit
R_{r,1},R_{r,2}\in p\mathcal{L}
with the property that
E_{1}+R_{r,1},E_{2}+R_{r,2}\in{L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
. We can modify
R_{r,i}
by
E_{i}
as follows:
there exits
u_{r,i}\in p\mathbb{Z}_{p}
such that
R^{\prime}_{r,i}:=R_{r,i}-u_{r,i}E_{i}\in p\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{rv}}
. It follows that
E_{i}+(1+u_{r_{i}})^{-1}R^{\prime}_{r,i}\in{L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
. Therefore, we can assume at the beginning that
R_{r,i}\in p\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{rv}}
.
Since
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{rv}}
decays rapidly to arbitrary order, the sequence
\{E_{i}+R_{r,i}\}_{r=h_{P}+1}^{\infty}
converges to a vector that is congruent to
E_{i}
mod
p
and lies in every
L_{P,r}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. As a result,
\mathcal{A}_{C^{/P}}
admits a rank-2 saturated lattice of formal special endomorphisms.
∎

Since
\mathcal{A}_{C^{/P}}
does not admit a rank-2 saturated lattice of formal special endomorphisms, follows from the lemma that
S^{\prime}(M)<S^{\prime}(M)\leq 1
. We have seen that this implies Theorem
6.0.1
.

6.2.
Q
-degeneracy and line configurations

We introduce the notions of
Q
-degeneracy and line configuration that effectively captures the combinatorics of the Frobenius-twisted quadratic forms
\{Q_{i}(t)\}_{i\geq 0}
associated to a collection of power series
\{\underline{x}(t),\underline{y}(t)\}
that arise in the context of §
5.2
. This can be thought of as an analogue of the Newton polygon. For maximal flexibility, we will build our tools separately from the setup in §
5.2
.

The following notion will be extremely important through out the section:

Definition 6.2.1
.

Let
s_{1},s_{2},...,s_{n}\in\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]
. We say that
s_{1},s_{2},...,s_{n}
are
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-hyper-independent
if for any
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},...,\alpha_{n}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
, we have
v_{t}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}s_{i})=\min_{1\leq i\leq n}\{v_{t}(\alpha_{i}s_{i})\}
. Equivalently,
s_{1},s_{2},...,s_{n}
are
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-hyper-independent if for any
d\in\mathbb{N}
, the series in the subset
\{s_{i}:v_{t}(s_{i})=d\}
have
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-linear independent leading coefficients.

Example 6.2.2
.

If
s_{1},...,s_{n}
are
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-hyper-independent, then so is
s_{1},...,s_{n},0
.

6.2.3.
Basic definitions

Definition 6.2.4
.

Let
\{x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n},y_{1},y_{2},...,y_{n}\}
be a collection of power series in
\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]
. For
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, the
r
th Frobenius-twisted quadratic form
Q_{r}
is defined by:

Q_{r}(\underline{x},\underline{y})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}^{p^{r}}y_{i}+x_{i}y_{i}^{p^{r}}).

If
s
is a non-negative integer, we say that the collection
\{\underline{x},\underline{y}\}
is
Q_{s}
-degenerate
, if for all
0\leq r\leq s
, we have

(42)

v_{t}\left(Q_{r}(\underline{x},\underline{y})\right)>\min_{1\leq i\leq n}\{v_{t}(x_{i}^{p^{r}}y_{i}),\;v_{t}(x_{i}y_{i}^{p^{r}})\}.

If both sides of (
42
) are
\infty
, the inequality holds by default.

Definition 6.2.5
.

The set
\mathbf{E}
of
admissible lines
(in short,
lines
) is the set of symbols of the form “
y=ax+b
” where
a,b\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\cup\{\infty\}
. When
a,b\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we say the line is
geometric
: it is a genuine line in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. When
a
or
b
equals
\infty
, we say the line is
degenerate
. Let
n
be a non-negative integer. A
line configuration
of
n
lines is an element
\{\Pi_{1},...,\Pi_{n}\}\in\mathbf{E}^{n}
. The following are some notions related to line configurations:

(1)

Let
\{x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n},y_{1},y_{2},...,y_{n}\}
be a collection of power series in
\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]
. To each
1\leq i\leq n
, we attach a line
\Pi_{i}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
, with equation

\Pi_{i}:y=\min\{v_{t}(x_{i}),v_{t}(y_{i})\}x+\max\{v_{t}(x_{i}),v_{t}(y_{i})\}.

This gives rise to a line configuration
\{\Pi_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
as per Definition
6.2.5
, which will be called the
line configuration associated to
\{\underline{x},\underline{y}\}
. In this paper,
we will mainly be concerned with line configurations that arise this way.

(2)

When there is no need to keep track of indices, we will write a line configuration as a multiset:

(43)

\{\Pi_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}=\{m(\ell_{j})\cdot\ell_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq w},

where
m(\ell_{j})
is the multiplicity of the line
\ell_{j}
.

(3)

Given a line configuration
\{\Pi_{i}:y=a_{i}x+b_{i}\}_{{1\leq i\leq n}}
, let
\Omega_{i}=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}|y\leq a_{i}x+b_{i}\}
(if the line is degenerate, then
\Omega_{i}
is defined to be
\mathbb{R}^{2}
). The region

\Omega:=\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\Omega_{i}

will be called the
Q
-polygon
attached to the line configuration. The
boundary

\partial\Omega
makes sense (for example, if all lines are degenerate, then
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\partial\Omega=\varnothing
).

(4)

A point on
\partial\Omega
with
x
-coordinates
p^{r},r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
will be called a
critical point
of
\Omega
. A critical point that lies on two lines of different slopes will be called
vertex-like
, otherwise it will be called
edge-like
. By our definition, a degenerate line can not contain any critical point.

(5)

A line in
\{\Pi_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
is said to be
redundant
over an interval
I\subseteq[1,\infty)
, if it does not contain any critical point in the region
I\times\mathbb{R}
. It is called
totally redundant
, if it is redundant over
[1,\infty)
. For example, degenerate lines are always totally redundant.

(6)

A line in
\{\Pi_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
is said to be
quasi-redundant
over an interval
I\subseteq[1,\infty)
, if it is redundant, or its intersection with
\Omega\cap(I\times\mathbb{R})
consists of only one point. For example, a quasi-redundant line which is not redundant over
I
intersects
\Omega
at exactly one point, which is necessarily a vertex-like critical point.

Definition 6.2.6
.

Notation as in Definition
6.2.5
.

(1)

Let
\Pi_{1}:y=a_{1}x+b_{1}
,
\Pi_{2}:y=a_{2}x+b_{2}
be two lines in
\mathbf{E}
, we say that
\Pi_{1}=\Pi_{2}
, if
a_{1}=a_{2},b_{1}=b_{2}
. We say that
\Pi_{1}

lies above

\Pi_{2}
, denoted by
\Pi_{1}\succeq\Pi_{2}
, if one of the following holds:

(a)

\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2}
are geometric, and
a_{1}\geq a_{2}
,
a_{1}+b_{1}\geq a_{2}+b_{2}
. Geometrically, this means
\Pi_{1}
lies above
\Pi_{2}
in the interval
[1,\infty)
.

(b)

\Pi_{1}
is degenerate and
\Pi_{2}
is geometric.

(c)

a_{1}=a_{2}=\infty
and
b_{1}\geq b_{2}
.

(d)

b_{1}=b_{2}=\infty
and
a_{1}\geq a_{2}
.

This defines a partial order on
\mathbf{E}
.

(2)

Two line configurations
\{\Pi_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
and
\{\Pi_{i}^{\prime}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
are said to be
equal
, if
\Pi_{i}=\Pi_{i}^{\prime}
for every
i
. A line configuration
\{\Pi_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
is said to
lie above
another line configuration
\{\Pi_{i}^{\prime}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
, if for every
i
,
\Pi_{i}\succeq\Pi_{i}^{\prime}
. We denote this by
\{\Pi_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}\succeq\{\Pi_{i}^{\prime}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
. This is a partial order on the set of line configurations of
n
lines.

Lemma 6.2.7
.

Suppose that
\{x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n},y_{1},y_{2},...,y_{n}\}
is a collection of power series in
\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]
and let
\{\Pi_{i}\}
be the associated line configuration.
If
\{\underline{x},\underline{y}\}
is
Q_{s}
-degenerate, then any critical point
of the
Q
-polygon within
[1,p^{s}]\times\mathbb{R}
must lie on either at least two lines (counting multiplicity), or a line of form
y=dx+d
.

Proof.

If (
42
) holds, then at least two elements in
\{x_{i}^{p^{r}}y_{i},x_{i}y_{i}^{p^{r}}\}_{i=1}^{n}
achieve the smallest valuation.
∎

Remark 6.2.8

(Redundant line principle)
.

Let
\{x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n},y_{1},y_{2},...,y_{n}\}
be a
Q_{s}
-degenerate collection of power series in
\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]
. Let
\{\Pi_{i}\}
be the associated line configuration. If
\{\Pi_{i}\}_{i\in\Delta\subseteq\{1,2,...,n\}}
are redundant over
[1,p^{s}]
, then the collection
\{x_{i},y_{i}\}_{i\in\{1,2,...,n\}\setminus\Delta}
is again
Q_{s}
-degenerate.

6.2.9.
Q
-isometry and maximal elements

Definition 6.2.10
.

Let
\{x_{1},...,x_{n},y_{1},...,y_{n}\}
be a collection of power series in
\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]
. The
Q
-isometry class
of
\{\underline{x},\underline{y}\}
is the set of all collections
\{x_{1}^{\prime},...,x_{n}^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime},...,y_{n}^{\prime}\}
such that

(\underline{x}^{\prime},\underline{y}^{\prime})=(\underline{x},\underline{y})\cdot R,

where
R\in\mathrm{SO}(Q_{0})(\mathbb{F}_{p})
, and
Q_{0}=\begin{bmatrix}O&I_{n}\\
I_{n}&O\end{bmatrix}
. For example, the following three operations produce elements in the
Q
-isometry class of
\{\underline{x},\underline{y}\}
:

(1)

re-indexing:
(x_{1}^{\prime},...,x_{n}^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime},...,y_{n}^{\prime})=(x_{\sigma(1)},...,x_{\sigma(n)},y_{\sigma(1)},...,y_{\sigma(n)})
for some
\sigma\in S_{n}
.

(2)

swapping:
x^{\prime}_{i},y^{\prime}_{i}\leftrightarrow y_{i},x_{i}
.

(3)

quantum re-indexing:
(x_{1}^{\prime},...,x_{n}^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime},...,y_{n}^{\prime})=((x_{1},...,x_{n})B,(y_{1},...,y_{n})B^{-1,T})
, where
B\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
.

It is clear that the
Q
-isometry class of a given collection is a finite set.

The
Q
-isometric class can be best understood in the following context: Fix
n
. We can form a
frame space

V_{\mathrm{frame}}=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{X_{1},...,X_{n},Y_{1},...,Y_{n}\},

where
X_{i},Y_{i}
are merely symbols. This is a quadratic
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-space with pairing

Q\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}X_{i}+b_{i}Y_{i}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}b_{i}.

We can then say that a collection
\{\underline{x},\underline{y}\}
of power series in
\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]
is an
\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]
-realization
of
\{\underline{X},\underline{Y}\}
, written as
\{\underline{X},\underline{Y}\}\rightarrowtail\{\underline{x},\underline{y}\}
. Then any element in the
Q
-isometry class of
\{\underline{x},\underline{y}\}
is induced from an isometry of
V_{\mathrm{frame}}
:

\{\underline{X},\underline{Y}\}\xrightarrow{R}\{\underline{X},\underline{Y}\}\rightarrowtail\{\underline{x},\underline{y}\}.

Definition 6.2.11
.

Let
\{x_{1},...,x_{n},y_{1},...,y_{n}\}
be a collection of power series in
\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]
and let
\{\Pi_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
be the associated line configuration. The
Q
-isometry class
of
\{\Pi_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
is the finite set of line configurations associated to the elements in the
Q
-isometry class of
\{\underline{x},\underline{y}\}
. A
maximal configuration
in the
Q
-isometry class of
\{\Pi_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
is a maximal element in the partial order
\succeq
, see Definition
6.2.6
(
2
). A collection
\{\underline{x},\underline{y}\}
is said to be
maximal
in its
Q
-isometry class, if the associated line configuration is maximal in its
Q
-isometry class.

Remark 6.2.12
.

The following observations are clear:

(1)

If
\{x_{1},...,x_{n},y_{1},...,y_{n}\}
be a collection of power series in
\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]
, maximal in its
Q
-isometry class. Then for any
\Sigma\subseteq\{1,2,...,n\}
, the subcollection
\{x_{i},y_{i}\}_{i\in\Sigma}
is also maximal in its own
Q
-isometry class.

(2)

The property of being maximal is preserved under re-indexing and swapping, but not under a general quantum re-indexing.

(3)

The multiset of lines in the line configuration is also preserved under re-indexing and swapping, but not under a general quantum re-indexing.

Maximal collections have nice properties. The following lemmas will be extremely useful:

Lemma 6.2.13
.

Suppose that
\{x_{1},...,x_{n},y_{1},...,y_{n}\}
is a collection of power series in
\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]
, which is maximal in its
Q
-isometry class. Then
x_{1},...,x_{n}
are
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-hyper-independent. The same holds for
y_{1},...,y_{n}
.

Proof.

Let
v\in\mathbb{N}
and
D_{v}=\{i\in\{1,2,...,n\}:v_{t}(x_{i})=v\}
. Let
\alpha_{i},i\in D_{v}
be the leading coefficient of
x_{i}
. It suffices to show that

\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in D_{v}}
are
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-independent. Suppose this is not the case. Up to re-indexing, we can take
D_{v}=\{1,2,...,l\}
and there is an
r\leq l
and
c_{1},c_{2},...,c_{r}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{*}
, such that
c_{1}\alpha_{1}+...+c_{r}\alpha_{r}=0
. Possibly re-indexing again, let
v_{t}(y_{1})=\max_{1\leq i\leq r}\{v_{t}(y_{i})\}
. Without loss of generality, let
c_{1}=1
. We can do a quantum re-indexing (Definition
6.2.10
) by
x_{1}^{\prime}=c_{1}x_{1}+...+c_{r}x_{r}
, and
x_{i}^{\prime}=x_{i}
if
i\geq 2
. The effect of this quantum re-indexing on
y_{i}
is
y_{1}^{\prime}=y_{1}
,
y_{i}^{\prime}=y_{i}-c_{i}y_{1}
when
2\leq i\leq r
and
y_{i}^{\prime}=y_{i}
when
i>r
. Now
v_{t}(x_{1}^{\prime})>v_{t}(x_{1})
and
v_{t}(y_{1}^{\prime})=v_{t}(y_{1})
, and when
2\leq i\leq r
we have
v_{t}(y_{i}^{\prime})\geq v_{t}(y_{i})
. As a result, while the new collection
\{\underline{x}^{\prime},\underline{y}^{\prime}\}
lies in the same
Q
-isometry class, its line configuration lies strictly above the original one, contradicting the maximality of
\{\underline{x},\underline{y}\}
.
∎

Lemma 6.2.14
.

Suppose that
\{x_{1},...,x_{n},y_{1},...,y_{n}\}
is a collection of power series in
\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]
, which is maximal in its
Q
-isometry class. Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
and let
C_{d}=\{i\in\{1,2,...,n\}:v_{t}(x_{i})=v_{t}(y_{i})=d\}
. Then

V^{+}_{d}:=\left\{\sum_{i\in C_{d}}c_{i}X_{i}+d_{i}Y_{i}:v_{t}\left(\sum_{i\in C_{d}}c_{i}x_{i}+d_{i}y_{i}\right)>d\right\}\subseteq V_{\mathrm{frame}}

has dimension at most 2. In addition, as a quadratic
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-space,
V^{+}_{d}
is nondegenerate of inert type when
\dim V^{+}_{d}=2
, and is nondegenerate when
\dim V^{+}_{d}=1
.

Proof.

Let
V_{d}=\left\{\sum_{i\in C_{d}}c_{i}X_{i}+d_{i}Y_{i}\right\}\subseteq V_{\mathrm{frame}}
. Up to re-indexing, we can assume that
C_{d}=\{1,2,...,r\}
. We first show that the radical of
V^{+}_{d}
is
\mathbf{0}
. Suppose this is not the case, then up to an isometry of
V_{d}
, we have
X_{1}\in V^{+}_{d},Y_{1}\notin V_{d}^{+}
. This means that there is a collection
\{\underline{x}^{\prime},\underline{y}^{\prime}\}
in the
Q
-isometric class of
\{\underline{x},\underline{y}\}
,
whose line configuration lies strictly above
5
5

5

We note that any isometry applied to the set
\{x_{1}\ldots x_{r},y_{1}\ldots y_{r}\}
must necessarily produce lines
\succeq
the original lines, as we have the conditions
v_{t}(x_{i})=v_{t}(y_{i})=d
.
that of
\{\underline{x},\underline{y}\}
. A contradiction to the maximality.

Now we show that
V^{+}_{d}
can not contain a hyperbolic plane (i.e., a nondegenerate dimension 2 subspace of split type). Suppose this is not the case, then up to an isometry of
V_{d}
, we have
X_{1},Y_{1}\in V_{d}^{+}
. This again means that there is a collection
\{\underline{x}^{\prime},\underline{y}^{\prime}\}
in the
Q
-isometric class of
\{\underline{x},\underline{y}\}
,
whose line configuration lies strictly above that of
\{\underline{x},\underline{y}\}
. A contradiction to the maximality.

Since
V_{d}^{+}
has trivial radical, and has no hyperbolic subspace, it can only happen that
V_{d}^{+}
is inert of dimension 2, nondegenerate of dimension 1, or trivial.
∎

Lemma 6.2.15
.

Suppose that
\{x_{1},...,x_{n},y_{1},...,y_{n}\}
is a collection of power series in
\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]
, which is maximal in its
Q
-isometry class. Suppose that
d=v_{t}(x_{1})=...=v_{t}(x_{n})=v_{t}(y_{1})\leq...\leq v_{t}(y_{n})
and
d<v_{t}(y_{n})
. Then for all
a_{i},b_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
, the
t
-valuation of
x_{n}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n-1}(a_{i}x_{i}+b_{i}y_{i})
equals
d
.

Proof.

Suppose that the
t
-valuation the expression is larger than
d
. Let

x^{\prime}_{n}=x_{n}-(\sum_{1\leq i\leq n-1}a_{i}b_{i})y_{n}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n-1}(a_{i}x_{i}+b_{i}y_{i})
,
y^{\prime}_{n}=y_{n}
. For
1\leq i<n
, let
x^{\prime}_{i}=x_{i}-b_{i}y_{n}
,
y^{\prime}_{i}=y_{i}-a_{i}y_{n}
. Then the new collection
\{\underline{x}^{\prime},\underline{y}^{\prime}\}
is in the same
Q
-isometry class but with strictly larger line configuration. Contradiction.
∎

6.3.
Critical points and shape of the
Q
-polygon

In the following, we understand the behavior of critical points (Definition
6.2.5
(
4
)) of a line configuration. The main result is Corollary
6.3.7
.

6.3.1.
Dependence results

If
s_{1},...,s_{n}\in\mathbb{F}
, the Moore matrix
(s_{j}^{(i-1)})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
will be denoted by
M(s_{1},...,s_{n})
.

Lemma 6.3.2
.

Let
\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n},\beta_{1},...,\beta_{n}\in{\mathbb{F}}^{*}
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and consider the quantity
\gamma_{r}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{p^{N+r}}\beta_{i}
.
Suppose that
\gamma_{r}=0
for all
0\leq r\leq n-2
.

(a)

If
\gamma_{r}=0
for
r=n-1
, then
\dim\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n}\}<n
.

(b)

If
\gamma_{r}\neq 0
for
r=n-1
, then
\dim\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n}\}=\dim\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\beta_{1},...,\beta_{n}\}=n
.

Proof.

Note that it suffices to establish the case where
N=0
. The general case reduces to
N=0
case by setting
\alpha_{i}^{\prime}=\alpha^{p^{N}}_{i}
, and noting that
\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n}
are
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-linearly dependent if and only if
\alpha^{\prime}_{1},...,\alpha^{\prime}_{n}
are
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-linearly dependent.

(a)

The condition is equivalent to
M(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n})\cdot[\beta_{1},...,\beta_{n}]^{T}=0
. If
\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n}
are
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-linearly independent, then
\det M(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n})\neq 0
. This implies that
\beta_{i}=0
for all
i
. A contradiction.

(b)

The matrix
P=M(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n})^{(n-1)}\cdot M(\beta_{1},...,\beta_{n})^{T}
is a lower triangular matrix with diagonal entries
\gamma_{n-1},\gamma_{n-1}^{p},...,\gamma_{n-1}^{p^{n-1}}
. In particular,
P
is invertible. This shows that
M(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n})
and
M(\beta_{1},...,\beta_{n})
are both invertible.

∎

Lemma 6.3.3
.

Let
\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n},\beta_{1},...,\beta_{n}\in{\mathbb{F}}^{*}
and consider the quantity
q_{r}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\alpha_{i}^{p^{r}}\beta_{i}+\alpha_{i}\beta_{i}^{p^{r}})
. Suppose that
q_{r}=0
for all
0\leq r\leq n-1
.

(a)

\dim\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n},\beta_{1},...\beta_{n}\}\leq n
.

(b)

If
\beta_{i}/\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
for all
i
, then
\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n}
are
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-linearly dependent.

Proof.

(a)

Consider the vector space
\mathbb{F}^{2n}_{p}
with standard coordinates, with quadratic form
\langle-,-\rangle
given by
Q_{0}
as in Definition
6.2.10
. We now base change to
\mathbb{F}
. Consider the vector

\mathbf{v}=(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n},\beta_{1},...\beta_{n})\in\mathbb{F}^{2n}
. We use
\varphi^{i}\mathbf{v}
to denote the
i
-th Frobenius twist of
\mathbf{v}
. Let
V=\mathrm{Span}\{\varphi^{i}\mathbf{v}\}_{0\leq i\leq n-1}
. Then
V
is totally isotropic.

Claim 6.3.4
.

\varphi V=V
. In particular,
V
descends to an
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-subspace of
\mathbb{F}^{2n}_{p}
.

The claim follows almost immediately from Proposition
4.2.3
(c) by first proving
q_{r}=0
for all
r\geq n
. However, we give a more elementary proof.
6
6

6

This proof was found with the help of Gemini 3.0.
Suppose that
\dim V<n
. Let
d<n
be the smallest integer such that
\varphi^{d}\mathbf{v}\in\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}}\{\varphi^{i}\mathbf{v}\}_{0\leq i\leq d-1}
. Iterating the Frobenius, we find that for all
r\geq d
,
\varphi^{r}\mathbf{v}\in\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}}\{\varphi^{i}\mathbf{v}\}_{0\leq i\leq d-1}
. It follows that
\varphi{V}=V
. Now suppose that
\dim V=n
. Then
V
and
\varphi V
are maximal isotropical subspaces of
\mathbb{F}^{2n}
. Clearly
\dim V\cap\varphi V\geq n-1
. It suffices to show that the inequality is strict. Suppose for the sake of contradiction that
\dim V\cap\varphi V=n-1
. Then there is a reflection in
\mathrm{O}(Q_{0})(\mathbb{F})\setminus\mathrm{SO}(Q_{0})(\mathbb{F})
that takes
V
to
\varphi V
. In particular,
V
and
\varphi V
are not in the same
\mathrm{SO}(Q_{0})(\mathbb{F})
-orbit. On the other hand, since
Q_{0}
is split over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, the orthogonal Grassmanian
\mathrm{OG}(n,\mathbb{F}^{2n})
parametrizing maximal isotropic subspaces is an
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-subscheme of
\mathrm{Gr}(n,\mathbb{F}^{2n}_{p})
with two components, each being an
\mathrm{SO}(Q_{0})
-orbit. Since
\varphi
preserves each component,
V
and
\varphi V
must lie in the same component, hence in the same

\mathrm{SO}(Q_{0})(\mathbb{F})
-orbit. We immediately arrive at a contradiction. So the claim holds.

Now let
V_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{F}^{2n}_{p}
be the subspace with
V_{0}\otimes\mathbb{F}=V
. Pick
n
vectors
\mathbf{w}_{1},...,\mathbf{w}_{n}
that spans
V_{0}
. Let
\theta_{1},...,\theta_{n}\in\mathbb{F}
be such that
\mathbf{v}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\theta_{i}\mathbf{w}_{i}
. Then
\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n},\beta_{1},...\beta_{n}\}\subseteq\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\theta_{1},...,\theta_{n}\}
, which has dimension no more than
n
.

(b)

Let
\beta_{i}=\eta_{i}\alpha_{i}
, where
\eta_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{*}
. We find that for
0\leq r\leq n-1
,

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}\alpha_{i}^{p^{r}+1}=0\Rightarrow M(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n})\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{1}\eta_{1}\\
\alpha_{2}\eta_{2}\\
\vdots\\
\alpha_{n}\eta_{n}\end{bmatrix}=0.

If
\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n}
are
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-linearly independent, then
\det M(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n})\neq 0
. This implies that
\alpha_{i}\eta_{i}=0
for all
i
, a contradiction.

∎

6.3.5.
Application to critical points

Corollary 6.3.6
.

Let
\{x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n},y_{1},y_{2},...,y_{n}\}
be a
Q_{s}
-degenerate collection of nonzero power series in
\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]
, with leading coefficients
\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n},\beta_{1},...\beta_{n}
, respectively. Suppose that
\max\{v_{t}(x_{1}),...,v_{t}(x_{n})\}\leq\min\{v_{t}(y_{1}),...,v_{t}(y_{n})\}
. Let
\{\Pi_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
be the associated line configuration.

(a)

Suppose that
\ell\in\{\Pi_{i}\}
is a non-redundant line
not
of the form
y=dx+d
, and let

\Sigma=\{i\in[1,n]:\Pi_{i}=\ell\}
.
Suppose moreover that
\dim\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in\Sigma}=|\Sigma|
. Then
\ell
contains at most
\min\{s+1,|\Sigma|-1\}
edge-like critical points within
[1,p^{s}]\times\mathbb{R}
. If
\ell
contains exactly
|\Sigma|-1
edge-like critical points within
[1,p^{s}]\times\mathbb{R}
, then
\dim\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\beta_{i}\}_{i\in\Sigma}=|\Sigma|
.

(b)

Suppose that
\ell\in\{\Pi_{i}\}
is a non-redundant line of the form
y=dx+d
, and let
\Sigma=\{i\in[1,n]:\Pi_{i}=\ell\}
. Then
\ell
contains an edge-like critical point with
x
-coordinate 1. Suppose moreover that at least one of the following conditions holds

•

\dim\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}\}_{i\in\Sigma}>|\Sigma|
,

•

\dim\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in\Sigma}=|\Sigma|
and
\alpha_{i}/\beta_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
for all
i\in\Sigma
.

Then
\ell
contains at most
\min\{s+1,|\Sigma|-1\}
edge-like critical points within
[1,p^{s}]\times\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Note that the line
\Pi_{i}
has equation
y=v_{t}(x_{i})x+v_{t}(y_{i})
, and it is of the form
y=dx+d
if and only if
v_{t}(x_{i})=v_{t}(y_{i})
.

(a)

Up to re-index, we can assume that
\Sigma
is the subset
\{1,2,...,|\Sigma|\}
. Without loss of generality, we can also assume that
|\Sigma|-1\leq s
. Suppose for the sake of contradiction that
\ell
contains
|\Sigma|
edge-like critical points within
[p^{b},p^{b+|\Sigma|-1}]\times\mathbb{R}\subseteq[1,p^{s}]\times\mathbb{R}
. Then over the interval
[p^{b},p^{b+|\Sigma|-1}]
, every line other than
\mathcal{L}
is redundant. Since for any
i\in\Sigma
we must have
v_{t}(x_{i})<v_{t}(y_{i})
, we find that, for all
0\leq r\leq|\Sigma|-1
, we have
\sum_{i=1}^{|\Sigma|}\alpha_{i}^{p^{b+r}}\beta_{i}=0
.
Lemma
6.3.2
(a) implies that
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in\Sigma}
are
\mathbb{F}_{p}
