-linearly dependent. Contradictory to our assumption. This shows that
\ell
contains no more than
|\Sigma|-1
edge-like critical points within
[1,p^{s}]\times\mathbb{R}
.

If
\ell
contains exactly
|\Sigma|-1
edge-like critical points, say, they lie within
[p^{b},p^{b+|\Sigma|-2}]\times\mathbb{R}\subseteq[1,p^{s}]\times\mathbb{R}
. Then for all
0\leq r\leq|\Sigma|-2
, we have
\sum_{i=1}^{|\Sigma|}\alpha_{i}^{p^{b+r}}\beta_{i}=0
. The same reason as in the last paragraph implies that the collection is not
Q_{|\Sigma|-1}
-degenerate. Now we can apply Lemma
6.3.2
(b) to conclude that
\{\beta_{i}\}_{i\in\Sigma}
are also
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-linearly independent.

(b)

Suppose that
\ell
does not contain an edge-like critical point with
x
-coordinate 1, then there exists a different non-redundant line, say
\Pi_{n}
, that contains a critical point with
x
-coordinate 1. In order for
\Pi_{n}
and
\ell
to be non-redundant,
\Pi_{n}
must have bigger slope and smaller
y
-intercept than
\ell
. It follows that
v_{t}(x_{n})>d>v_{t}(y_{n})
, violating our assumption on valuations. So
\ell
contains an edge-like critical point with
x
-coordinate 1. The rest of the proof is similar to (a): instead of using Lemma
6.3.2
, we use Lemma
6.3.3
(a) for the first bullet point, and Lemma
6.3.3
(b) for the second bullet point.

∎

Corollary 6.3.7
.

Let
\{x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n},y_{1},y_{2},...,y_{n}\}
be a
Q_{s}
-degenerate collection of nonzero power series in
\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]
, which is maximal in its
Q
-isometry class. Suppose that
\max\{v_{t}(x_{1}),...,v_{t}(x_{n})\}\leq\min\{v_{t}(y_{1}),...,v_{t}(y_{n})\}
. Write the line configuration
\{\Pi_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
as a multiset
\{n_{j}\cdot\ell_{j}^{\prime}\}_{1\leq j\leq u}
; cf. Definition
6.2.5
(
2
).

(a)

Each
\ell^{\prime}_{i}
contains at most
\min\{s+1,n_{j}-1\}
edge-like critical points within
[1,p^{s}]\times\mathbb{R}
.

(b)

Let
\mathcal{R}
be the set of indices
i
such that
\Pi_{i}
is not quasi-redundant over
[1,p^{s}]
; cf. Definition
6.2.5
(
6
), then
|\mathcal{R}|\geq s+2
.

(c)

Suppose that
|\mathcal{R}|=s+2
. Write
\{\Pi_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{R}}
as a multiset
\{m_{j}\cdot\ell_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq w}
. Then

(i)

\forall 1\leq j,j^{\prime}\leq w
,
\ell_{j}\cap\ell_{j^{\prime}}
is a vertex-like critical point within
[1,p^{s}]\times\mathbb{R}
.

(ii)

Every
\ell_{j}
contains exactly
m_{j}-1
edge-like critical points within
[1,p^{s}]\times\mathbb{R}
.

(See Figure
2
for a graph of the line configuration when the slope of
\ell_{j}
’s are strictly increasing.)

Proof.

Let
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}
be the leading coefficients of
x_{i},y_{i}
.

(a)

Since the collection is maximal, by Lemma
6.2.13
, the condition that “
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in\Sigma}
are
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-linearly independent” in Corollary
6.3.6
(a)
is met. Similarly, the condition of
Corollary
6.3.6
(b)
is also met up to a
Q
-isometry that does not change the line configuration: By Lemma
6.2.13
, we know that

\dim\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in\Sigma}=\dim\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\beta_{i}\}_{i\in\Sigma}=|\Sigma|.

By
Lemma
6.2.14
, we know that

\dim\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}\}_{i\in\Sigma}\geq 2|\Sigma|-2.

So if the first condition is not met, i.e.,
\dim\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}\}_{i\in\Sigma}\leq|\Sigma|
, then we must have
|\Sigma|\leq 2
. If
|\Sigma|=1
, then we must have
\alpha_{i}/\beta_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
for the unique
i\in\Sigma
, so the second condition is met. If
|\Sigma|=2
, then the space
V_{d}^{+}
in Lemma
6.2.14
is inert. It is clear that by a
Q
-isometry that does not change the line configuration, we can assume that
\alpha_{i}/\beta_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
, so the second condition is met.

(b)

Let
\{\Pi_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{R}}=\{m_{j}\cdot\ell_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq w}
. Let
N
be the number of critical points in
[1,p^{s}]\times\mathbb{R}
that lie on
\ell_{1},...,\ell_{w}
. Since every critical point in
[1,p^{s}]\times\mathbb{R}
lies on some
\ell_{j}
, we have

N=s+1.

On the other hand, each
\ell_{j}
contains at most
m_{j}-1
edge-like critical points within
[1,p^{s}]\times\mathbb{R}
by (a). In addition,
w
distinct lines can have at most
w-1
intersections, so there are at most
w-1
vertex like critical points within
[1,p^{s}]\times\mathbb{R}
. As a result,

(44)

N\leq w-1+\sum_{j=1}^{w}(m_{j}-1)=-1+\sum_{j=1}^{w}m_{j}.

It follows that

-1+\sum_{j=1}^{w}m_{j}\geq N=s+1.

So
|\mathcal{R}|=\sum_{j=1}^{w}m_{j}\geq s+2
.

(c)

If
|\mathcal{R}|=s+2
, then (
44
) is an equality. This means that any intersection
\ell_{j}\cap\ell_{j^{\prime}}
is a vertex-like critical point within
[1,p^{s}]\times\mathbb{R}
, and every
\ell_{j}
contains exactly
m_{j}-1
edge-like critical points within
[1,p^{s}]\times\mathbb{R}
.

∎

\ell_{w}
\displaystyle\ell_{w-1}
\displaystyle\ell_{w-2}
\displaystyle\ell_{1}
\displaystyle\Omega
\displaystyle\vdots
1
\displaystyle p^{2}
\displaystyle p
\displaystyle\cdots

Figure 2
.
Line configuration of
\{m_{j}\cdot\ell_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq w}
with critical points.

6.4.
Pattern of the first order decay

Let recall the setup in §
5.2
: we have a collection

(45)

\{x_{1},...,x_{m},y_{1},...,y_{m}\}

of nonzero series in
\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]
given by the equation of the formal curve
C
. We also have
Q_{0}(t)=...=Q_{a-1}(t)=0
and
Q_{a}(t)\neq 0
. We have defined
h_{i}=v_{t}(Q_{i}(t))
. Furthermore,
a_{1}=a<a_{2}\ldots<a_{k}
is the maximal sequence of integers such that
a_{k}\leq m-1
, and
h_{a_{1}}\geq h_{a_{2}}\ldots\geq h_{a_{k}}
. Note that any collection in the
Q
-isometry class of (
45
) is
Q_{a_{k}-1}
-degenerate.

Setup 6.4.1
.

We will always work with a collection (
45
) which is maximal in its
Q
-isometry class.

Definition 6.4.2
.

Let
\Lambda\subseteq\mathcal{L}
denote a sublattice of
\mathcal{L}
. We will put a bar on
\Lambda
to denote the mod
p\mathcal{L}
reduction of
\Lambda
, regraded as a sublattice of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
. For example,
\overline{\mathcal{L}_{1}}=\mathcal{L}_{1}/p\mathcal{L}=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\{e_{1},...,e_{m},f_{1},...,f_{m}\}\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
. We denote by
\overline{e^{\prime}},\overline{f^{\prime}},\overline{e_{i}},\overline{f_{i}}
the image of
e^{\prime},f^{\prime},e_{i},f_{i}
in
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
. We will also naturally identify
\overline{\mathcal{L}_{1}}
with
V_{\mathrm{frame}}
; cf. §
6.2.10
, by
\overline{e_{i}}\rightarrow X_{i}
and
\overline{f_{i}}\rightarrow Y_{i}
.

Definition 6.4.3
.

(1)

Let
\{\Pi_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq m}
be the line configuration associated to the collection (
45
). Let
r
be the number of quasi-redundant lines over
[1,p^{a_{k}}]
, and let
r_{0}\leq r
be the number of redundant lines over
[1,p^{a_{k}}]
. Let
n=m-r
, and
n_{0}=m-r_{0}
.

(2)

If
n\leq a_{k}+1
, we say that the collection
\{\underline{x},\underline{y}\}
is
thin
. Otherwise, it is
thick
.

(3)

A
borderline
is a line
\Pi_{i}
which is not quasi-redundant over
[1,p^{a_{k}}]
, and has equation
y=dx+d
. If a borderline exists, then it is unique.

(4)

Define the
redundant subspace

\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{rd}}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{L}_{1}}
to be the subspace spanned by all
\overline{e_{i}},\overline{f_{i}}
for those
i
such that
\Pi_{i}
is quasi-redundant over
[1,p^{a_{k}}]
.

(5)

Define the
borderline subspace

\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{bd}}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{rv}}
as follows: if a borderline exists, and let
d
be the border, then
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{bd}}
is the subspace
V^{+}_{d}\subseteq V_{\mathrm{frame}}=\overline{\mathcal{L}_{1}}
in Lemma
6.2.14
, which is either
\mathbf{0}
, or nondegenerate of dimension 1, or inert of dimension 2. If a borderline does not exist, then we set
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{bd}}=\mathbf{0}
.

(6)

Define
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{brd}}:=\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{bd}}\oplus\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{rd}}
. Here we are using the notation
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{brd}}
instead of
\overline{\mathcal{L}_{\text{brd}}}
since there is no apriori defined
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-lattice
{\mathcal{L}_{\text{brd}}}
. The reader is encouraged to ignore this notational nuance: later on, we will define a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-lattice
{\mathcal{L}_{\text{brd}}}
whose mod
p\mathcal{L}
reduction is
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{brd}}
, and then
\overline{\mathcal{L}_{\text{brd}}}=\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{brd}}
.

Assumption 6.4.4
.

Starting from a maximal collection
\{\underline{x},\underline{y}\}
, we may perform finitely many steps of re-indexing and swapping so that
\{\underline{x},\underline{y}\}
satisfies one of the two following two assumptions (the two assumptions
may not
be mutually compatible):

(A1)

\Pi_{m},\Pi_{m-1},...,\Pi_{n_{0}+1}
are redundant over
[1,p^{a_{k}}]
,

\forall i\in[1,m]
,
v_{t}(x_{i})\leq v_{t}(y_{i})
,

v_{t}(x_{1})\leq v_{t}(x_{2})\leq...\leq v_{t}(x_{n_{0}})
.

(A2)

\Pi_{m},\Pi_{m-1},...,\Pi_{n_{0}+1}
are redundant over
[1,p^{a_{k}}]
,

\Pi_{n_{0}},\Pi_{n_{0}-1},...,\Pi_{n+1}
are quasi-redundant but not redundant over
[1,p^{a_{k}}]
,

\forall i\in[1,m]
,
v_{t}(x_{i})\leq v_{t}(y_{i})
,

v_{t}(x_{1})\leq v_{t}(x_{2})\leq...\leq v_{t}(x_{n})
.

Remark 6.4.5
.

As the property of being maximal is preserved under re-indexing and swapping, imposing one of the above assumptions on
\{\underline{x},\underline{y}\}
is consistent maximality.

Lemma 6.4.6
.

Under Assumption
6.4.4
(A1)
, we have
v_{t}(x_{n_{0}})\leq v_{t}(y_{i})
for all
1\leq i\leq m
. Furthermore, we have

(46)

v_{t}(x_{1})\leq v_{t}(x_{2})\leq...\leq v_{t}(x_{n_{0}})\leq v_{t}(y_{n_{0}})\leq v_{t}(y_{n_{0}-1})\leq...\leq v_{t}(y_{1}).

Proof.

We first prove that
v_{t}(x_{n_{0}})\leq v_{t}(y_{i})
for all
i
. Suppose that this is not the case, let
j
be such that
v_{t}(y_{j})=\min\{v_{t}(y_{1}),...,v_{t}(y_{m})\}
. Then
v_{t}(y_{j})<v_{t}(x_{n_{0}})
. Then
j\neq n_{0}
and
v_{t}(x_{j})\leq v_{t}(y_{j})<v_{t}(x_{n_{0}})\leq v_{t}(y_{n_{0}})
. This means that
\Pi_{n_{0}}\succ\Pi_{j}
, rendering
\Pi_{n_{0}}
totally redundant. A contradiction. To show that (
46
) holds, note that if there is some
v_{t}(y_{i})>v_{t}(y_{i-1})
, then
\Pi_{i}\succ\Pi_{i-1}
, rendering
\Pi_{i}
totally redundant. A contradiction.

∎

We have the following two key propositions.

Proposition 6.4.7
.

Assume
6.4.4
(A2)
.

(a)

We have
v_{t}(x_{n})\leq v_{t}(y_{i})
for all
1\leq i\leq m
, and
v_{t}(x_{n})<v_{t}(y_{i})
if
n<i\leq m
. Furthermore,

(47)

v_{t}(x_{1})\leq v_{t}(x_{2})\leq...\leq v_{t}(x_{n})\leq v_{t}(y_{n})\leq v_{t}(y_{n-1})\leq...\leq v_{t}(y_{1}).

(b)

Suppose that the collection is thin. Then
n=a_{k}+1
. Moreover, we have

(48)

v_{t}(y_{n-i-1})-v_{t}(y_{n-i})=p^{i}[v_{t}(x_{n-i})-v_{t}(x_{n-i-1})],\;\;0\leq i\leq n-2.

(49)

h_{a_{k}}\geq v_{t}(y_{1})+p^{a_{k}}v_{t}(x_{1}).

(c)

Suppose that the collection is thick. Then

(50)

p^{i}[v_{t}(x_{n-i})-v_{t}(x_{n-i-1})]\leq v_{t}(y_{n-i-1})-v_{t}(y_{n-i})\leq p^{i+1}[v_{t}(x_{n-i})-v_{t}(x_{n-i-1})],\;\;0\leq i\leq n-2

(51)

h_{a_{k}}\geq v_{t}(y_{1})+p^{a_{k}}v_{t}(x_{1})

Proof.

(a)

Ignore the assertion
v_{t}(x_{n})<v_{t}(y_{i})
for
n<i\leq m
, the statement follows directly from Lemma
6.4.6
by throwing away quasi-redundant indices. Now we treat the case
n<i\leq m
separately. It suffices to show that
v_{t}(x_{n})\neq v_{t}(y_{i})
for all
n<i\leq m
. Suppose this is not the case, i.e., there exists some such
i
such that
v_{t}(x_{i})\leq v_{t}(y_{i})=v_{t}(x_{n})\leq v_{t}(y_{n})
. Then
\Pi_{n}\succeq\Pi_{i}
. Since
\Pi_{i}
is quasi-redundant over
[1,p^{a_{k}}]
,
\Pi_{n}
must also be quasi-redundant. Contradiction.

(b)

Note that the claim “
n=a_{k}+1
” only depends on the multiset of lines, so it suffices to prove
n\geq a_{k}+1
under Assumption
6.4.4
(A1)
. Note that the subcollection
\{x_{i},y_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n_{0}}
is again maximal and
Q_{a_{k}-1}
-degenerate. Apply Corollary
6.3.7
to
\{x_{i},y_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n_{0}}
with
s=a_{k}-1
. Note that all conditions of the corollary are met (the condition
\max\{v_{t}(x_{1}),...,v_{t}(x_{n_{0}})\}\leq\min\{v_{t}(y_{1}),...,v_{t}(y_{n_{0}})\}
is satisfied because of Lemma
6.4.6
). It follows that
n\geq a_{k}+1
by the second part Corollary
6.3.7
. Since being “thin” means
n\leq a_{k}+1
, we find that
n=a_{k}+1
.

For the formulas (
48
) and (
49
), we use the third part of Corollary
6.3.7
. To help visualize it, we note that the critical points and the non-quasi-redundant lines are arranged in Figure
2
. Now switch back to Assumption
6.4.4
(A2)
. It is clear that
\Pi_{n-i-1}\cap\Pi_{n-i}
contains a point with
x
-coordinate
p^{i}
. As a result,

p^{i}v_{t}(x_{n-i-1})+v_{t}(y_{n-i-1})=p^{i}v_{t}(x_{n-i})+v_{t}(y_{n-i}).

This proves (
48
).

Note that
\Pi_{1}
lies below other lines in the collection
\{\Pi_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
over the interval
[p^{a_{k}},\infty)
. If
\Pi_{i}
is quasi-redundant, then it surely lies above
\Pi_{1}
, at least over the point
x=p^{a_{k}}
. It follows that

(52)

h_{a_{k}}\geq\Pi_{1}(p^{a_{k}})=v_{t}(x_{1})p^{a_{k}}+v_{t}(y_{1}).

(c)

The thick case can be deduced in a manner similar to the thin case. In fact, the graph of
\{\Pi_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
again looks like Figure
2
, except that the intersection of two lines may no longer contain a vertex-like critical point. So the outcome is weaker:
\Pi_{n-i}\cap\Pi_{n-i-1}
contains an intersection point with
x
-coordinate in
[p^{i},p^{i+1}]
. Using this, we get (
50
). By a similar reason as in the thin case, we have
h_{a_{k}}\geq v_{t}(x_{1})p^{a_{k}}+v_{t}(y_{1})
.

∎

Proposition 6.4.8
.

Assume
6.4.4
(A2)
. Let
d=v_{t}(x_{n})=\max\{v_{t}(x_{i})\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
. We have the following description of
\overline{L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
:

(a)

If
r\leq d
, then

\overline{L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\overline{e^{\prime}},\overline{f^{\prime}},\overline{f_{1}},\overline{f_{2}},...,\overline{f_{m}}\}\oplus\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\overline{e_{i}}:v_{t}(x_{i})\geq r\}_{1\leq i\leq m}.

(b)

If
d<r\leq h_{a_{k}}
, then

\overline{L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\overline{e^{\prime}},\overline{f^{\prime}},\overline{f_{i}}:v_{t}(y_{i})\geq r\}_{1\leq i\leq n}\oplus\overline{\Delta}_{r},

where

\overline{\Delta}_{r}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{brd}}\oplus\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\overline{f_{i}}:v_{t}(y_{i})<r\}_{1\leq i\leq n}

is a subspace whose projection to
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{brd}}
is an isometrical embedding.

(c)

If
r>h_{a_{k}}
and
a_{k}=a
, then

\overline{L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{brd}}\oplus\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\overline{e^{\prime}},\overline{f^{\prime}},\overline{f_{1}},\overline{f_{2}},...,\overline{f_{n}}\}

is a subspace whose projection to
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{brd}}
is an isometrical embedding.

Proof.

Recall that we are working in the setup that
\{\Pi_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq m}
is maximal in its
Q
-isometry class. By Lemma
6.2.13
,
x_{1},...,x_{m}
are
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-hyper-independent, the same holds for
y_{1},...,y_{m}
. By Proposition
6.4.7
(a)
, we also have
d\leq v_{t}(y_{i})
for all
1\leq i\leq m
and
d<v_{t}(y_{i})
for
i>n
. In addition,
\overline{e^{\prime}},\overline{f^{\prime}}\in\overline{L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
for all
r\leq h_{a_{k}}
. These results will be freely used in the proof without mentioning.

(a)

The
r\leq d
case follows easily from the above mentioned results.

(b)

Suppose now that
d<r\leq h_{a_{k}}
. Up to a
Q
-isometry that keeps Assumption
6.4.4
(A2)
, we can assume that
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{bd}}=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\overline{e_{n}}-\gamma_{n}\overline{f_{n}}\}
(when
\dim\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{bd}}=1
) or
\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\overline{e_{n-1}}-\gamma_{n-1}\overline{f_{n-1}},\overline{e_{n}}-\gamma_{n}\overline{f_{n}}\}
(when
\dim\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{bd}}=2
). We then define the following
relevant subspace
which is complementary to
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{bd}}
:

\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{rv}}:=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\overline{e^{\prime}},\overline{f^{\prime}},\overline{e_{j}},\overline{f_{i}}\}_{1\leq i\leq n,1\leq j\leq n-\dim\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{bd}}}.

Since
y_{1},...,y_{m}
are
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-hyper-independent, we see that
\overline{L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\cap\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{rv}}=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\overline{e^{\prime}},\overline{f^{\prime}},\overline{f_{i}}:v_{t}(y_{i})\geq r\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
. Suppose that
e\in\overline{L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
is an element that is not contained in
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{rv}}
. After removing its components in
\overline{e^{\prime}},\overline{f^{\prime}}
, we can assume that
e\in\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\overline{e_{i}},\overline{f_{i}}\}_{1\leq i\leq m}
. We can uniquely write
e=e_{\mathrm{brd}}+e_{\mathrm{rv}}
, where
0\neq e_{\mathrm{brd}}\in\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{brd}}
and
e_{\mathrm{rv}}\in\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{rv}}
. More explicitly:

e_{\mathrm{rv}}=\sum_{j=1}^{n-\dim\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{bd}}}a_{j}\overline{e_{j}}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{j}\overline{f_{j}},\;\;\;e_{\mathrm{brd}}=\heartsuit+\sum_{i=n+1}^{m}(c_{i}\overline{e_{i}}+d_{i}\overline{f_{i}}),

where
\heartsuit
is 0,
c(\overline{e_{n}}-\gamma_{n}\overline{f_{n}})
or a linear combination of
\overline{e_{n-1}}-\gamma_{n-1}\overline{f_{n-1}},\overline{e_{n}}-\gamma_{n}\overline{f_{n}}
, depending on what
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{bd}}
is. Let
x\in\mathbb{F}[\![t]\!]
be the power series corresponding to
e
, similar for
x_{\mathrm{brd}}
and
x_{\mathrm{rv}}
.

In the following, we claim that
e_{\mathrm{rv}}\in\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\overline{f_{i}}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
. In other words,
a_{j}=0
in the above expression. Note that (
6.4.8
) follows immediately from the claim.

We prove the claim by contradiction. Suppose the claim does not hold. Then there exists a smallest integer
j_{0}
such that
a_{j_{0}}\neq 0
. Let
\lambda\leq j_{0}
be the smallest integer that
v_{t}(x_{\lambda})=v_{t}(x_{j_{0}})
. Then
v_{t}(x_{\mathrm{rv}})=v_{t}(x_{\lambda})\leq d<r
. On the other hand, we know that
v_{t}(x)\geq r
. It follows that
v_{t}(x_{\mathrm{brd}})=v_{t}(x_{\mathrm{rv}})
and the leading terms of
x_{\mathrm{brd}}
and
x_{\mathrm{rv}}
must cancel out. Let
d^{\prime}=\min_{1\leq i\leq m}\{v_{t}(y_{i})\}
, this quantity is no smaller than
d
. Suppose that
v_{t}(x_{\lambda})<d^{\prime}
. Then any
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-combination of
y_{i}
’s has degree bigger than
v_{t}(x_{\lambda})
. On the other hand,
\overline{e_{n+1-i}}-\gamma_{n+1-i}\overline{f_{n+1-i}}
for
1\leq i\leq\dim\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{bd}}
all have degree no less than
d+1>v_{t}(x_{\lambda})
. As a result, if the leading terms of
x_{\mathrm{brd}}
and
x_{\mathrm{rv}}
cancel, then
x_{1},...,x_{m}
are not
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-hyper-independent. So this case can not happen. Now suppose that
v_{t}(x_{\lambda})=d^{\prime}
. Then
d=d^{\prime}
. By Proposition
6.4.7
(a)
and the non-redundancy of
\{\Pi_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
, we have
d=v_{t}(x_{\lambda})=v_{t}(x_{\lambda+1})=...=v_{t}(x_{n})=v_{t}(y_{n})=v_{t}(y_{n-1})=...=v_{t}(y_{\lambda})>v_{t}(y_{\lambda-1})
.
Since
v_{t}(y_{i})>d
for all
n<i\leq m
, we find that

(53)

\sum_{j=\lambda}^{n-\dim\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{bd}}}a_{j}x_{j}+\sum_{j=\lambda}^{n}b_{j}y_{j}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i>n\\
v_{t}(x_{i})=d\end{subarray}}c_{i}x_{i}

has degree bigger than
d
. Now up to a re-indexing we can assume that the set
\{i>n:v_{t}(x_{i})=d\}
is
\{n+1,n+2,...,n+l\}
, and furthermore
v_{t}(y_{n+1})\leq...\leq v_{t}(y_{n+l})
. Applying Lemma
6.2.15
to the collection
\{x_{\lambda},x_{\lambda+1},...,x_{n},...x_{n+l};y_{\lambda},y_{\lambda+1},...,y_{n},...,y_{n+l}\}
, we find that (
53
) has degree equal to
d
. This leads to a contradiction.

(c)

Take
r=h_{a_{k}}
in
(b)
and use the fact that
h_{a_{k}}>v_{t}(y_{1})
from Proposition
6.4.7
, we see that
\overline{L^{\prime}_{P,h_{a_{k}}}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}=\overline{\mathcal{L}_{0}}\oplus\overline{\Delta}_{h_{a_{k}}}
, where
\overline{\Delta}_{h_{a_{k}}}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{brd}}\oplus\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\overline{f_{i}}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
whose projection to
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{brd}}
is an isometrical embedding.

Now suppose that
r>h_{a_{k}}
and
a_{k}=a
. The lattice
\mathcal{L}_{0}
decays rapidly to arbitrary order. So
\overline{L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\cap\overline{\mathcal{L}_{0}}=\mathbf{0}
. Since
\overline{L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\subseteq\overline{L^{\prime}_{P,h_{a_{k}}}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
, the desired claim follows easily.

∎

6.5.
Estimating
S^{\prime}(M)
: preparation

We continue to use the setup in §
6.4
, and will assume that our maximal collection satisfies Assumption
6.4.4
(A2)
.

In this section, we make various preparations for computing
S^{\prime}(M)
. First, we split out cases from three perspectives. Second, we define auxiliary lattices
L_{P,r}^{\prime\prime}
generalizing the construction in the toy example §
6.1
, based on a cases by case study (however, the explicit computation of
L_{P,r}^{\prime\prime}
, and the relation between
S^{\prime}(M)
and
S^{\prime\prime}(M)
will be carried out in the next two sections §
6.6
\sim
§
6.7
). Third, we compute local density for
L_{P,r}^{\prime\prime}
. Fourth, we estimate a quantity
D_{0}
which will appear in various computation later on.

6.5.1.
Split out cases and set up notations

We have three different rules to split out cases: depending on whether
a_{k}>a
, we have two cases; depending on the dimension of

\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{bd}}
, we have three cases; depending on whether
n>a_{k}+1
, we have two cases. Let’s spell them out:

(1)

If
a_{k}=a
(resp.
a_{k}>a
), we say that we are in
tight
(resp.
loose
)
case
.

(2)

If
\dim\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{bd}}=i\in\{0,1,2\}
, we say that we are in
borderline case

i
.

(3)

If
n>a_{k}+1
(resp.
n=a_{k}+1
), we say that we are in
thick
(resp.
thin
)
case
. This is already defined in Definition
6.4.3
, and the fact that
n\leq a_{k}+1
implies
n=a_{k}+1
is proved in Proposition
6.4.7
.

The three different rules do not depend on each other, so we have
2\times 3\times 2=12
subcases in total. For example, when
a_{k}=a
,
\dim\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{bd}}=2
and
n>a_{k}+1
, we are in tight thick borderline case 2.

Notation 6.5.2
.

To easy notation, from now on we will write
c_{i}=v_{t}(x_{i})
and
d_{i}=v_{t}(y_{i})
for all
i\in[1,m]
. We rewrite the formulae (
47
), (
48
), (
50
), (
49
) and (
51
) from Proposition
6.4.7
as follows:

(54)

\text{thin \& thick cases:\;\;\;\;}c_{1}\leq c_{2}\leq...\leq c_{n}\leq d_{n}\leq d_{n-1}\leq...\leq d_{1}.

(55)

\text{thin case:\;\;\;\;}d_{i}-d_{i+1}=p^{n-1-i}(c_{i+1}-c_{i}),\;\;1\leq i\leq n-1.

(56)

\text{thick case:\;\;\;\;}p^{n-1-i}(c_{i+1}-c_{i})\leq d_{i}-d_{i+1}\leq p^{n-i}(c_{i+1}-c_{i}),\;\;1\leq i\leq n-1.

(57)

\text{thin \& thick cases:\;\;\;\;}h_{a_{k}}\geq p^{a_{k}}c_{1}+d_{1}.

6.5.3.
Auxiliary lattices: the tight case

Assume that
a_{k}=a
. In this section, we define a sequence of auxiliary lattices
L_{P,r}^{\prime\prime}
, generalizing the construction in the toy example §
6.1
.

Recall we have the borderline subspace
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{bd}}
and redundant subspace
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{rd}}
as per Definition
6.4.3
. We now define
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-lattices
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{bd}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{rd}}
as follows. We define
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{rd}}:=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{e_{j},f_{j}\}_{n<j\leq m}
. If
\dim\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{bd}}=0
, we define
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{bd}}=\mathbf{0}
. If
\dim\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{bd}}=1
(resp. 2), then up to a
Q
-isometry that keeps Assumption
6.4.4
(A2)
, we can assume
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{bd}}=\{\overline{e_{n}}-\gamma_{n}\overline{f_{n}}\}
(resp.
\{\overline{e_{n-1}}-\gamma_{n-1}\overline{f_{n-1}},\overline{e_{n}}-\gamma_{n}\overline{f_{n}}\}
), and we define
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{bd}}=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\{e_{n}-\gamma_{n}f_{n}\}
(resp.
\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\{e_{n-1}-\gamma_{n-1}f_{n-1},e_{n}-\gamma_{n}f_{n}\}
), where by abuse of notation
\gamma_{n-1},\gamma_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
are Teichmuller lifts of
\gamma_{n-1},\gamma_{n}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
. Let
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{brd}}:=\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{bd}}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{rd}}
. For
0\leq i\leq n
, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{>i}=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\{e^{\prime},f^{\prime},{e}_{j},{f}_{j^{\prime}}\}_{i<j\leq n,1\leq j^{\prime}\leq n}.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\leq i}=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\{e^{\prime},f^{\prime},{f}_{j^{\prime}}\}_{1\leq j^{\prime}\leq i}.

Note that by definition, we have
\mathcal{L}_{>n}=\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\leq n}
. These lattices are closely related to the description in Proposition
6.4.8
.

Moreover, for each
j\geq 1
, define
H_{j}:=h_{P}(1+p^{a_{k}+1}+...+p^{(a_{k}+1)(j-1)})
and
H_{0}:=0
.

Finally, define
L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}
to be the
\mathbb{Z}
-lattice such that
L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{l}=L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{l}
for
l\neq p
, and

L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{brd}}+p^{j}\mathcal{L}_{>0}+p^{j+1}\mathcal{L},\,\,\,\,&r\in(H_{j},H_{j}+p^{(a_{k}+1)j}c_{1}],\\
&\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{brd}}+p^{j}\mathcal{L}_{>1}+p^{j+1}\mathcal{L},\,\,\,\,&r\in(H_{j}+p^{(a_{k}+1)j}c_{1},H_{j}+p^{(a_{k}+1)j}c_{2}],\\
&\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\vdots\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;,\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;&\vdots\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;,\\
&\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{brd}}+p^{j}\mathcal{L}_{>n-1}+p^{j+1}\mathcal{L},\,\,\,\,&r\in(H_{j}+p^{(a_{k}+1)j}c_{n-1},H_{j}+p^{(a_{k}+1)j}c_{n}],\\
&\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{brd}}+p^{j}\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\leq n}+p^{j+1}\mathcal{L},\,\,\,\,&r\in(H_{j}+p^{(a_{k}+1)j}c_{n},H_{j}+p^{(a_{k}+1)j}{d_{n}}],\\
&\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{brd}}+p^{j}\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\leq n-1}+p^{j+1}\mathcal{L},\,\,\,\,&r\in(H_{j}+p^{(a_{k}+1)j}d_{n},H_{j}+p^{(a_{k}+1)j}{d_{n-1}}],\\
&\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\vdots\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;,&\vdots\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;,\\
&\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{brd}}+p^{j}\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\leq 0}+p^{j+1}\mathcal{L},\,\,\,\,&r\in(H_{j}+p^{(a_{k}+1)j}d_{1},H_{j+1}].\\
\end{aligned}\right.

Remark 6.5.4
.

We have
L^{\prime}_{P,r}=L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}
for
r\leq c_{n}
. We also have
L^{\prime}_{P,r}=L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}
in the special case where all vectors in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{brd}}
never decay. Note that
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{brd}}
is saturated in
\mathcal{L}
with rank
2(m-n+1)\leq 2(m-a)
. The equality holds only in the thin case.

Remark 6.5.5
.

The chain of lattices
L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
decay in a pattern similar to Example
1.5.3
.

6.5.6.
Auxiliary lattices: the loose case

Assume that
a_{k}>a
. We will again construct a sequence of auxiliary lattices
L_{P,r}^{\prime\prime}
. First, define
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{brd}}
,
\mathcal{L}_{>i},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\leq i}
and
H_{j}
in the same way as §
6.5.3
. For
r\leq H_{1}=h_{P}
, we define
L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}
the same way as §
6.5.3
. We won’t need auxiliary lattices when
r>h_{a_{k}}
.

6.5.7.
Local density computation

Below we state a local density formula, using the following notation. Let
\mathsf{Q}
be the set of isomorphism classes of self-dual quadratic forms over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. Define
\chi:\mathsf{Q}\to\{-1,0,1\}
by setting
\chi=0
for odd-dimensional forms,
1
(resp.
-1
) for even-dimensional split (resp. nonsplit) forms. For
\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
, let
\mathbb{F}_{p}(\alpha)
denote
\mathbb{F}_{p}
equipped with the quadratic form
q(x)=\alpha x^{2}
. For
W\in\mathsf{Q}
, write
W(\alpha)=W\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}(\alpha)
.

Lemma 6.5.8
.

Let
\Lambda
be a self-dual quadratic lattice over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
M
a positive integer coprime to
p
. Write
\overline{\Lambda}
for
\Lambda\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
. Then

(58)

\delta(p,\Lambda,M)=\bigl(1-\chi(\overline{\Lambda})\,p^{-\mathrm{rk\,}(\Lambda)/2}\bigr)\bigl(1+\chi\bigl(\mathbb{F}_{p}(M)\oplus\overline{\Lambda}(-1)\bigr)\,p^{(1-\mathrm{rk\,}(\Lambda))/2}\bigr).

Proof.

Use the fact that
\delta(p,\Lambda,M)=p^{1-\mathrm{rk}\overline{\Lambda}}\#\{x\in\overline{\Lambda}|\overline{Q}(x)=M\}
and apply
[
Kit93
, Thm. 1.3.2]
.
∎

Now we are ready to compute the local density of
L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
(in both tight and loose cases).

Definition 6.5.9
.

Let
M
be coprime to
p
. For
0\leq i\leq n
, define
\delta_{i}=\delta(p,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{brd}}+\mathcal{L}_{>i},M)
and
\delta_{i}^{\prime}=\delta(p,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{brd}}+\mathcal{L}_{\leq i}^{\prime},M)
.

Remark 6.5.10
.

It is clear from the definition that
\delta(p,L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p},M)
equals
\delta_{0}
when
r\in(0,c_{1}]
, equals
\delta_{i}
when
r\in(c_{i},c_{i+1}]
and
i\in[1,n-1]
, equals
\delta_{n}^{\prime}
when
r\in(c_{n},d_{n}]
, equals
\delta_{j}^{\prime}
when
r\in(d_{j+1},d_{j}]
and
j\in[1,n-1]
, equals
\delta^{\prime}_{0}
when
r\in(d_{1},h_{a_{k}}]
. In the tight case, we furthermore have
\delta(p,L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p},M)=\delta^{\prime}_{0}
for all
r\in(d_{1},\infty)
.

Corollary 6.5.11
.

(1)

In borderline case 2,
\delta_{i}=1-p^{i-m}
for
i\in[0,n-1]
and
\delta_{j}^{\prime}=1+p^{n-m-1}
for
j\in[0,n]
.

(2)

In borderline case 1,
\delta_{i}=1-p^{i-m}
for
i\in[0,n-1]
and
\delta_{j}^{\prime}\leq 1+p^{n-m}
for
j\in[0,n]
.

(3)

In borderline case 0,
\delta_{n}=1-p^{i-m}
for
i\in[0,n-1]
and
\delta_{j}^{\prime}=1-p^{n-m}
for
j\in[0,n]
.

Proof.

Direct computation using Lemma
6.5.8
.
∎

6.5.12.
The mysterious quantity
D_{0}

Define the following quantity

D_{0}=d_{n}+p^{n-1}c_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}p^{n-i-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i}).

Later on, this quantity will appear mysteriously in various computations, but we don’t have a very good geometric explanation of it.

Lemma 6.5.13
.

In thin case, we have
D_{0}\leq h_{a_{k}}
. In thick case, we have

D_{0}+(p-1)(c_{n}-c_{1})+(p-1)d_{n}\leq p^{n-(a_{k}+1)}h_{a_{k}}.

Proof.

Recall that
h_{a_{k}}\geq p^{a_{k}}c_{1}+d_{1}
by (
57
). We can use (
55
) or the first inequality of
(\ref{eq:fatv2})
to get

d_{1}=d_{n}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(d_{i}-d_{i+1})\geq d_{n}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}p^{n-1-i}(c_{i+1}-c_{i}).

Using (
57
), we get

h_{a_{k}}\geq d_{n}+p^{a_{k}}c_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}p^{n-i-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i})

In the thin case, we have
a_{k}=n-1
. So
h\geq D_{0}
.
In the thick case, we have

\displaystyle p^{n-(a_{k}+1)}h_{a_{k}}

\displaystyle\geq p^{n-(a_{k}+1)}d_{n}+p^{n-1}c_{1}+p^{n-(a_{k}+1)}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}p^{n-i-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i})

\displaystyle\geq(p-1)d_{n}+d_{n}+p^{n-1}c_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}p^{n-i-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i})+(p-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i})

\displaystyle\geq(p-1)d_{n}+(p-1)(c_{n}-c_{1})+D_{0}.

∎

6.6.
Estimating
S^{\prime}(M)
: the tight case

In this section we assume
a_{k}=a
. We will compare
S^{\prime}(M)
to
S^{\prime\prime}(M)
and explicitly compute
S^{\prime\prime}(M)
, and use this to prove Theorem
6.0.1
in the tight case. Following Remark
3.2.4
, we can write
S^{\prime}(M)
as the following series (we will use
h_{a}
instead of
h_{P}
, the two quantities are equal; cf. Notation
5.2.1
):

S^{\prime}(M)=\frac{p^{a+1}-1}{h_{a}}\frac{1}{p(1+p^{-(m+1)})}\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{\delta(p,L^{\prime}_{P,r},M)}{|L^{\prime}_{P,1}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}:L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}|}.

We then define

S^{\prime\prime}(M):=\frac{p^{a+1}-1}{h_{a}}\frac{1}{p(1+p^{-(m+1)})}\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{\delta(p,L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r},M)}{[L^{\prime\prime}_{P,1}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}:L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}]},

where the auxiliary lattices
L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}
are as in §
6.5.3
. In the following we prove a partial generalization of Lemma
6.1.2
that compares
S^{\prime}(M)
to
S^{\prime\prime}(M)
:

Lemma 6.6.1
.

Notation as above. For all
M
coprime to
p
we have
S^{\prime}(M)\leq S^{\prime\prime}(M)
.

Proof.

The strategy is the same as Lemma
6.1.2
, and we use Proposition
6.4.8
as the main input. In the following, we show that for all
r\geq 1
,

(59)

\frac{\delta(p,L^{\prime}_{P,r},M)}{[L^{\prime}_{P,1}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}:L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}]}\leq\frac{\delta(p,L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r},M)}{[L^{\prime\prime}_{P,1}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}:L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}]}.

By Remark
6.5.4
, it suffices to treat the case where
r>c_{n}
. By the Decay Lemma
5.5.1
(also see Remark
5.3.7
), the
relevant lattice

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{rv}}:=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{{e^{\prime}},{f^{\prime}},{e_{j}},{f_{i}}\}_{1\leq i\leq n,1\leq j\leq n-\dim\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{bd}}}

decays rapidly to arbitrary order. So by construction we always have

(60)

[L^{\prime}_{P,1}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}:L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}]\geq[L^{\prime\prime}_{P,1}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}:L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}].

Suppose that (
60
) is a strict inequality. If
\delta(p,L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r},M)\neq 0
, then (
59
) holds and is a strict inequality; if
\delta(p,L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r},M)=0
(this happens only when
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{brd}}=\mathbf{0}
), then the isometrical embedding results in Proposition
6.4.8
imply that

\delta(p,L^{\prime}_{P,r},M)=0
, hence (
59
) holds and is an equality. Suppose that (
60
) is an equality, then the isometrical embedding in Proposition
6.4.8
is an isomorphism. It follows that
\delta(p,L^{\prime}_{P,r},M)=\delta(p,L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r},M)
. So (
59
) is an equality. As a result, (
59
) always holds, and we have
S^{\prime}(M)\leq S^{\prime\prime}(M)
.
∎

In the rest of this section we conduct a case by case study. Even though
a_{k}=a
, we will still keep the notation
a_{k}
. This is because some computations hold even when
a_{k}>a
, and will be reused in the loose situation. To ease the computation of
S^{\prime\prime}(M)
, we decompose it to two parts:

(61)

S^{\prime\prime}(M)=S^{\prime\prime}_{1}(M)+S^{\prime\prime}_{>1}(M),

where
S^{\prime\prime}_{1}(M)
is the sub-sum from
r=1
to
h_{a_{k}}
, while
S^{\prime\prime}_{>1}(M)
is the sub-sum from
r=h_{a_{k}}+1
to
\infty
.

6.6.2.
Borderline case 2

The case where
\dim\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{1,\mathrm{bd}}=2
is harder than others, since there are potentially more special endomorphisms that resist decay. We will treat this case first. Once this is done, the remaining cases are nothing but easier variants.

First we have
c_{n-1}=c_{n}=d_{n}=d_{n-1}
. We then define

\displaystyle D_{\delta}:=c_{1}\delta_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i}){p}^{-i}\delta_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(d_{i}-d_{i+1})p^{2+i-2n}\delta_{i}^{\prime}+(h_{a_{k}}-d_{1})p^{2-2n}\delta_{0}^{\prime},

\displaystyle D:=c_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i}){p}^{-i}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(d_{i}-d_{i+1})p^{2+i-2n}+(h_{a_{k}}-d_{1})p^{2-2n}.

Here
\delta_{i},\delta_{i}^{\prime}
are from Definition
6.5.9
. Then it is easy to see from Remark
6.5.10
that

(62)

\displaystyle S_{1}^{\prime\prime}(M)=\frac{p^{a+1}-1}{h_{a}}\frac{D_{\delta}}{p(1+p^{-{(m+1)}})}.

\displaystyle S_{>1}^{\prime\prime}(M)=\frac{p^{a+1}-1}{h_{a}}\frac{\delta_{0}^{\prime}D}{p(1+p^{-{(m+1)}})}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{p^{(a_{k}+1)j}}{p^{2nj}}.

Lemma 6.6.3
.

We have
D\leq p^{-a_{k}}h_{a_{k}}
and
D_{\delta}\leq p^{-a_{k}}h_{a_{k}}
.

Proof.

We first treat the thin case. Using the computation in Corollary
6.5.11
.

(63)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:thinv2})}}{{=}}c_{1}+c_{1}p^{2-2n+a_{k}}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i}){p}^{-i}+p^{1-n}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i})

(64)

\displaystyle=c_{1}+c_{1}p^{2-2n+a_{k}}+p^{1-n}(c_{n}-c_{1})+p^{1-n}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i}){p}^{n-i-1}

(65)

\displaystyle=p^{1-n}(c_{1}(p^{a_{k}+1-n}-1)+c_{n}+p^{n-1}c_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i}){p}^{n-i-1})

(66)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c_{n}=d_{n})}}{{=}}p^{1-n}(c_{1}(p^{a_{k}+1-n}-1)+D_{0})

(67)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(n=a_{k}+1)}}{{=}}p^{1-n}D_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Lemma~\ref{lm:D_0}}}}{{\leq}}p^{-a_{k}}h_{a_{k}}.

\displaystyle D_{\delta}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:thinv2})}}{{=}}c_{1}(1-p^{-m})+c_{1}p^{2-2n+a_{k}}(1+p^{n-m-1})+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i})(p^{-i}+p^{1-n})

\displaystyle=c_{1}(1-p^{-m})+c_{1}p^{2-2n+a_{k}}(1+p^{n-m-1})+p^{1-n}(c_{n}-c_{1})+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}p^{-i}(c_{i+1}-c_{i})

\displaystyle=c_{1}(p^{a_{k}+1-n-m}-p^{-m}+p^{2-2n+a_{k}}-p^{1-n})+c_{1}+p^{1-n}c_{n}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}p^{-i}(c_{i+1}-c_{i})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(n=a_{k}+1)}}{{=}}p^{1-n}D_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Lemma~\ref{lm:D_0}}}}{{\leq}}p^{-a_{k}}h_{a_{k}}.

Now we treat the thick case: the computation can be reduced to the thin case. To begin with, we have

(68)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:fatv2})}}{{\leq}}c_{1}+c_{1}p^{2-2n+a_{k}}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i}){p}^{-i}+p^{2-n}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i})

(69)

\displaystyle=c_{1}+c_{1}p^{2-2n+a_{k}}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i}){p}^{-i}+p^{1-n}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i})+p^{1-n}(p-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i}).

As compared to (
63
), we have
p^{2-n}
rather than
p^{1-n}
in the last summation of (
68
). But we can put it into the form as in (
69
), leaving the computation of the first half of (
69
) identical to (
64
)
\sim
(
66
). As a result, we get

(70)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle\leq p^{1-n}(c_{1}(p^{a_{k}+1-n}-1)+D_{0})+p^{1-n}(p-1)(c_{n}-c_{1})

(71)

\displaystyle\leq p^{1-n}(c_{1}(p^{a_{k}+1-n}-1)+(p-1)(c_{n}-c_{1})+D_{0})

(72)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(n>a_{k}+1)}}{{<}}p^{1-n}((p-1)(c_{n}-c_{1})+D_{0})

(73)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Lemma~\ref{lm:D_0}}}}{{\leq}}p^{-a_{k}}h_{a_{k}}.

Similarly strategy works for
D_{\delta}
in the thick case. We get

\displaystyle D_{\delta}

\displaystyle=c_{1}(p^{a_{k}+1-n-m}-p^{-m}+p^{2-2n+a_{k}}-p^{1-n})+p^{1-n}\left[D_{0}+(p-1)(1+p^{n-m-1})(c_{n}-c_{1})\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(n>a_{k}+1)}}{{<}}p^{1-n}\left[D_{0}+(p-1)(1+p^{n-m-1})(c_{n}-c_{1})\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(n\leq m)}}{{<}}p^{1-n}\left[D_{0}+2(p-1)(c_{n}-c_{1})\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Lemma~\ref{lm:D_0}}}}{{\leq}}p^{-a_{k}}h_{a_{k}}.

∎

Remark 6.6.4
.

Lemma
6.6.3
still holds when
a<a_{k}
, and will also be used in the easy situation.

Now plug lemma
6.6.3
into (
62
), and equate
a_{k}=a
. We obtain

(74)

\displaystyle S^{\prime\prime}(M)=S_{1}^{\prime\prime}(M)+S_{>1}^{\prime\prime}(M)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1-p^{-a-1}}{1+p^{-{(m+1)}}}\left(1+\frac{1+p^{n-m-1}}{p^{2n-a-1}-1}\right)

(75)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{(p^{n}-p^{a+1})(p^{a+m+1}+p^{a+n}+p^{a}+p^{m+n})}{p^{a}(p^{m+1}+1)(p^{2n}-p^{a+1})}.

In the thick case, (
75
) is strictly smaller than
1
. By Lemma
6.6.1
, we get
S^{\prime}(M)\leq S^{\prime\prime}(M)<1
. In the thin case, (
75
) equals
1
. Therefore we need the following extra input (which is a partial generalization of the second half of Lemma
6.1.2
):

Lemma 6.6.5
.

Suppose that we are in the tight thin borderline case 2, and that
\mathcal{A}_{C^{/P}}
does not admit a rank
2(m-a)
saturated lattice of formal special endomorphisms. Then
S^{\prime}(M)<S^{\prime\prime}(M)
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Lemma
6.1.2
. In the tight thin borderline case 2,
\delta(p,L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r},M)\neq 0
. So if (
60
) is a strict inequality, then so is (
59
). Therefore
S^{\prime}(M)=S^{\prime\prime}(M)
implies that (
60
) is an equality for all
r
. It then follows from Proposition
6.4.8
that for
r>h_{a_{k}}
, that the projection of
\overline{L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{brd}}\oplus\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{\overline{e^{\prime}},\overline{f^{\prime}},\overline{f_{1}},\overline{f_{2}},...,\overline{f_{n}}\}
to
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{brd}}
is an isometric isomorphism. In particular,
\overline{L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
does not depend on
r
. We then pick
2(m-a)
vectors in
\mathcal{L}
whose reductions form a basis of
\overline{L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
. A limit argument as in Lemma
6.1.2
yields a rank
2(m-a)
saturated lattice that is contained every
\overline{L^{\prime}_{P,r}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
. Therefore
\mathcal{A}_{C^{/P}}
admits a rank
2(m-a)
saturated lattice of formal special endomorphisms. This contradiction shows that we must have
S^{\prime}(M)<S^{\prime\prime}(M)
.
∎

The following consequence is immediate:

Corollary 6.6.6
.

Theorem
6.0.1
holds in the tight borderline case 2.

6.6.7.
Borderline case 1

Suppose that
\dim\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{1,\mathrm{bd}}=1
. We then have
c_{n}=d_{n}
. Define

\displaystyle D_{\delta}=c_{1}\delta_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i}){p}^{-i}\delta_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(d_{i}-d_{i+1})p^{1+i-2n}\delta_{i}^{\prime}+(h_{a_{k}}-d_{1})p^{1-2n}\delta_{0}^{\prime},

\displaystyle D=c_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i})p^{-i}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(d_{i}-d_{i+1})p^{1+i-2n}+(h_{a_{k}}-d_{1})p^{1-2n}.

Then
S_{1}^{\prime\prime}(M)
and
S_{>1}^{\prime\prime}(M)
can still be computed by the formula (
62
) (with the meaning of
D_{\delta}
and
D
replaced by the new definition given above).

Lemma 6.6.8
.

D<p^{-a_{k}}h_{a_{k}}
and
D_{\delta}<p^{-a_{k}}h_{a_{k}}
.

Proof.

The computation is completely similar to the proof of Lemma
6.6.3
. Of course one can directly do the dirty computation, but we can also use the computation already done in Lemma
6.6.3
to relive the burden. In fact, in the computation of Lemma
6.6.3
, we only treated
c_{i}
and
d_{i}
as symbols and didn’t use specific properties of them, except in (
66
) we have used the fact
c_{n}=d_{n}
(which is also true in this case). Let’s now do the thin case: note that

\displaystyle D<c_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i}){p}^{-i}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(d_{i}-d_{i+1})p^{2+i-2n}+(h_{a_{k}}-d_{1})p^{2-2n}.

But according to the computation (
63
)
\sim
(
67
), the RHS equals
p^{1-n}D_{0}
. It follows immediately from Lemma
6.5.13
that
D<p^{-a_{k}}h_{a_{k}}
. Similarly,

D_{\delta}=c_{1}\delta_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i}){p}^{-i}\delta_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(d_{i}-d_{i+1})p^{2+i-2n}(p^{-1}+p^{n-m-1})+(h_{a_{k}}-d_{1})p^{2-2n}(p^{-1}+p^{n-m-1}),

and one verifies that the RHS is smaller than the
D_{\delta}
computed in Lemma
6.6.3
. So we immediately get
D_{\delta}<p^{-a_{k}}h_{a_{k}}
as well.
The thick case can be treated similarly.
∎

Use Lemma
6.6.8
and equate
a_{k}=a
. We have

\displaystyle S^{\prime\prime}(M)

\displaystyle=S_{1}^{\prime\prime}(M)+S_{>1}^{\prime\prime}(M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(n\geq a+1)}}{{<}}\frac{1-p^{-a-1}}{1+p^{-{(m+1)}}}\left(1+\frac{2}{p^{a+2}-1}\right)<1.

Corollary 6.6.9
.

Theorem
6.0.1
holds in the tight borderline case 1.

6.6.10.
Borderline case 0

Define

\displaystyle D_{\delta}=c_{1}\delta_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i}){p}^{-i}\delta_{i}+(d_{n}-c_{n})p^{-n}\delta_{n}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(d_{i}-d_{i+1})p^{i-2n}\delta_{i}^{\prime}+(h_{a_{k}}-d_{1})p^{-2n}\delta_{0}^{\prime},

\displaystyle D=c_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i})p^{-i}+(d_{n}-c_{n})p^{-n}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(d_{i}-d_{i+1})p^{i-2n}+(h_{a_{k}}-d_{1})p^{-2n}.

Then
S_{1}^{\prime\prime}(M)
and
S_{>1}^{\prime\prime}(M)
can still be computed by the formula (
62
) (with the meaning of
D_{\delta}
and
D
replaced by the new definition given above).

Lemma 6.6.11
.

D<p^{-a_{k}}h_{a_{k}}
and
D_{\delta}<p^{-a_{k}}h_{a_{k}}
.

Proof.

This can be dealt with the strategy explained in Lemma
6.6.8
, i.e., reduce to the computation already done in Lemma
6.6.3
. For the thin case, we have

\displaystyle D<c_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i}){p}^{-i}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(d_{i}-d_{i+1})p^{2+i-2n}+(h_{a_{k}}-d_{1})p^{2-2n}+(d_{n}-c_{n})p^{-n}.

According to the computation (
63
)
\sim
(
65
) (note that we cannot use (
66
) since
c_{n}
may not be equal to
d_{n}
in this case), the RHS equals

\displaystyle p^{1-n}(c_{n}+p^{n-1}c_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i}){p}^{n-i-1})+(d_{n}-c_{n})p^{-n}

\displaystyle<p^{1-n}(d_{n}+p^{n-1}c_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(c_{i+1}-c_{i}){p}^{n-i-1})=p^{1-n}D_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Lemma~\ref{lm:D_0}}}}{{\leq}}p^{1-n}h_{a_{k}}.

The computation for
D_{\delta}
, as well as the thick case can be dealt in the same way.
∎

Use Lemma
6.6.11
and equate
a_{k}=a
, we find that

\displaystyle S^{\prime\prime}(M)=S_{1}^{\prime\prime}(M)+S_{>1}^{\prime\prime}(M)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(n\geq a+1)}}{{<}}\frac{1-p^{-a-1}}{1+p^{-(m+1)}}\left(1+\frac{2}{p^{a+3}-1}\right)<1.

Corollary 6.6.12
.

Theorem
6.0.1
holds in the tight borderline case 0.

6.7.
Estimating
S^{\prime}(M)
: the loose case

Suppose that
a_{k}>a
. As compared to the tight situation, this is much easier, and we can treat all borderline cases all at once.

We will make the decomposition
S^{\prime}(M)=S^{\prime}_{1}(M)+S^{\prime}_{>1}(M)
similar to (
61
), where
S^{\prime}_{1}(M)
is the sub-sum of
S^{\prime}(M)
from
r=1
to
h_{a_{k}}
, while
S^{\prime}_{>1}(M)
is the sub-sum from
r=h_{a_{k}}+1
to
\infty
. On the other hand, since
L^{\prime\prime}_{P,r}
is only defined for
r\leq h_{a_{k}}
, we can only make sense of
S^{\prime\prime}_{1}(M)
.

Lemma 6.7.1
.

Notation as above. For all
M
coprime to
p
we have
S^{\prime}_{1}(M)\leq S^{\prime\prime}_{1}(M)
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to Lemma
6.6.1
.
∎

Define the quantity
D_{\delta}
exactly the same way as in the tight situation. As noted in Remark
6.6.4
, we still have the bound
D_{\delta}\leq p^{-a_{k}}h_{a_{k}}
(in all thin and tight and all borderline cases). Note that
h_{a}\geq h_{a_{k}}
. The same computation as in §
6.6
shows that in all cases we have

S^{\prime}_{1}(M)\leq S_{1}^{\prime\prime}(M)\leq\frac{p^{a+1}-1}{h_{a}}\frac{D_{\delta}}{p(1+p^{-{(m+1)}})}\leq\frac{p^{a-a_{k}}-p^{-a_{k}-1}}{1+p^{-{(m+1)}}}\frac{h_{a_{k}}}{h_{a}}<\frac{p^{-1}}{1+p^{-{(m+1)}}}.

On the other hand, it is easy to estimate
S_{>1}^{\prime}(M)
: one can just use the coarsest bounds. By Theorem
5.5.1
, we have a rank
a+a_{k}+2
saturated sublattice of rapid decay. Each primitive vector in this sublattice can at most lift to
h_{a_{k}}
. Upon using the coarsest upper bound for the local density (which is
2
), we find that

S_{>1}^{\prime}(M)\leq\frac{p^{a+1}-1}{h_{a}}\frac{2}{p(1+p^{-(m+1)})}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{p^{(a_{k}+1)j}h_{a_{k}}}{p^{(a+a_{k}+2)j}}\leq\frac{2p^{-1}}{1+p^{-(m+1)}}.

As a result, we have

S^{\prime}(M)=S_{1}^{\prime}(M)+S_{>1}^{\prime}(M)<\frac{3p^{-1}}{1+p^{-(m+1)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(p\geq 3)}}{{<}}1.

Proof of Theorem
6.0.1
.

The above paragraph treats the loose case. The tight case is established in Corollaries
6.6.6
,
6.6.9
and
6.6.12
.
∎

7.
Decay of special endomorphisms: supersingular but not superspecial

We closely follow the exposition and notation in
[
MST22a
, Sections 4 and 5]
. The computations are similar to the ones in
loc. cit.
and the ones carried out in the superspecial case above, and so we will be content with only providing an outline of the calculations.

We work at a supersingular point
P
. Set
n=\frac{t_{P}}{2}
and
m=(b-2n)/2
. To ease exposition, we set
n^{\prime}=n-1
. Note that the case
n^{\prime}=0
is precisely the superspecial case, and so we have that
n^{\prime}>0
. As recalled in
Section

4.1
, we have a decomposition of
W
-lattices
\mathbb{L}=\mathbb{L}_{0}\oplus\mathbb{L}_{1}
and hence a decomposition of their Tate modules
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{1}
. Recall that
\mathcal{L}_{1}
is self-dual over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, so
\mathrm{rank}_{W}\mathbb{L}_{0}=\mathrm{rank}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{L}_{0}=2n
and
\mathrm{rank}_{W}\mathbb{L}_{1}=\mathrm{rank}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{L}_{1}=2m
. In order to keep notation consistent within our paper, we diverge from the notation in
[
MST22a
]
and make the following changes.

Table 1
.
Changes in notation between current paper and
[
MST22a
]

Object

Notation in
[
MST22a
]

Notation in current paper

Basis for
\mathcal{L}_{0}

\{e_{i},f_{i}:1\leq i\leq n\}

\{e^{\prime}_{i},f^{\prime}_{i}:1\leq i\leq n\}

Basis for
\mathcal{L}_{1}

\{e^{\prime}_{j},f^{\prime}_{j}:1\leq j\leq m\}

\{e_{j},f_{j}:1\leq j\leq m\}

Basis for
\mathbb{L}_{0}

\{v_{i},w_{i}:1\leq i\leq n\}

\{v^{\prime}_{i},w^{\prime}_{i}:1\leq i\leq n\}

Coordinates (1st set)

\{x_{i},y_{i}:1\leq i\leq n^{\prime}\}

\{x^{\prime}_{i},y^{\prime}_{i}:1\leq i\leq n^{\prime}\}

Coordinates (2nd set)

\{x^{\prime}_{j},y^{\prime}_{j}:1\leq j\leq m\}

\{x_{j},y_{j}:1\leq j\leq m\}

Function
Q_{i},0\leq i\leq n^{\prime}-1

y_{i+1}

y^{\prime}_{i+1}=Q_{i}

Function
Q_{n^{\prime}}

y_{n}

Q=\sum_{i=1}^{n^{\prime}}x^{\prime}_{i}y^{\prime}_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}x_{i}y_{i}=Q_{n^{\prime}}

Function
Q_{n^{\prime}+a}

(a>0)

y_{n+1},y_{n+2},\dots

Q_{n^{\prime}+a}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}(x_{i}y_{i}^{p^{a}}+x_{i}^{p^{a}}y_{i})

Note that
\{e_{j},f_{j}:1\leq j\leq m\}
is a basis for
\mathbb{L}_{1}
as well, and the basis
\{v^{\prime}_{i},w^{\prime}_{i}:1\leq i\leq n\}
for
\mathbb{L}_{0}
is provided by
[
MST22a
, Lem. 4.5]
. Again let
R:=W[\![x^{\prime}_{i},y^{\prime}_{i},x_{j},y_{j}]\!]_{1\leq i\leq n^{\prime},1\leq j\leq m}
be the structure ring of
\mathscr{S}^{/P}_{W}
. The Frobenius operator
\Frob
on
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}(R)\simeq\mathbb{L}\otimes R
is given by
(uB)\circ\sigma
with respect to
\{v^{\prime}_{i},w^{\prime}_{i},e_{j},f_{j}\}
, where
u
is a matrix with entries in
R
and
B
is a matrix with entries in
\frac{1}{p}W\subset w[1/p]
. The general formulas for
u
and
B
for a supersingular
P
that extends
Eq.

14
in §
4.2.1
for the superspecial case is spelled out in
[
MST22a
, §4.8]
.

Combining these formulae with the argument of
Proposition

4.2.3
, we find that the local equations of the newton strata through
P
are defined by vanishing of the
Q_{i}
’s.

Proposition 7.0.1

(Ogus)
.

(a)

For
0\leq i<m+n^{\prime}-1
, the Newton stratum of height
i+2
is defined by the vanishing of the equations
Q_{0},\ldots Q_{i}
.

(b)

The supersingular stratum is defined by the vanishing of the equations
Q_{0},Q_{1},\ldots Q_{n^{\prime}+m-1}
.

(c)

For
i\geq n^{\prime}+m
, we have
Q_{i}
is in the ideal generated by
Q_{0}\ldots Q_{n^{\prime}+m-1}
.

We will recall notation for future use. Let
A
(resp.
B
,
D
) denote the top left
2n\times 2n
(resp. top right
2n\times 2m
, bottom left
2m\times 2n
) block of
u^{\prime}-I
where
u^{\prime}
is as in
[
MST22a
, Section 4.11]
. As in
[
MST22a
, Section 4.6]
, we let
S_{0}
be the change-of-coordinates matrix from
\{e^{\prime}_{i},f^{\prime}_{i}\}
to
\{v^{\prime}_{i},w^{\prime}_{i}\}
, i.e. the first (resp. last)
n
columns of
S_{0}
are the coordinates of the
v^{\prime}_{i}
(resp.
w^{\prime}_{i}
) expressed in terms of the
e^{\prime}_{i},f^{\prime}_{i}
. As in
loc. cit.
, there is a unique matrix
S^{\prime}_{0}\in\textrm{GL}_{2n}(W)
such that

S_{0}=S^{\prime}_{0}\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}p^{-1}I&0\\
\hline\cr 0&I\end{array}\right].

7.1.
Setup with local curve

We will assume that
Q_{0}(t),\ldots Q_{a-1}(t)=0
and that
Q_{a}(t)
is the first non-zero function of
t
. We refer to
[
MST22a
, Section 5.7]
for the definition of the matrices
K_{i}
. The matrices
K_{i}
are defined for
i=1,\ldots n+1
in
loc. cit.
. We define
K_{i}
for every
i
. Indeed, set
A_{n+i}=CD^{(i)}
. This is the
2n\times 2n
matrix which is zero everywhere except for the
(n,2n)
-entry, and this entry is
p^{-1}Q_{n^{\prime}+i}
. Define
K_{n+i}=S^{\prime}_{0}A_{n+i}({S^{\prime}}_{0}^{-1})^{(i)}
. We note that
[
MST22a
, Lemma 5.9 (2)]
is true for
K_{i}
with values of
i
greater than
n+1
as well, and the same proof goes through. The discussion immediately after
loc. cit.
also holds for arbitrary
i
. We set
h_{i}:=v_{t}(Q_{i}(t))
.

Remark 7.1.1
.

The valuations of these functions were denoted by
a_{i}
in
[
MST22a
]
.

Note that we have
h_{i}=\infty
for
i<a
. Let
a_{1}=a<a_{2}\ldots<a_{k}
be the maximal sequence of integers such that
a_{k}\leq n+m-1
, and
h_{a_{1}}\geq h_{a_{2}}\ldots\geq h_{a_{k}}
. There are three main cases.

(1)

a_{1}<n^{\prime},a_{k}\leq n^{\prime}
.

(2)

n^{\prime}\leq a_{1}
.

(3)

a_{1}<n^{\prime}<a_{k}
.

Cases 2 and 3 are treated identically to establish rapid decay of a subspace having rank at least
2a+2
, and Cases 1 and 3 are treated identically to prove Theorem
3.2.3
. We will therefore only analyze Cases 1 and 2, and only briefly comment on Case 3.

7.2.
Rapid decay

Case 1

In this case, we will show that a sufficiently large-rank sublattice of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
decays, and this will be sufficient for our purposes.

Definition 7.2.1
.

We say that a primitive vector
v\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
decays rapidly if
p^{r}v
does not lift to an endomorphism modulo
t^{1+h_{a}(1+p^{a+1}+p^{2(a+1)}+\ldots p^{r(a+1)})}
. We say that a saturated sublattice of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
decays rapidly if every vector in that sublattice decays rapidly.

Theorem 7.2.2
.

There is a saturated sublattice of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
having rank
2a_{1}+2
that decays rapidly.

7.2.3.
Proof of rapid decay in Case 1

The treatment of rapid decay for Case 1 is almost identical to the analysis carried out in
[
MST22a
, Section 5]
. Indeed, it suffices to solely work with
K_{i}
with
a_{1}+1\leq i\leq a_{k}+1
. The results in
loc. cit.
through Lemma 5.12 are just general and therefore hold. Lemma 5.13 of
loc. cit.
has the following strengthening.

Lemma 7.2.4
.

Let
\alpha_{a_{1}}\ldots\alpha_{a_{k}}\in\mathbb{F}
be not all zero. Consider the linear combination
\bar{R}=\sum_{i=a_{1}}^{a_{k}}\alpha_{i}\sigma^{i}(\overline{{R}_{n+1}})
. Then
\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\{v\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{2n}:\bar{R}v=0\}\leq a_{k}-a_{1}
.

The proof is the same.

Now, the proof of the decay lemma goes through exactly as in Theorem 5.2 and Section 5.14 of
[
MST22a
]
. We note that 5.14 requires
a_{k}\leq n^{\prime}
– indeed, without this assumption, one must a-priori work with
K_{i}
for all values of
i
bounded above by
n^{\prime}+m-1
(and this is precisely what we do in Cases 2 and 3). Therefore,
[
MST22a
, Theorem 5.15]
is only true in the setting of this case. However, the proof of
[
MST22a
, Theorem 5.2]
together with Lemma
7.2.4
in place of
[
MST22a
, Lemma 5.13]
actually shows that a saturated sub-lattice of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
having rank at least
2n-(a_{k}-a_{1})
decays rapidly. We have that
2n-(a_{k}-a_{1})\geq 2a_{1}+2
(with equality holding only when
a_{k}=a_{1}=n^{\prime}
).

Cases 2 and 3

We will work in the setting of Case 2. We again note that the argument for rapid decay that we outline holds verbatim for Case 3.

The setup with words, valuations, etc is the same as in the superspecial case. Specifically, the definitions, lemmas, propositions, and proofs from Section
5.3
all hold with no change.

The results regarding isotropic vectors in
\mathcal{L}_{1}
also hold with little change in the statements and no change in the proofs. Indeed, we have the following results.

Lemma 7.2.5
.

The number of distinct
i
such that
\nu_{t}(x_{i})\geq h_{a_{k}}
is at most
m-1-(a_{k}-n^{\prime})=m+n-2-a_{k}
.

The proof is identical to the proof of Lemma
5.4.1
.

Corollary 7.2.6
.

(a)

Let
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{L}
be a saturated sub-lattice having rank
m-(a_{k}-n^{\prime})=m+n-1-a_{k}
such that
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\bmod p\subset\mathcal{L}_{1}\bmod p
, and such that
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\bmod p
is isotropic. Then there is at least one primitive vector that decays rapidly to first order. In other words, there is a primitive
v\in\mathcal{L}
such that
v_{t}(x_{v})<h_{a_{k}}
.

(b)

There exists a saturated sublattice
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{L}_{1}
having rank
2(a_{k}-n^{\prime})
such that every primitive vector decays rapidly to first order.

The proof is idential to the proof of Corollary
5.4.2

We are now ready to outline the proof of the decay lemma in this setting.

Proof.

The setup and notation is identical, and we have the exact analogues of Claims
5.5.2
and
5.5.3
. The proof of Claim
5.5.2
goes through in this setting. The setting of Claim
5.5.3
in the superspecial setting is somewhat degenerate. But the analysis in
[
MST22a
, Proof of Theorem 5.2]
goes through to show the existence of a saturated sublattice of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
with rank
2n-T_{r}(a_{h_{k}})
that decays rapidly. Indeed, in the notation of
loc. cit.
,
T_{r}(a_{h_{k}})
is just
\mu_{J}-\mu_{J}
, which is the only thing required.

All of this proves the existence of a saturated sublattice of
\mathcal{L}
having rank
2n+2(a_{k}-n^{\prime})-(a_{k}-a_{1})=2a_{k}+2-(a_{k}-a_{1})\geq 2a_{1}+2
that decays rapidly to
r
th order. An argument identical to the one used in the superspecial case applies to deduce rapid-decay to all orders.
∎

7.3.
Local-global comparison

7.3.1.
Cases 1 and 3

The analysis in Cases 1 and 3 to compare the local and global Eisenstein series is identical. Indeed, there is no first-order decay analysis required. We are now ready to prove Theorem
3.2.3
, which we restate for the reader’s convenience.

Theorem 7.3.2
.

Let
P\in C(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
be a supersingular point. Suppose that we are in the setting of Case 1 or 3, i.e. that
a\leq n-1
. Then, there is a positive real number
\alpha<1
such that for every integer
M
that is relatively prime to
p
and is represented by
L
, we have
S^{\prime}(M)=\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{q_{L_{r}^{\prime}}(M)}{g_{P}(M)}<\alpha
.

Remark 7.3.3
.

The local and global contribution at a supersingular point is effected both by the generic newton stratum of the curve and the Artin invariant of the supersingular point. The
p
-power part of the discriminant of the lattice
\mathcal{L}
grows as the Artin invariant grows. On the other hand, we need faster rates of decay when the generic Newton stratum is close to being supersingular. When
a<n^{\prime}
, the saving we obtain from the Artin invariant is large enough that it suffices to prove that a sufficiently large-rank lattice decays rapidly without requiring a first-order analysis. We do not require an algebraicity input either, as the numerics dictate that the formal curve cannot sit inside a formal exceptional locus. Indeed, being contained inside a formal exceptional locus would require that
C
admits formal special endomorphisms by a self-dual rank
2m+2n-(2a-2)
lattice. However, any such lattice must be contained in
\mathcal{L}_{1}
, which has rank
2m
which is strictly smaller than
2m+2n-(2a-2)
by our assumptions on
a
.

Proof.

By the decay lemma in this case, we have that
\sqrt{|(L_{r}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\vee}/(L_{r}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{p})|}\geq p^{n}
if
r\leq h_{a}
,
\geq p^{n+2a+2}
if
h_{a}<r\leq h_{a}(p^{a+1}+1)
,
\geq p^{r+4a}
if
h_{a}(p^{a+1}+1)<r\leq h_{a}(p^{2a+2}+p^{a+1}+1)
, etc. Using the coarse estimate of
[
MST22a
, Lemma 7.16]
, we then obtain

\displaystyle\frac{h_{a}}{p^{a+1}-1}\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{q_{L_{r}^{\prime}}(M)}{g_{P}(M)}

\displaystyle\leq(\frac{2}{1-p^{(-b-2)/2}})(\frac{h_{a}(1+1/p)}{p^{n}}+\frac{h_{a}p^{a+1}}{p^{2a+2+n}}+\frac{h_{a}p^{2a+2}}{p^{4a+4+n}}+\cdots)

\displaystyle=\frac{2h_{a}}{p^{n}(1-p^{(-b-2)/2})}(1/p+\frac{1}{1-p^{-(a+1)}}).

As we are in the non-ordinary locus, we have
a>0
. Substituting the coarse bound
3/2>\dfrac{1}{1-3^{-2}}\geq\dfrac{1}{1-p^{-(a+1)}}
, we obtain a strict upper bound of
\dfrac{3h_{a}}{p^{n}(1-p^{-n})}
. It suffices to prove that

\frac{3h_{a}}{p^{n}(1-p^{-n})}\leq\frac{h_{a}}{p^{a+1}-1}\Leftrightarrow\frac{3}{p^{n}-1}\leq\frac{1}{p^{a+1}-1}\Leftrightarrow 3p^{a+1}-3\leq p^{n}-1.

This follows directly from the fact that
a<n^{\prime}
, or
a+1<n
.
∎

7.3.4.
Case 2

The first order analysis in the superspecial case goes through verbatim in this setting. Indeed, the case that does not follow immediately from the arguments used in Cases 1 and 3 is when
a_{k}=a_{1}\geq n^{\prime}
. In this setting, the sublattice
\mathcal{L}_{0}
decays rapidly. The analysis of first-order decay for vectors in
\mathcal{L}_{1}
is identical to the superspecial case. The analysis in Section
6
yields the following result.

Theorem 7.3.5
.

Let
C
be as in Section
7.1
and let
P
be the closed point of
C
. If
\mathcal{A}_{C^{/P}}
does not admit a saturated lattice of formal special endomorphisms of rank
2(m-a)
, then there exists a constant
\alpha<1
, depending only on
C
and
P
, such that for
M\in\mathbb{N}
coprime to
p
, we have
S^{\prime}(M)=\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{{q_{L_{r,P}^{\prime}}(M)}}{g_{P}(M)}<\alpha.

8.
The algebraization theorem

The main result of this section is to finish the proof of Theorem
3.2.3
. We recall the setting. We have a supersingular point
P\in C
, with local parameter
t
. Let
k[\![t]\!]
denote the complete local ring of
C
at
P
. We have shown the existence of
\alpha<1
such that the conclusion of Theorem
3.2.3
holds
unless
the module of formal special endomorphisms at
k[\![t]\!]
has rank
b-2a
. Recall that the Decay Lemma (Theorem
5.5.1
, Theorem
7.2.2
) implies that the module of formal special endomorphisms at
k[\![t]\!]
has rank at most
b-2a
. We will now prove that under the assumptions of Theorem
1.4.1
, the module of formal special endomorphisms at
k[\![t]\!]
has rank strictly less than
b-2a
.

Remark 8.0.1
.

For the rest of the paper,
m
will index special divisors.

Remark 8.0.2
.

We note that in the inert case (see
4.1
for the definition of the inert case), the quantity
b-2a
is zero if our Newton stratum contains the supersingular locus as a divisor. Indeed, in this setting, we necessarily have

\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{q_{L^{\prime}_{P,r}}(m)}{g_{P}(m)}=1.

This equality is of course expected, as we know that the reduced locus of the intersection of every special divisors with this Newton stratum is contained in the supersingular locus.

We now introduce our setup before stating our result.

Setup 8.0.3
.

Let
U
be a connected smooth affine curve over
\mathbb{F}
with a nonconstant morphism
\iota:U\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{a+1,\mathbb{F}}\setminus\mathscr{S}_{a+2,\mathbb{F}}
, i.e., the image of
U
lies in the height
a+1
but not height
a+2
Newton stratum. Let
\iota_{0}:U_{0}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{a+1,\mathbb{F}_{q}}\setminus\mathscr{S}_{a+2,\mathbb{F}_{q}}
be a model of
\iota
over a sufficiently large finite field, i.e., a map defined
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
whose base change to
\mathbb{F}
is
\iota
.

Since
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris},U_{0}}
has constant Newton polygon,
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris},U_{0}}
admits a slope filtration in the category of
F
-crystals over
U_{0}
:

(76)

0=\Fil_{-2}\subseteq\Fil_{-1}\subseteq\Fil_{0}\subseteq\Fil_{1}=\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris},U_{0}},

where
\Fil_{-1}
is the slope
-1/(a+1)
part,
\Fil_{0}
is the slope
\leq 0
part. The rank of
\mathrm{gr}_{-1},\mathrm{gr}_{0}
and
\mathrm{gr}_{1}
are
a+1
,
b-2a
and
a+1
, respectively.

Formal Brauer groups

Note that the
F
-crystal
\mathbb{L}_{\textrm{EB}}:=\Fil_{0}/\Fil_{-1}
is a Dieudonné crystal, the main result of
[
dJ95
]
implies that
\mathbb{L}_{\textrm{EB}}=\mathbb{D}(\mathbf{\Psi})
where
\mathbb{D}
is the contravariant crystalline Dieudonné functor and
\mathbf{\Psi}
is a
p
-divisible group over
U_{0}
, called the
enlarged formal Brauer group
. For a K3 surface over finite field, this recovers the enlarged formal Brauer group introduced in
[
AM77
, §IV]
and
[
NO85b
, §3]
, and the connected subgroup of this group is just the classical formal Brauer group associated to the K3 surface.

Theorem 8.0.4
.

Let
U
be in Setup
8.0.3
. Let
D
be the function field of
U
and
E
be the completion of
D
at a place (not necessarily a place corresponding to a point in
U
). Suppose that

\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris},E}
admits a rank
b-2a
lattice of formal special endomorphisms. Then
\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{A}_{D})\supsetneq\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{A})
, where
\mathcal{A}
is the universal Kuga–Satake abelian scheme.

Further, if the quadratic form on this lattice of formal special endomorphisms is self-dual, then the slope filtration of the enlarged formal Brauer group over
D
(and in fact, over
U
) splits.

Given the results of Sections
5
,
6
and
7
, Theorem
3.2.3
(and therefore Theorem
1.4.1
) follows directly from Theorem
8.0.4
.

8.1.
Splittings

Let
X
be a
U_{0}
-scheme, we say that
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris},X}

splits in the middle
, if

(77)

\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris},X}\simeq\mathrm{gr}_{0,X}\oplus\mathscr{E}_{X},

where
\mathscr{E}_{X}
is an extension of
\Fil_{-1,X}
by
\mathrm{gr}_{1,X}
. Rationally, we also say that
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris},X}[p^{-1}]

splits in the middle
, if (
77
) holds after up to inverting
p
.

Note that the
p
-divisible group
\mathbf{\Psi}
sits inside the classical connected-étale sequence

(78)

1\rightarrow\mathbf{\Psi}_{\text{con}}\rightarrow\mathbf{\Psi}\rightarrow\mathbf{\Psi}_{\text{ét}}\rightarrow 1.

Applying
\mathbb{D}(-)
to (
78
), we recover

(79)

0\rightarrow\mathrm{gr}_{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{L}_{\textrm{EB}}\rightarrow\mathrm{gr}_{1}\rightarrow 0.

Lemma 8.1.1
.

Suppose that
X
is a regular excellent scheme. The following are equivalent:

(1)

\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris},X}
splits in the middle,

(2)

the projection
\Fil_{0,X}\rightarrow\mathrm{gr}_{0,X}
admits a section,

(3)

the embedding
\mathrm{gr}_{0,X}\rightarrow\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{EB},X}
admits a retraction,

(4)

\mathbf{\Psi}_{X}
splits as
\mathbf{\Psi}_{\mathrm{con},X}\times\mathbf{\Psi}_{\text{é}\mathrm{t},X}
.

Proof.

The equivalence of (1), (2), (3) follows easily from the existence of quadratic pairing on
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris},X}
. The equivalence of (3) and (4) follows from the fully-faithfulness of the crystalline Dieudonné functor; cf.
[
DM99
, Theorem 4.6]
.
∎

Remark 8.1.2
.

The above lemma has an obvious rational version, i.e.,
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris},X}[p^{-1}]
splits in the middle if and only if
\Fil_{0,X}[p^{-1}]\rightarrow\mathrm{gr}_{0,X}[p^{-1}]
admits a section, if and only if
\mathrm{gr}_{0,X}[p^{-1}]\rightarrow\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{EB},X}[p^{-1}]
admits a retraction, if and only if
\mathbf{\Psi}_{X}
splits as
\mathbf{\Psi}_{\mathrm{con},X}\times\mathbf{\Psi}_{\text{é}\mathrm{t},X}
up to isogeny. The proof is similar.

Construction 8.1.3
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris},X}[p^{-1}]
splits in the middle, we can construct an endomorphism of
\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{cris},X}[p^{-1}]
which is canonical up to scaling. Let’s recall how to do this: since
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris},X}[p^{-1}]
splits, we have

\det\mathrm{gr}_{0,X}[p^{-1}]=\bigwedge^{b-2a}\mathrm{gr}_{0,X}[p^{-1}]\hookrightarrow\bigwedge^{b-2a}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris},X}[p^{-1}].

Now
\mathrm{gr}_{0,X}[p^{-1}]
is self-dual (due to the quadratic pairing), we have
\det\mathrm{gr}_{0,X}[p^{-1}]=\mathbbm{1}_{X}
, the constant object. By Remark
2.1.1
, we have

\mathbbm{1}_{X}\hookrightarrow\bigwedge^{b-2a}\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris},X}[p^{-1}]\hookrightarrow\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{cris},X}[p^{-1}]).

So any global section of
\mathbbm{1}_{X}
gives rise to a global section of
\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{cris},X}[p^{-1}])
. This is equivalent to saying that
\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{cris},X}[p^{-1}]
admits an endomorphism corresponding to that global section, canonical up to scaling. We will call this endomorphism
\nu_{\mathrm{spl},X}
.

Proof of Theorem
8.0.4
.

The fact that
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris},E}
admits a rank
b-2a
lattice of formal special endomorphisms implies that the slope 0 constant
F
-isocrystal

\mathbbm{1}^{\oplus(b-2a)}_{E}
embeds into
\Fil_{0,E}[p^{-1}]\subseteq\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris},E}[p^{-1}]
. This implies that
\mathrm{gr}_{0,E}[p^{-1}]\simeq\mathbbm{1}^{\oplus(b-2a)}_{E}
, and the quotient map
\Fil_{0,E}[p^{-1}]\rightarrow\mathrm{gr}_{0,E}[p^{-1}]
admits a section. So by Lemma
8.1.1
and the remark following that,
\mathbf{\Psi}_{E}\simeq\mathbf{\Psi}_{\mathrm{con},E}\times\mathbf{\Psi}_{\text{é}\mathrm{t},E}
up to isogeny. Since the coordinate ring of
U_{0}
is
p
-integrally closed in
E
in the sense of
[
Jia25
, §5.1]
, we can apply Proposition 5.2 of
loc.cit
to deduce that
\mathbf{\Psi}_{U_{0}}\simeq\mathbf{\Psi}_{\mathrm{con},U_{0}}\times\mathbf{\Psi}_{\text{é}\mathrm{t},U_{0}}
up to isogeny.

Let
D_{0}
be the function field of
U_{0}
, which is a finitely generated field over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. By Lemma
8.1.1
again, we find that
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris},D_{0}}[p^{-1}]
splits in the middle. Therefore Construction
8.1.3
gives rise to an endomorphism
\nu_{\mathrm{spl},D_{0}}\in\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{cris},D_{0}})\otimes{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. By crystalline isogeny theorem; cf.
[
dJ98
, Theorem 2.6]
, we have
\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{H}_{\mathrm{cris},D_{0}})\otimes{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}=\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{A}_{D_{0}})\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. On the other hand, the existence of
\nu_{\mathrm{spl},D_{0}}
accounts for the splitting of
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris},D_{0}}[p^{-1}]
in the middle, since
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{cris}}[p^{-1}]
does not split in the middle over the generic point of
\mathscr{S}_{a+1,\mathbb{F}_{q}}
, we have

\nu_{\mathrm{spl},D_{0}}\notin\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{A})\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. It follows that
\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{A})\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}\subsetneq\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{A}_{D_{0}})\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Therefore
\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{A})\subsetneq\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{A}_{D_{0}})
.

Furthremore, suppose that the quadratic form on this rank
b-2a
lattice of formal special endomorphisms is self-dual, then it is saturated. It follows that

\mathrm{gr}_{0,E}
is a trivial
F
-crystal, and the quotient map
\Fil_{0,E}\rightarrow\mathrm{gr}_{0,E}
admits a section (integrally). A similar argument as in the first paragraph implies that

\mathbf{\Psi}_{U_{0}}\simeq\mathbf{\Psi}_{\mathrm{con},U_{0}}\times\mathbf{\Psi}_{\text{é}\mathrm{t},U_{0}}
.
∎
